1st Midterm: Exam-Style Problems

Exam-style problems on Units 1 to 5, worth 10 points each like the Midterm 1 problem, written for this portal and solved step by step. They are practice material, not official exercises; each one was checked with SymPy.

16 exam-style problems on Units 1 to 5, written for this portal. The Midterm 1 papers of 2011 to 2024 are one problem of 10 points with 5 to 7 parts; the Exam simulator draws one of these and times it.

Problem · Unit 1 · 10 points · 60 min · Standard exam level

P01 — Bead on a vertical helical wire: Frenet triad, constant angular acceleration and the reaction

A rigid wire has the shape of a circular helix of radius $R$ wound around the vertical axis $Oz$ of the inertial frame $S_0=\{O;\mathbf i,\mathbf j,\mathbf k\}$ ($z$ upward, uniform gravity $-g\,\mathbf k$). In cylindrical coordinates $(\rho,\varphi,z)$, with $\varphi$ measured from the $x$ axis and counter-clockwise seen from above, the wire is

$$\rho=R,\qquad z=b\,\varphi\qquad(b>0)$$

so it rises a height $2\pi b$ (the pitch) per turn. A bead $P$ of mass $m$ slides without friction along the wire. At $t=0$ it is released from rest at $\varphi=\varphi_0$.

Figure 1. Bead $P$ on the smooth helix $\rho=R$, $z=b\varphi$ (pitch $2\pi b$), $z$ up and gravity $-g\,\mathbf k$; $\varphi$ is the azimuth from the $x$ axis, and $\{\mathbf t,\mathbf n,\mathbf b\}$ is the Frenet triad at $P$.

Use the cylindrical basis $\{\mathbf e_\rho,\mathbf e_\varphi,\mathbf k\}$ with $\mathbf e_\rho=\cos\varphi\,\mathbf i+\sin\varphi\,\mathbf j$, $\mathbf e_\varphi=-\sin\varphi\,\mathbf i+\cos\varphi\,\mathbf j$, and the intrinsic (Frenet) triad $\{\mathbf t,\mathbf n,\mathbf b\}$ of the helix, with $\mathbf t$ oriented towards increasing $\varphi$. Write $h=\sqrt{R^2+b^2}$.

Setup

  • Data: $m$, $R$, $b$, $g$, $\varphi_0$. In the numerical part (f): $R=0.30$ m, $b=0.40$ m (so $h=0.50$ m), $g=9.81$ m s$^{-2}$.
  • Hypotheses: point particle, rigid fixed wire, no friction (the reaction has no component along $\mathbf t$), $S_0$ inertial.
  • The bead is threaded on the wire, so the constraint is bilateral: the reaction may point either way in the normal plane.
  • Notation: the scalar $b$ is the pitch parameter of the helix ($z=b\varphi$); the bold $\mathbf b$ is the binormal unit vector. They are unrelated.

Part (a) · 1.5 points

Degrees of freedom and kinematics

How many degrees of freedom has $P$? Classify the constraints. Write the position, velocity and acceleration of $P$ in the cylindrical basis as functions of $\varphi$, $\dot\varphi$, $\ddot\varphi$.

Step 1. Count

The configuration of a free particle needs 3 coordinates $(\rho,\varphi,z)$. The wire imposes $f_1=\rho-R=0$ and $f_2=z-b\varphi=0$: two independent holonomic equations (positions only), with no explicit time (scleronomic), and two-sided (bilateral). Hence $3-2=1$ degree of freedom (Notes §2.7), and $\varphi$ is a convenient coordinate: $\rho$ and $z$ follow from it.

Step 2. Change of basis

The cylindrical basis turns with $\varphi$; its relation to the inertial basis is

$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf e_\rho\ \mathbf e_\varphi\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix}=[R]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf i\ \mathbf j\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix},\qquad [R]=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\varphi&\sin\varphi&0\ -\sin\varphi&\cos\varphi&0\ 0&0&1\end{pmatrix}$$

Checks: rows 1 and 2 have length $\cos^2\varphi+\sin^2\varphi=1$ and $-\cos\varphi\sin\varphi+\sin\varphi\cos\varphi=0$, row 3 is $\mathbf k$, so $[R][R]^T=I$; $\det[R]=\cos^2\varphi+\sin^2\varphi=+1$ (right-handed, $\mathbf e_\rho\times\mathbf e_\varphi=\mathbf k$). Differentiating the rows with the chain rule, $\dfrac{d\mathbf e_\rho}{d\varphi}=\mathbf e_\varphi$ and $\dfrac{d\mathbf e_\varphi}{d\varphi}=-\mathbf e_\rho$.

Step 3. Position and velocity

$\mathbf r=\rho\,\mathbf e_\rho+z\,\mathbf k=R\,\mathbf e_\rho+b\varphi\,\mathbf k$. The cylindrical velocity (Notes §2.6.2) is $\mathbf v=\dot\rho\,\mathbf e_\rho+\rho\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi+\dot z\,\mathbf k$. Here $\dot\rho=0$ and, by the chain rule, $\dot z=\dfrac{dz}{d\varphi}\dot\varphi=b\dot\varphi$:

$$\mathbf v=R\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi+b\dot\varphi\,\mathbf k,\qquad |\mathbf v|=\sqrt{R^2\dot\varphi^2+b^2\dot\varphi^2}=h\,|\dot\varphi|$$

Step 4. Acceleration

The cylindrical acceleration is $\mathbf a=(\ddot\rho-\rho\dot\varphi^2)\mathbf e_\rho+(\rho\ddot\varphi+2\dot\rho\dot\varphi)\mathbf e_\varphi+\ddot z\,\mathbf k$. With $\rho=R$, $\dot\rho=\ddot\rho=0$ and $\ddot z=\dfrac{d}{dt}(b\dot\varphi)=b\ddot\varphi$:

$$\mathbf a=-R\dot\varphi^2\,\mathbf e_\rho+R\ddot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi+b\ddot\varphi\,\mathbf k$$

Check by direct differentiation of $\mathbf v$ with $\dot{\mathbf e}_\varphi=-\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\rho$: $\dot{\mathbf v}=R\ddot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi+R\dot\varphi(-\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\rho)+b\ddot\varphi\,\mathbf k$, the same result.

Key results
  • 1 degree of freedom, coordinate $\varphi$; two holonomic, scleronomic, bilateral constraints $\rho-R=0$, $z-b\varphi=0$.
  • $\mathbf v=R\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi+b\dot\varphi\,\mathbf k$, $\ |\mathbf v|=h\,|\dot\varphi|$.
  • $\mathbf a=-R\dot\varphi^2\,\mathbf e_\rho+R\ddot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi+b\ddot\varphi\,\mathbf k$.

Part (b) · 2 points

Intrinsic triad, curvature and torsion

Obtain the unit vectors $\mathbf t$, $\mathbf n$, $\mathbf b$ of the helix in the cylindrical basis, its curvature $\kappa$ and torsion $\tau$, and the intrinsic components of the acceleration of $P$.

Step 1. Arc length and tangent

$ds=|\mathbf v|\,dt=h\,d\varphi$ (taking $s$ increasing with $\varphi$), so $s=h(\varphi-\varphi_0)$ and $\dot s=h\dot\varphi$. The unit tangent (Notes §2.4) is

$$\mathbf t=\frac{d\mathbf r}{ds}=\frac{d\mathbf r}{d\varphi}\frac{d\varphi}{ds}=\frac1h\big(R\,\mathbf e_\varphi+b\,\mathbf k\big)$$

using $\dfrac{d\mathbf e_\rho}{d\varphi}=\mathbf e_\varphi$.

Step 2. Curvature and normal

With $\dfrac{d\mathbf e_\varphi}{d\varphi}=-\mathbf e_\rho$ and $\mathbf k$ constant:

$$\frac{d\mathbf t}{ds}=\frac1h\frac{d\mathbf t}{d\varphi}=\frac1h\cdot\frac{R}{h}\big(-\mathbf e_\rho\big)=\frac{R}{h^2}\,(-\mathbf e_\rho)$$

The modulus is the curvature and the unit vector the principal normal: $\kappa=R/h^2=R/(R^2+b^2)$, $\mathbf n=-\mathbf e_\rho$. The normal is horizontal and points to the axis, although the helix is inclined.

Step 3. Binormal and torsion

$\mathbf b=\mathbf t\times\mathbf n=\dfrac1h(R\,\mathbf e_\varphi+b\,\mathbf k)\times(-\mathbf e_\rho)$. With $\mathbf e_\varphi\times\mathbf e_\rho=-\mathbf k$ and $\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_\rho=\mathbf e_\varphi$:

$$\mathbf b=\frac1h\big(R\,\mathbf k-b\,\mathbf e_\varphi\big)$$

Then $\dfrac{d\mathbf b}{ds}=\dfrac1h\cdot\dfrac{-b}{h}\dfrac{d\mathbf e_\varphi}{d\varphi}=\dfrac{b}{h^2}\,\mathbf e_\rho=-\dfrac{b}{h^2}\,\mathbf n$, so $\tau=b/h^2=b/(R^2+b^2)$. In matrix form the triad is $(\mathbf t,\mathbf n,\mathbf b)^T=[Q]\,(\mathbf e_\rho,\mathbf e_\varphi,\mathbf k)^T$ with rows $(0,\ R/h,\ b/h)$, $(-1,\ 0,\ 0)$, $(0,\ -b/h,\ R/h)$: the rows are unit and mutually orthogonal ($R^2/h^2+b^2/h^2=1$, $-bR/h^2+bR/h^2=0$), so $[Q][Q]^T=I$, and expanding along the second row $\det[Q]=-(-1)\big(\tfrac Rh\tfrac Rh+\tfrac bh\tfrac bh\big)=+1$: $\{\mathbf t,\mathbf n,\mathbf b\}$ is right-handed. The third Frenet formula $\dfrac{d\mathbf n}{ds}=-\kappa\,\mathbf t+\tau\,\mathbf b$ checks: $\dfrac1h\dfrac{d(-\mathbf e_\rho)}{d\varphi}=-\dfrac{\mathbf e_\varphi}{h}$ and $-\dfrac{R}{h^3}(R\mathbf e_\varphi+b\mathbf k)+\dfrac{b}{h^3}(R\mathbf k-b\mathbf e_\varphi)=-\dfrac{R^2+b^2}{h^3}\mathbf e_\varphi=-\dfrac{\mathbf e_\varphi}{h}$.

Step 4. Intrinsic acceleration

The intrinsic acceleration (Notes §2.5) is $\mathbf a=\ddot s\,\mathbf t+\kappa\dot s^2\,\mathbf n$. With $\dot s=h\dot\varphi$, $\ddot s=h\ddot\varphi$ and $\kappa\dot s^2=\dfrac{R}{h^2}h^2\dot\varphi^2$:

$$\mathbf a=h\ddot\varphi\,\mathbf t+R\dot\varphi^2\,\mathbf n$$

Cross-check with (a): $\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf t=\dfrac1h(R\cdot R\ddot\varphi+b\cdot b\ddot\varphi)=h\ddot\varphi$, $\ \mathbf a\cdot\mathbf n=R\dot\varphi^2$, $\ \mathbf a\cdot\mathbf b=\dfrac1h(-bR\ddot\varphi+Rb\ddot\varphi)=0$.

Key results
  • $\mathbf t=\dfrac{R\,\mathbf e_\varphi+b\,\mathbf k}{h}$, $\ \mathbf n=-\mathbf e_\rho$ (horizontal, towards the axis), $\ \mathbf b=\dfrac{-b\,\mathbf e_\varphi+R\,\mathbf k}{h}$.
  • $\kappa=\dfrac{R}{R^2+b^2}$, $\ \tau=\dfrac{b}{R^2+b^2}$.
  • $\mathbf a=h\ddot\varphi\,\mathbf t+R\dot\varphi^2\,\mathbf n$, no binormal component.

Part (c) · 1 point

Forces on the bead

Write the weight and the reaction of the wire in the intrinsic basis. How many unknowns does the problem have?

Step 1. Weight

$\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf t=b/h$, $\ \mathbf k\cdot\mathbf n=-\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf e_\rho=0$, $\ \mathbf k\cdot\mathbf b=R/h$. Hence

$$\mathbf W=-mg\,\mathbf k=-\frac{mgb}{h}\,\mathbf t-\frac{mgR}{h}\,\mathbf b$$

The weight has no component along $\mathbf n$, because $\mathbf n$ is horizontal.

Step 2. Reaction

The wire is smooth: its reaction is orthogonal to the tangent, i.e. it lies in the normal plane spanned by $\mathbf n$ and $\mathbf b$ (Notes §6.5):

$$\mathbf N=N_n\,\mathbf n+N_b\,\mathbf b$$

The three scalar equations of Newton's law will determine the three unknowns $\varphi(t)$, $N_n$, $N_b$.

Key results
  • $\mathbf k=\dfrac{b}{h}\mathbf t+\dfrac{R}{h}\mathbf b$, so $\mathbf W=-mg\dfrac bh\,\mathbf t-mg\dfrac Rh\,\mathbf b$.
  • $\mathbf N=N_n\,\mathbf n+N_b\,\mathbf b$; unknowns $\varphi(t)$, $N_n$, $N_b$.

Part (d) · 2 points

Equation of motion and reaction

Obtain the equation of motion free of reactions and integrate it. Obtain $N_n$ and $N_b$ as functions of $\dot\varphi$. Does the bead move like a pendulum?

Step 1. Projections

With $\mathbf a$ of (b) and the forces of (c):

$$\mathbf t:\ mh\ddot\varphi=-\frac{mgb}{h},\qquad \mathbf n:\ mR\dot\varphi^2=N_n,\qquad \mathbf b:\ 0=-\frac{mgR}{h}+N_b$$

Step 2. Equation of motion

The tangential equation contains no reaction:

$$\ddot\varphi=-\frac{gb}{h^2}=-\frac{gb}{R^2+b^2}$$

The right-hand side does not depend on $\varphi$: there is no restoring term and no equilibrium. The tangential acceleration is $\ddot s=h\ddot\varphi=-g\,\dfrac bh=-g\sin\alpha$, with $\alpha$ the constant inclination of the helix ($\sin\alpha=b/h$): the bead moves exactly as on an inclined plane wound around a cylinder. It is not a pendulum.

Step 3. Integration

Integrating once between $0$ and $t$ (Barrow's rule, $\dot\varphi(0)=0$): $\dot\varphi(t)-\dot\varphi(0)=\displaystyle\int_0^t\ddot\varphi\,dt'=-\frac{gb}{h^2}\,[t']_0^t$, so $\dot\varphi=-\dfrac{gb}{h^2}t$. Integrating again with $\varphi(0)=\varphi_0$:

$$\varphi(t)=\varphi_0+\int_0^t\Big(-\frac{gb}{h^2}t'\Big)dt'=\varphi_0-\frac{gb}{h^2}\Big[\frac{t'^2}{2}\Big]_0^t=\varphi_0-\frac{gb}{2h^2}\,t^2$$

The bead descends ($\varphi$ and $z=b\varphi$ decrease).

Step 4. Reactions

$$N_n=mR\dot\varphi^2,\qquad N_b=\frac{mgR}{h}$$

$N_b$ supports the part of the weight normal to the wire and is constant; $N_n$ (pointing to the axis) supplies the centripetal force of the horizontal circular motion and grows with the speed.

Key results
  • $\ddot\varphi=-\dfrac{gb}{R^2+b^2}$: constant. $\ \varphi(t)=\varphi_0-\dfrac{gb}{2h^2}t^2$.
  • $N_n=mR\dot\varphi^2$, $\ N_b=\dfrac{mgR}{h}$ (constant).

Part (e) · 1.5 points

First integral

Show that the mechanical energy is conserved and use it to write $\dot\varphi^2$ and $N_n$ as functions of $\varphi$.

Step 1. Power of the forces

$\mathbf v=h\dot\varphi\,\mathbf t$ and $\mathbf N\cdot\mathbf t=0$, so $\mathbf N\cdot\mathbf v=0$: the reaction of a fixed smooth curve does no work (Notes §6.4). The weight is conservative with $V=mgz=mgb\varphi$. Hence (Notes §4.5)

$$E=\tfrac12m|\mathbf v|^2+mgz=\tfrac12mh^2\dot\varphi^2+mgb\varphi=\text{const}$$

Step 2. Same result from the equation of motion

Multiply $h^2\ddot\varphi=-gb$ by $\dot\varphi$: $h^2\dot\varphi\ddot\varphi=\dfrac{d}{dt}\Big(\tfrac12h^2\dot\varphi^2\Big)$ (chain rule) and $-gb\dot\varphi=\dfrac{d}{dt}(-gb\varphi)$. Integrating from $0$ to $t$ with Barrow's rule: $\tfrac12h^2\dot\varphi^2-0=-gb(\varphi-\varphi_0)$.

Step 3. Speed and reaction along the wire

$$\dot\varphi^2=\frac{2gb(\varphi_0-\varphi)}{h^2},\qquad N_n=mR\dot\varphi^2=\frac{2mgRb(\varphi_0-\varphi)}{h^2}$$

With $\kappa=R/h^2$ and the height fallen $\Delta z=b(\varphi_0-\varphi)$: $N_n=2mg\kappa\,\Delta z$, i.e. $N_n=m\kappa v^2$ with $v^2=2g\Delta z$.

Key results
  • $E=\tfrac12mh^2\dot\varphi^2+mgb\varphi=mgb\varphi_0$.
  • $\dot\varphi^2=\dfrac{2gb(\varphi_0-\varphi)}{h^2}$, $\ N_n=\dfrac{2mgRb(\varphi_0-\varphi)}{h^2}=2mg\,\kappa\,\Delta z$ with $\Delta z=b(\varphi_0-\varphi)$.

Part (f) · 2 points

One full turn: time, speed and reaction

Find the time $t_1$ the bead needs to complete one turn and its speed then. For $R=0.30$ m, $b=0.40$ m, $g=9.81$ m s$^{-2}$ give $\kappa$, $\tau$, $\ddot\varphi$, $t_1$, the speed and the reaction (in units of $mg$) at $t_1$. Is the acceleration vector of the bead constant?

Step 1. Time for one turn

From $\varphi(t_1)=\varphi_0-2\pi$: $\dfrac{gb}{2h^2}t_1^2=2\pi\Rightarrow t_1=\sqrt{\dfrac{4\pi h^2}{gb}}=2h\sqrt{\dfrac{\pi}{gb}}$.

Step 2. Speed after one turn

From (e) with $\varphi_0-\varphi=2\pi$: $\dot\varphi_1^2=\dfrac{4\pi gb}{h^2}$ and $v_1=h|\dot\varphi_1|=\sqrt{4\pi gb}$. Equivalently $v_1=h|\dot\varphi(t_1)|=h\dfrac{gb}{h^2}t_1=\dfrac{gb}{h}\cdot2h\sqrt{\dfrac{\pi}{gb}}=\sqrt{4\pi gb}$. Since the fall per turn is $2\pi b$, $v_1=\sqrt{2g\cdot2\pi b}$: the speed depends only on the height fallen, not on $R$.

Step 3. Numbers

$h=\sqrt{0.09+0.16}=0.5$ m; $\kappa=0.3/0.25=1.2$ m$^{-1}$; $\tau=0.4/0.25=1.6$ m$^{-1}$; $\ddot\varphi=-9.81\cdot0.4/0.25=-15.696$ rad s$^{-2}$, $\ddot s=h\ddot\varphi=-7.848$ m s$^{-2}$ ($\sin\alpha=0.8$, $\alpha=53.13^\circ$).

$$t_1=2(0.5)\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{9.81\cdot0.4}}=0.8948\ \text{s},\qquad v_1=\sqrt{4\pi\cdot9.81\cdot0.4}=7.022\ \text{m/s},\qquad |\dot\varphi_1|=\frac{v_1}{h}=14.04\ \text{rad/s}$$

Step 4. Reaction after one turn

$$\frac{N_n}{mg}=\frac{R\dot\varphi_1^2}{g}=\frac{4\pi Rb}{h^2}=4\pi\cdot\frac{0.12}{0.25}=1.92\pi\approx6.032,\qquad \frac{N_b}{mg}=\frac Rh=0.6$$

$|\mathbf N|=mg\sqrt{6.032^2+0.6^2}\approx6.062\,mg$. The tangential acceleration is constant, but $\mathbf a=h\ddot\varphi\,\mathbf t+R\dot\varphi^2\,\mathbf n$ has a growing normal part and a direction that turns with the triad: the acceleration vector is not constant.

Key results
  • $t_1=2h\sqrt{\dfrac{\pi}{gb}}$, $\ v_1=\sqrt{4\pi gb}=\sqrt{2g\,(2\pi b)}$ (independent of $R$).
  • $\kappa=1.2$ m$^{-1}$, $\tau=1.6$ m$^{-1}$, $\ddot\varphi=-15.70$ rad s$^{-2}$, $\ddot s=-7.848$ m s$^{-2}$, $t_1=0.8948$ s, $v_1=7.022$ m/s, $|\dot\varphi_1|=14.04$ rad/s.
  • $N_n=1.92\pi\,mg\approx6.032\,mg$, $N_b=0.6\,mg$, $|\mathbf N|\approx6.062\,mg$. The acceleration is not constant: its normal part $R\dot\varphi^2$ grows.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $\kappa$ and $\tau$ are m/m$^2$ = m$^{-1}$; $gb/h^2$ is (m s$^{-2}$)(m)/m$^2$ = s$^{-2}$, an angular acceleration; $t_1=2h\sqrt{\pi/(gb)}$ has m/$\sqrt{\text{m}^2\,\text{s}^{-2}}$ = s; $N=mR\dot\varphi^2$ is kg m s$^{-2}$ = N.
  • Limits: $b\to0$ (horizontal ring): $\ddot\varphi\to0$, the bead stays at rest and $N_b\to mg$ carries the whole weight. $R\to0$ (vertical straight wire): $\ddot z=b\ddot\varphi=-g\,b^2/h^2\to-g$ (free fall), $\kappa\to0$ and the reaction vanishes. $\kappa$ is largest for $b\to0$ and $\tau$ is largest for $b=R$.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) expecting a pendulum-type equation: on a helix the gravity component along the wire is the same everywhere, so $\ddot\varphi$ is constant; (2) taking $\mathbf n$ along the "radius of the coil" tilted with the helix: it is exactly $-\mathbf e_\rho$, horizontal; (3) forgetting $N_b$, which exists even at rest; (4) concluding that the acceleration is constant because $\ddot\varphi$ is: the normal acceleration $R\dot\varphi^2$ grows linearly with the height fallen.
Problem · Unit 4 · 10 points · 60 min · High difficulty / trap

P02 — Particle on a smooth paraboloid of revolution: axial angular momentum, circular orbits and radial oscillations

A particle $P$ of mass $m$ moves on the inner face of a smooth, fixed paraboloid of revolution whose axis $Oz$ is vertical (upward; uniform gravity $-g\,\mathbf k$). In cylindrical coordinates $(r,\varphi,z)$ of the inertial frame $S_0=\{O;\mathbf i,\mathbf j,\mathbf k\}$ the surface is

$$z=\frac{r^2}{2c}\qquad(c>0)$$

with $O$ at its vertex. The particle rests on the surface from above (the contact can only push $P$ away from the surface, towards the inside of the bowl). The azimuth $\varphi$ is measured from the $x$ axis, counter-clockwise seen from above.

Figure 1. Particle $P$ on the smooth inner surface $z=r^2/(2c)$ (axis $Oz$ vertical, gravity $-g\,\mathbf k$). Left: meridian plane through $P$ with the unit normal $\mathbf n$; right: top view with the azimuth $\varphi$.

Use the cylindrical basis $\{\mathbf e_r,\mathbf e_\varphi,\mathbf k\}$, and write $q=\sqrt{1+r^2/c^2}$ and $\Omega_0=\sqrt{g/c}$.

Setup

  • Data: $m$, $c$, $g$. In part (f): initial radius $r_0=c/2$, $\dot r(0)=0$, initial velocity horizontal and tangent to the parallel with speed $v_0=2r_0\Omega_0=c\,\Omega_0$; for numbers, $c=1$ m and $g=9.81$ m s$^{-2}$.
  • Hypotheses: point particle, no friction, $S_0$ inertial. The contact is unilateral: the model is valid while the normal reaction is non-negative (checked in (f)).

Part (a) · 1.5 points

Degrees of freedom and kinematics

How many degrees of freedom has $P$ while it stays on the surface? Write its velocity and acceleration in the cylindrical basis using the constraint.

Step 1. Count

A free particle needs $(r,\varphi,z)$. The surface $f=z-r^2/(2c)=0$ is one holonomic equation without explicit time: $3-1=2$ degrees of freedom (Notes §2.7), with generalized coordinates $r$ and $\varphi$. The constraint is unilateral, because the particle is only pressed against the surface.

Step 2. Derivatives of $z$

By the chain rule, $\dot z=\dfrac{dz}{dr}\dot r=\dfrac{r\dot r}{c}$. By the product rule, $\ddot z=\dfrac1c\dfrac{d}{dt}(r\dot r)=\dfrac{\dot r\,\dot r+r\ddot r}{c}=\dfrac{\dot r^2+r\ddot r}{c}$.

Step 3. Change of basis

The cylindrical basis turns with $\varphi$; its relation to the inertial basis is

$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf e_r\ \mathbf e_\varphi\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix}=[R]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf i\ \mathbf j\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix},\qquad [R]=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\varphi&\sin\varphi&0\ -\sin\varphi&\cos\varphi&0\ 0&0&1\end{pmatrix}$$

Checks: rows 1 and 2 have length $\cos^2\varphi+\sin^2\varphi=1$ and $-\cos\varphi\sin\varphi+\sin\varphi\cos\varphi=0$, row 3 is $\mathbf k$, so $[R][R]^T=I$; $\det[R]=\cos^2\varphi+\sin^2\varphi=+1$ (right-handed, $\mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_\varphi=\mathbf k$). By the chain rule, $\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi$ and $\dot{\mathbf e}_\varphi=-\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_r$.

Step 4. Velocity and acceleration

In cylindrical coordinates (Notes §2.6.2), $\mathbf v=\dot r\,\mathbf e_r+r\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi+\dot z\,\mathbf k$ and $\mathbf a=(\ddot r-r\dot\varphi^2)\mathbf e_r+(r\ddot\varphi+2\dot r\dot\varphi)\mathbf e_\varphi+\ddot z\,\mathbf k$. With Step 2:

$$\mathbf v=\dot r\,\mathbf e_r+r\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi+\frac{r\dot r}{c}\,\mathbf k,\qquad |\mathbf v|^2=q^2\dot r^2+r^2\dot\varphi^2$$
$$\mathbf a=(\ddot r-r\dot\varphi^2)\,\mathbf e_r+(2\dot r\dot\varphi+r\ddot\varphi)\,\mathbf e_\varphi+\frac{\dot r^2+r\ddot r}{c}\,\mathbf k$$
Key results
  • 2 degrees of freedom, coordinates $(r,\varphi)$; one holonomic, scleronomic, unilateral constraint.
  • $\dot z=r\dot r/c$, $\ \ddot z=(\dot r^2+r\ddot r)/c$.
  • $\mathbf a=(\ddot r-r\dot\varphi^2)\mathbf e_r+(2\dot r\dot\varphi+r\ddot\varphi)\mathbf e_\varphi+\dfrac{\dot r^2+r\ddot r}{c}\mathbf k$.

Part (b) · 1 point

Forces

Obtain the unit normal of the surface and write the forces on $P$.

Step 1. Normal vector

$\nabla f=\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial r}\mathbf e_r+\dfrac1r\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\varphi}\mathbf e_\varphi+\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial z}\mathbf k=-\dfrac rc\,\mathbf e_r+\mathbf k$, of modulus $\sqrt{1+r^2/c^2}=q$ (Notes §6.3). Its $\mathbf k$ component is positive, so it points into the bowl, the side on which the particle lies:

$$\mathbf n=\frac{1}{q}\Big(-\frac rc\,\mathbf e_r+\mathbf k\Big)$$

Check: $\mathbf n\cdot\mathbf v=\dfrac1q\Big(-\dfrac{r\dot r}{c}+\dfrac{r\dot r}{c}\Big)=0$.

Step 2. Forces

The weight $\mathbf W=-mg\,\mathbf k$ and the smooth normal reaction $\mathbf N=N\,\mathbf n$ (Notes §6.4). Contact requires $N\ge0$. Note that $\mathbf N$ lies in the meridian plane (it has no $\mathbf e_\varphi$ component) but does not pass through $O$ in general.

Key results
  • $\mathbf n=\dfrac{-(r/c)\,\mathbf e_r+\mathbf k}{q}$ (towards the inside of the bowl).
  • $\mathbf W=-mg\,\mathbf k$, $\ \mathbf N=N\mathbf n$ with $N\ge0$ for contact.

Part (c) · 2 points

Equations of motion and first integrals

Project Newton's law, show that the component $H=r^2\dot\varphi$ of the specific angular momentum along the axis is conserved, write the energy and obtain one equation for $r(t)$ free of $N$. Give $N$ as a function of the state.

Step 1. Projections

With $\mathbf a$ of (a), $\mathbf N\cdot\mathbf e_r=-Nr/(cq)$, $\mathbf N\cdot\mathbf k=N/q$:

$$\mathbf e_r:\ m(\ddot r-r\dot\varphi^2)=-\frac{N r}{c\,q}\qquad\mathbf e_\varphi:\ m(2\dot r\dot\varphi+r\ddot\varphi)=0\qquad\mathbf k:\ m\frac{\dot r^2+r\ddot r}{c}=\frac Nq-mg$$

Step 2. Axial angular momentum

By the product rule, $\dfrac{d}{dt}(r^2\dot\varphi)=2r\dot r\dot\varphi+r^2\ddot\varphi=r(2\dot r\dot\varphi+r\ddot\varphi)$, so the $\mathbf e_\varphi$ equation is $\dfrac mr\dfrac{d}{dt}(r^2\dot\varphi)=0$: $H=r^2\dot\varphi$ is constant. Physical reason (Notes §4.8): $\mathbf k\cdot(\mathbf r\times\mathbf F)$ is the moment about the $z$ axis; $\mathbf W$ is parallel to the axis and $\mathbf N$ lies in the meridian plane, which contains the axis, so both have zero moment about it. The full vector $\mathbf H_O$ is not conserved ($\mathbf r\times\mathbf N\neq\mathbf 0$ in general): only its axial component is.

Step 3. Equation for $r$

From $\mathbf k$: $\dfrac Nq=m\Big(\dfrac{\dot r^2+r\ddot r}{c}+g\Big)$. Substituting in $\mathbf e_r$ (which contains $\dfrac{r}{c}\cdot\dfrac Nq$) and using $\dot\varphi=H/r^2$:

$$\ddot r-\frac{H^2}{r^3}=-\frac rc\Big(\frac{\dot r^2+r\ddot r}{c}+g\Big)\ \Longrightarrow\ \Big(1+\frac{r^2}{c^2}\Big)\ddot r+\frac{r\dot r^2}{c^2}-\frac{H^2}{r^3}+\frac{gr}{c}=0$$

Step 4. Energy

$\mathbf N\cdot\mathbf v=0$ (fixed smooth surface) and $\mathbf W$ derives from $V=mgz=mgr^2/(2c)$, so $E=\tfrac12m|\mathbf v|^2+V$ is constant (Notes §4.5). With $|\mathbf v|^2$ of (a) and $r\dot\varphi=H/r$:

$$\frac Em=\frac12\Big[\Big(1+\frac{r^2}{c^2}\Big)\dot r^2+\frac{H^2}{r^2}\Big]+\frac{gr^2}{2c}$$

Check: $\dfrac{d}{dt}\dfrac Em=\dot r\Big[\Big(1+\dfrac{r^2}{c^2}\Big)\ddot r+\dfrac{r\dot r^2}{c^2}-\dfrac{H^2}{r^3}+\dfrac{gr}{c}\Big]=0$ by Step 3 (the term $\dfrac{r\dot r^3}{c^2}$ comes from the chain rule on $\dfrac{r^2}{c^2}\dot r^2$).

Step 5. Reaction

$N=mq\Big(\dfrac{\dot r^2+r\ddot r}{c}+g\Big)$, with $\ddot r$ from Step 3.

Key results
  • $H=r^2\dot\varphi=$ const (the moment about the axis of $\mathbf W$ and $\mathbf N$ is zero; $\mathbf H_O$ itself is not conserved).
  • $\Big(1+\dfrac{r^2}{c^2}\Big)\ddot r+\dfrac{r\dot r^2}{c^2}-\dfrac{H^2}{r^3}+\dfrac{gr}{c}=0$.
  • $\dfrac Em=\tfrac12\Big[\Big(1+\dfrac{r^2}{c^2}\Big)\dot r^2+\dfrac{H^2}{r^2}\Big]+\dfrac{gr^2}{2c}$; $\ N=mq\Big(\dfrac{\dot r^2+r\ddot r}{c}+g\Big)$.

Part (d) · 1.5 points

Horizontal circular orbits

Find the angular rate of a horizontal circular motion at radius $r_0$, its speed and the normal reaction. Discuss the dependence on $r_0$.

Step 1. Rate

With $\dot r=\ddot r=0$ and $H=r_0^2\dot\varphi$: $\dfrac{H^2}{r_0^3}=r_0\dot\varphi^2=\dfrac{gr_0}{c}$, hence

$$\dot\varphi^2=\frac gc\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\Omega_0=\sqrt{\frac gc},\qquad T=2\pi\sqrt{\frac cg},\qquad v=r_0\Omega_0$$

All horizontal circles are described in the same time, whatever their radius (isochronous orbits). This is the trap of the item: on a cone or on a sphere the rate depends on the radius; on the paraboloid the slope $dz/dr=r/c$ grows exactly in proportion to the centripetal acceleration $r\Omega^2$ needed.

Step 2. Reaction

$N=mq_0(0+g)=mg\sqrt{1+r_0^2/c^2}$. Its vertical component $N/q_0=mg$ carries the weight; its horizontal component $Nr_0/(cq_0)=mgr_0/c=mr_0\Omega_0^2$ is the centripetal force.

Key results
  • $\dot\varphi^2=g/c$: $\Omega_0=\sqrt{g/c}$, the same for every $r_0$; period $2\pi\sqrt{c/g}$; speed $r_0\Omega_0$.
  • $N=mg\sqrt{1+r_0^2/c^2}$.

Part (e) · 2 points

Small radial oscillations about a circular orbit

Perturb the circular orbit of radius $r_0$ keeping $H=r_0^2\Omega_0$. Obtain the frequency $\omega_r$ of the small radial oscillations and the ratio $\omega_r/\Omega_0$. For which $r_0$ is the perturbed orbit closed after one revolution? What happens for $r_0\to0$ and $r_0=c$?

Step 1. Linearisation

$G(r,\dot r)=\dfrac{F(r)-r\dot r^2/c^2}{1+r^2/c^2}$ with $F(r)=\dfrac{H^2}{r^3}-\dfrac{gr}{c}$, and $F(r_0)=0$ by (d). Put $r=r_0+\varepsilon$ with $\varepsilon$, $\dot\varepsilon$ small. $\partial G/\partial\dot r=-2r\dot r/(c^2q^2)$ vanishes at $\dot r=0$. Since $F(r_0)=0$, $\dfrac{\partial G}{\partial r}\Big|_{r_0}=\dfrac{F'(r_0)}{1+r_0^2/c^2}$ (the derivative of the denominator multiplies $F(r_0)=0$). With $F'(r)=-\dfrac{3H^2}{r^4}-\dfrac gc$ and $H^2=r_0^4\,g/c$:

$$F'(r_0)=-\frac{3g}{c}-\frac gc=-\frac{4g}{c}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\ddot\varepsilon+\frac{4g}{c\,(1+r_0^2/c^2)}\,\varepsilon=0$$

Step 2. Frequency and ratio

$$\omega_r^2=\frac{4gc}{c^2+r_0^2},\qquad\frac{\omega_r}{\Omega_0}=\sqrt{\frac{4c^2}{c^2+r_0^2}}=\frac{2c}{\sqrt{c^2+r_0^2}}$$

The orbit is stable (positive $\omega_r^2$) for every $r_0$. The trap: the radial frequency is not $\Omega_0$, and the factor $1+r_0^2/c^2$ (the inertia of the vertical motion that comes with $\dot r$) cannot be dropped.

Step 3. Special radii

  • $r_0\to0$: ratio $2$: two radial oscillations per revolution, a centred ellipse, as for an isotropic harmonic bowl ($z\approx r^2/(2c)$ is then the potential of a linear spring of stiffness $mg/c$).
  • $r_0=c$: ratio $\sqrt2$, irrational: the perturbed orbit precesses and never closes.
  • $r_0=\sqrt3\,c$: ratio $2c/\sqrt{4c^2}=1$: one radial oscillation per revolution, a closed orbit.
Key results
  • $\omega_r^2=\dfrac{4g}{c\,(1+r_0^2/c^2)}=\dfrac{4gc}{c^2+r_0^2}$, $\quad\dfrac{\omega_r}{\Omega_0}=\dfrac{2c}{\sqrt{c^2+r_0^2}}$.
  • Ratio 1 for $r_0=\sqrt3\,c$ (closed after one revolution); $2$ for $r_0\to0$; $\sqrt2$ for $r_0=c$ (not closed).

Part (f) · 2 points

Turning points and contact

The particle starts at $r_0$ with $\dot r=0$ and a horizontal velocity $v_0$ tangent to the parallel. Find the turning radii. Then take $r_0=c/2$, $v_0=2r_0\Omega_0$: give the turning radii, $\ddot r$ and $N$ at both, and decide whether the particle ever leaves the surface.

Step 1. Constants

$\mathbf v(0)=v_0\mathbf e_\varphi$, so $H=r_0\cdot r_0\dot\varphi(0)=r_0v_0$ and $E/m=\tfrac12v_0^2+\dfrac{gr_0^2}{2c}$.

Step 2. Turning radii

At $\dot r=0$ the energy of (c) gives $\dfrac{H^2}{r^2}+\dfrac{gr^2}{c}=v_0^2+\dfrac{gr_0^2}{c}$. Multiply by $u=r^2$:

$$\frac gc u^2-\Big(v_0^2+\frac{gr_0^2}{c}\Big)u+r_0^2v_0^2=0\ \Longleftrightarrow\ (u-r_0^2)\Big(\frac{gu}{c}-v_0^2\Big)=0$$

(expanding the product gives back the three terms). Roots: $r=r_0$ and $r^*=v_0\sqrt{c/g}=v_0/\Omega_0$. If $v_0>r_0\Omega_0$ the particle climbs to $r^*>r_0$; if $v_0=r_0\Omega_0$ both coincide (circular orbit of (d)). The time between them is a quadrature: from the energy, $dt=\dfrac{q\,dr}{\sqrt{2E/m-H^2/r^2-gr^2/c}}$.

Step 3. The numerical case

$r_0=c/2$, $v_0=c\Omega_0$: $r^*=c\Omega_0/\Omega_0=c$. So $r$ oscillates between $c/2$ and $c$ ($z$ between $c/8$ and $c/2$). With $H^2=r_0^2v_0^2=c^3g/4$, $E/m=\tfrac58gc$. For $c=1$ m: $\Omega_0=v_0=3.132$ (rad/s and m/s), $\omega_r(r_0)=\tfrac{4}{\sqrt5}\Omega_0=5.603$ rad/s for the small oscillations of (e), which do not apply here because the amplitude is large.

At the turning points $\dot r=0$, so $q^2\ddot r=\dfrac{H^2}{r^3}-\dfrac{gr}{c}$:

  • $r=c/2$: $\dfrac{H^2}{r^3}=\dfrac{c^3g/4}{c^3/8}=2g$, $\dfrac{gr}{c}=\dfrac g2$, $q^2=\dfrac54$: $\ddot r=\dfrac{3g/2}{5/4}=\dfrac65g$.
  • $r=c$: $\dfrac{H^2}{r^3}=\dfrac g4$, $\dfrac{gr}{c}=g$, $q^2=2$: $\ddot r=-\dfrac{3g}{8}$.

Then $N=mq\Big(\dfrac{r\ddot r}{c}+g\Big)$: at $r=c/2$, $N=m\dfrac{\sqrt5}{2}\Big(\dfrac35g+g\Big)=\dfrac{4}{\sqrt5}mg\approx1.789\,mg$; at $r=c$, $N=m\sqrt2\Big(-\dfrac38g+g\Big)=\dfrac{5\sqrt2}{8}mg\approx0.884\,mg$.

Step 4. Contact along any motion

From Step 3 of (c), multiplied by $r$: $r\ddot r\,q^2=\dfrac{H^2}{r^2}-\dfrac{r^2\dot r^2}{c^2}-\dfrac{gr^2}{c}$. Adding $q^2\dot r^2$ to both sides and using $q^2-r^2/c^2=1$:

$$q^2\big(\dot r^2+r\ddot r\big)=\dot r^2+\frac{H^2}{r^2}-\frac{gr^2}{c}$$

From the energy of (c), $q^2\dot r^2=2E/m-H^2/r^2-gr^2/c$. Multiplying the previous line by $q^2$ and substituting $q^2\dot r^2$:

$$q^4\big(\dot r^2+r\ddot r\big)=\frac{2E}{m}-\frac{H^2}{r^2}-\frac{gr^2}{c}+q^2\frac{H^2}{r^2}-q^2\frac{gr^2}{c}=\frac{2E}{m}+\frac{H^2}{c^2}-\Big(2+\frac{r^2}{c^2}\Big)\frac{gr^2}{c}$$

because $(q^2-1)\dfrac{H^2}{r^2}=\dfrac{H^2}{c^2}$ and $(1+q^2)\dfrac{gr^2}{c}=\Big(2+\dfrac{r^2}{c^2}\Big)\dfrac{gr^2}{c}$. So

$$\dot r^2+r\ddot r=\frac{1}{q^4}\Big[\frac{2E}{m}+\frac{H^2}{c^2}-\Big(2+\frac{r^2}{c^2}\Big)\frac{gr^2}{c}\Big]$$

Substituting in $N=mq\big[(\dot r^2+r\ddot r)/c+g\big]=\dfrac{m}{cq^3}\big[q^4(\dot r^2+r\ddot r)+gc\,q^4\big]$ and using the identity

$$gc\,q^4-\Big(2+\frac{r^2}{c^2}\Big)\frac{gr^2}{c}=gc\Big(1+\frac{2r^2}{c^2}+\frac{r^4}{c^4}\Big)-\frac{2gr^2}{c}-\frac{gr^4}{c^3}=gc$$

we get $N=\dfrac{m}{cq^3}\Big(\dfrac{2E}{m}+\dfrac{H^2}{c^2}+gc\Big)$, and with $cq^3=(c^2+r^2)^{3/2}/c^2$:

$$N=\frac{m\,\big(2c^2E/m+H^2+gc^3\big)}{(c^2+r^2)^{3/2}}$$

With $V=mgz\ge0$ the energy is positive, so $N>0$ on every motion: the particle never leaves the inner face. In the numerical case $2c^2E/m+H^2+gc^3=\tfrac54gc^3+\tfrac14gc^3+gc^3=\tfrac52gc^3$, so $N=\dfrac{5mgc^3}{2(c^2+r^2)^{3/2}}$, decreasing from $1.789\,mg$ at $r=c/2$ to its minimum $0.884\,mg$ at $r=c$, in agreement with Step 3.

Key results
  • $(u-r_0^2)\Big(\dfrac{gu}{c}-v_0^2\Big)=0$: turning radii $r_0$ and $r^*=v_0\sqrt{c/g}=v_0/\Omega_0$.
  • Case: $r^*=c$; $\ddot r(c/2)=\tfrac65g$ (outwards), $\ddot r(c)=-\tfrac38g$; $N(c/2)=\tfrac{4}{\sqrt5}mg\approx1.789\,mg$, $N(c)=\tfrac{5\sqrt2}{8}mg\approx0.884\,mg$.
  • On every motion $N=\dfrac{m\,(2Ec^2/m+H^2+gc^3)}{(c^2+r^2)^{3/2}}>0$: contact is never lost. Here $N=\dfrac{5mgc^3}{2(c^2+r^2)^{3/2}}$.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $c$ is a length, so $g/c$ and $\omega_r^2=4gc/(c^2+r_0^2)$ are s$^{-2}$; $H=r^2\dot\varphi$ is m$^2$ s$^{-1}$, $H^2/r^3$ and $gr/c$ are m s$^{-2}$; in $N$, $(2c^2E/m+H^2+gc^3)$ is m$^4$ s$^{-2}$ and $(c^2+r^2)^{3/2}$ is m$^3$: $N$ is kg m s$^{-2}$.
  • Limits: $c\to\infty$ (flat floor): $\Omega_0\to0$, no horizontal circle without a wall. $r_0\ll c$: the bowl acts as an isotropic linear spring, $\omega_r=2\Omega_0$ and the orbits are centred ellipses.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) claiming that the angular momentum about $O$ is conserved because "the reaction is normal": only the axial component is; (2) expecting the orbital rate to depend on $r_0$ (it does not on a paraboloid); (3) linearising without the factor $1+r_0^2/c^2$, which gives $\omega_r=2\Omega_0$ for every radius; (4) solving the quartic for the turning points instead of noticing that $r_0$ is a root; (5) not checking $N\ge0$ for a unilateral contact.
Problem · Unit 5 · 10 points · 60 min · High difficulty / trap

P03 — Bead on a parabolic wire spinning about its axis: Coriolis side force, Jacobi integral and the critical rate

A smooth rigid wire lies in a vertical plane and has the shape of the parabola $y=x^2/(2p)$ ($p>0$), with its vertex at $O$ and its axis along the upward vertical. The wire is driven by a motor so that it spins about that vertical axis with constant angular rate $\Omega>0$. A bead $P$ of mass $m$ is threaded on the wire.

Let $S_0=\{O;\mathbf i_0,\mathbf j_0,\mathbf k_0\}$ be inertial ($\mathbf j_0$ vertical upward, gravity $-g\,\mathbf j_0$) and $S_1=\{O;\mathbf i_1,\mathbf j_1,\mathbf k_1\}$ be attached to the wire, with $\mathbf j_1=\mathbf j_0$, the wire in the plane $x_1y_1$ and $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\Omega\,\mathbf j_0$. At $t=0$ both frames coincide, so $\mathbf i_1$ has turned the angle $\theta=\Omega t$ about $\mathbf j_0$. The position of $P$ on the wire is given by its abscissa $x$ along $\mathbf i_1$ (positive or negative).

Figure 1. Wire $y=x^2/(2p)$ in the plane $x_1y_1$ of $S_1$, which turns with $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\Omega\,\mathbf j$ about the vertical axis; $\mathbf k_1$ is normal to the plane of the wire, gravity $-g\,\mathbf j$.

Setup

  • Data: $m$, $p$, $g$, $\Omega$. In part (g): $\Omega^2=2g/p$ and the bead is released at rest relative to the wire at $x_0=p/2$.
  • Hypotheses: point particle, no friction, bead threaded on the wire (bilateral constraint), $S_0$ inertial.
  • Write $q=\sqrt{1+x^2/p^2}$.

Part (a) · 1 point

Degrees of freedom and frames

Count the degrees of freedom and classify the constraints in $S_0$ and in $S_1$. Write the change-of-basis matrix $[{}_0R_1]$, check it, and give $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}$ and $\boldsymbol\alpha_{10}$.

Step 1. Count

In $S_1$ the wire is fixed: $z_1=0$ and $y_1-x_1^2/(2p)=0$ are two holonomic equations without time, so $3-2=1$ degree of freedom, coordinate $x$ (Notes §2.7). Seen from $S_0$ the same curve moves, so the constraints contain $t$ through $\theta=\Omega t$: rheonomic. This is why we work in $S_1$.

Step 2. Change of basis

The columns of $[{}_0R_1]$ are the components of $\mathbf i_1,\mathbf j_1,\mathbf k_1$ in $\mathcal B_0$ (Notes §1.5): $\mathbf i_1=\cos\theta\,\mathbf i_0-\sin\theta\,\mathbf k_0$, $\mathbf j_1=\mathbf j_0$, $\mathbf k_1=\sin\theta\,\mathbf i_0+\cos\theta\,\mathbf k_0$:

$$[{}_0R_1]=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&0&\sin\theta\\0&1&0\\-\sin\theta&0&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix},\qquad\det[{}_0R_1]=\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta=1,\qquad [{}_0R_1][{}_0R_1]^T=I$$

Step 3. Angular velocity

$\dot{\mathbf i}_1=\dot\theta(-\sin\theta\,\mathbf i_0-\cos\theta\,\mathbf k_0)=-\Omega\,\mathbf k_1$ and $\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf i_1=\Omega\,\mathbf j_1\times\mathbf i_1=-\Omega\,\mathbf k_1$ (Poisson, Notes §1.7), so $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\dot\theta\,\mathbf j_0=\Omega\,\mathbf j_0$, and $\boldsymbol\alpha_{10}=\dot{\boldsymbol\omega}_{10}=\mathbf 0$ because $\Omega$ is constant.

Key results
  • 1 degree of freedom, coordinate $x$. Two holonomic constraints, scleronomic in $S_1$ ($z_1=0$, $y_1=x_1^2/(2p)$), rheonomic in $S_0$.
  • $[{}_0R_1]=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&0&\sin\theta\\0&1&0\\-\sin\theta&0&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$, $\det=1$, $RR^T=I$; $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\Omega\,\mathbf j_0$, $\boldsymbol\alpha_{10}=\mathbf 0$.

Part (b) · 1.5 points

Relative and absolute kinematics

Write $\mathbf r$, $\mathbf v_{rel}$, $\mathbf a_{rel}$ in $\mathcal B_1$, and the absolute acceleration of $P$ with the Coriolis theorem.

Step 1. Relative motion

$\mathbf r=x\,\mathbf i_1+\dfrac{x^2}{2p}\mathbf j_1$. Differentiating in $S_1$ (where $\mathbf i_1,\mathbf j_1$ are constant), with the chain rule $\dfrac{d}{dt}\dfrac{x^2}{2p}=\dfrac{x\dot x}{p}$ and the product rule $\dfrac{d}{dt}\dfrac{x\dot x}{p}=\dfrac{\dot x^2+x\ddot x}{p}$:

$$\mathbf v_{rel}=\dot x\,\mathbf i_1+\frac{x\dot x}{p}\mathbf j_1,\qquad \mathbf a_{rel}=\ddot x\,\mathbf i_1+\frac{x\ddot x+\dot x^2}{p}\mathbf j_1$$

Step 2. Transport and Coriolis terms

$O$ is fixed and $\boldsymbol\alpha_{10}=\mathbf 0$, so (Notes §3.2) $\mathbf a=\mathbf a_{rel}+\boldsymbol\omega\times(\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf r)+2\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf v_{rel}$. With $\mathbf j_1\times\mathbf i_1=-\mathbf k_1$ and $\mathbf j_1\times\mathbf j_1=\mathbf 0$:

$$\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf r=-\Omega x\,\mathbf k_1,\qquad \boldsymbol\omega\times(\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf r)=\Omega\,\mathbf j_1\times(-\Omega x\,\mathbf k_1)=-\Omega^2x\,\mathbf i_1,\qquad 2\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf v_{rel}=-2\Omega\dot x\,\mathbf k_1$$
$$\mathbf a=\big(\ddot x-\Omega^2x\big)\mathbf i_1+\frac{x\ddot x+\dot x^2}{p}\mathbf j_1-2\Omega\dot x\,\mathbf k_1$$
Key results
  • $\mathbf v_{rel}=\dot x\Big(\mathbf i_1+\dfrac xp\mathbf j_1\Big)$, $\ \mathbf a_{rel}=\ddot x\Big(\mathbf i_1+\dfrac xp\mathbf j_1\Big)+\dfrac{\dot x^2}{p}\mathbf j_1$.
  • $\mathbf a_{tr}=-\Omega^2x\,\mathbf i_1$, $\ \mathbf a_{cor}=-2\Omega\dot x\,\mathbf k_1$, $\ \mathbf a=\mathbf a_{rel}+\mathbf a_{tr}+\mathbf a_{cor}$.

Part (c) · 1.5 points

Forces and inertia forces

Write the applied forces, the reaction of the wire and the inertia forces of $S_1$ in $\mathcal B_1$. Which of them do work in $S_1$?

Step 1. Applied force and reaction

$\mathbf W=-mg\,\mathbf j_1$. The tangent of the wire is along $\boldsymbol\tau=\mathbf i_1+\dfrac xp\mathbf j_1$; the normal plane is spanned by the in-plane normal $\mathbf n=\dfrac1q\Big(-\dfrac xp\mathbf i_1+\mathbf j_1\Big)$ and by $\mathbf k_1$. A smooth wire gives (Notes §6.5) $\mathbf N=N_n\,\mathbf n+N_z\,\mathbf k_1$, two unknowns.

Step 2. Inertia forces

In $S_1$ Newton's law reads $m\mathbf a_{rel}=\mathbf W+\mathbf N+\mathbf F_{cf}+\mathbf F_{cor}$ (Notes §4.9), with

$$\mathbf F_{cf}=-m\,\boldsymbol\omega\times(\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf r)=m\Omega^2x\,\mathbf i_1,\qquad \mathbf F_{cor}=-2m\,\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf v_{rel}=2m\Omega\dot x\,\mathbf k_1$$

There is no Euler term ($\boldsymbol\alpha=\mathbf 0$) and no translational term ($O$ fixed).

Step 3. Work in $S_1$

$\mathbf N\cdot\mathbf v_{rel}=0$ and $\mathbf F_{cor}\cdot\mathbf v_{rel}=0$ (both orthogonal to the tangent). $\mathbf W$ has potential $mgy=\dfrac{mgx^2}{2p}$ and $\mathbf F_{cf}$ has potential $V_{cf}=-\tfrac12m\Omega^2x^2$ (check: $-\dfrac{dV_{cf}}{dx}=m\Omega^2x$).

Key results
  • $\mathbf W=-mg\,\mathbf j_1$; $\ \mathbf N=N_n\mathbf n+N_z\mathbf k_1$ with $\mathbf n=\dfrac{-(x/p)\mathbf i_1+\mathbf j_1}{q}$.
  • $\mathbf F_{cf}=m\Omega^2x\,\mathbf i_1$ (potential $-\tfrac12m\Omega^2x^2$), $\ \mathbf F_{cor}=2m\Omega\dot x\,\mathbf k_1$ (no work in $S_1$).

Part (d) · 2 points

Equation of motion and reactions

Obtain the equation of motion free of reactions and the two components of the reaction.

Step 1. Tangential projection

Dot $m\mathbf a_{rel}=\mathbf W+\mathbf N+\mathbf F_{cf}+\mathbf F_{cor}$ with $\boldsymbol\tau=\mathbf i_1+\dfrac xp\mathbf j_1$. Left: $m\Big[\ddot x+\dfrac xp\cdot\dfrac{x\ddot x+\dot x^2}{p}\Big]$. Right: $\mathbf W\cdot\boldsymbol\tau=-\dfrac{mgx}{p}$, $\mathbf F_{cf}\cdot\boldsymbol\tau=m\Omega^2x$, and $\mathbf N\cdot\boldsymbol\tau=\mathbf F_{cor}\cdot\boldsymbol\tau=0$:

$$\Big(1+\frac{x^2}{p^2}\Big)\ddot x+\frac{x}{p^2}\dot x^2=x\Big(\Omega^2-\frac gp\Big)$$

Step 2. Out-of-plane projection

$\mathbf a_{rel}\cdot\mathbf k_1=0$, so $0=N_z+2m\Omega\dot x$:

$$N_z=-2m\Omega\dot x$$

The wire must cancel the Coriolis force. Seen from $S_0$: a bead moving outwards must gain the azimuthal speed $\Omega x$ of the wire; the drag direction of the rotation at $P$ is $\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf r=-\Omega x\,\mathbf k_1$, and for $x>0$, $\dot x>0$ the wire indeed pushes along $-\mathbf k_1$.

Step 3. In-plane normal projection

Dot with $q\,\mathbf n=-\dfrac xp\mathbf i_1+\mathbf j_1$. Left: $m\Big[-\dfrac xp\ddot x+\dfrac{x\ddot x+\dot x^2}{p}\Big]=\dfrac{m\dot x^2}{p}$. Right: $-mg$ (weight) $-\dfrac{m\Omega^2x^2}{p}$ (centrifugal) $+qN_n$:

$$N_n=\frac mq\Big(\frac{\dot x^2}{p}+\frac{\Omega^2x^2}{p}+g\Big)$$

a sum of positive terms: the wire always pushes the bead towards the concave side.

Step 4. Cross-check in $S_0$

As an extension (Lagrange equations are not in the course notes), the Lagrange equation of $L=\tfrac12m|\mathbf v|^2-mgy$ with the absolute velocity $\mathbf v=\mathbf v_{rel}-\Omega x\,\mathbf k_1$, $|\mathbf v|^2=q^2\dot x^2+\Omega^2x^2$, gives the same equation (checked with SymPy).

Key results
  • $\Big(1+\dfrac{x^2}{p^2}\Big)\ddot x+\dfrac{x}{p^2}\dot x^2=x\Big(\Omega^2-\dfrac gp\Big)$.
  • $N_z=-2m\Omega\dot x$; $\ N_n=\dfrac mq\Big(\dfrac{\dot x^2}{p}+\dfrac{\Omega^2x^2}{p}+g\Big)>0$.

Part (e) · 1 point

Jacobi first integral

Obtain a first integral of the motion. Is it the mechanical energy of the bead in $S_0$?

Step 1. Exact derivatives

By the product and chain rules, $\dfrac{d}{dt}\Big[\tfrac12\Big(1+\dfrac{x^2}{p^2}\Big)\dot x^2\Big]=\Big(1+\dfrac{x^2}{p^2}\Big)\dot x\ddot x+\dfrac{x\dot x}{p^2}\dot x^2=\dot x\Big[\Big(1+\dfrac{x^2}{p^2}\Big)\ddot x+\dfrac{x}{p^2}\dot x^2\Big]$, and $\dot x\cdot x\Big(\Omega^2-\dfrac gp\Big)=\dfrac{d}{dt}\Big[\tfrac12\Big(\Omega^2-\dfrac gp\Big)x^2\Big]$. Integrating from $0$ to $t$ (Barrow's rule):

$$J=\frac12\Big(1+\frac{x^2}{p^2}\Big)\dot x^2+\frac12\Big(\frac gp-\Omega^2\Big)x^2=J(0)$$

Step 2. Meaning

$\tfrac12|\mathbf v_{rel}|^2=\tfrac12q^2\dot x^2$ and the potentials of (c): $J=\tfrac12|\mathbf v_{rel}|^2+gy-\tfrac12\Omega^2x^2$. In $S_1$ only $\mathbf W$ and $\mathbf F_{cf}$ work, so this "relative energy" is conserved. In $S_0$, $\mathbf N\cdot\mathbf v=N_z\mathbf k_1\cdot(-\Omega x\,\mathbf k_1)=2m\Omega^2x\dot x\neq0$: the motor feeds energy to the bead.

Key results
  • $J=\tfrac12\Big(1+\dfrac{x^2}{p^2}\Big)\dot x^2+\tfrac12\Big(\dfrac gp-\Omega^2\Big)x^2=$ const (per unit mass).
  • It is the energy in $S_1$ including the centrifugal potential; the energy in $S_0$ is not conserved (the motor works through $N_z$).

Part (f) · 1.5 points

Relative equilibrium and the critical rate

Find the positions of relative equilibrium and their stability for $\Omega^2<g/p$, $\Omega^2=g/p$ and $\Omega^2>g/p$. Give the frequency of small oscillations when it exists. What happens at the critical rate?

Step 1. Equilibria

$\dot x=\ddot x=0$ in (d) gives $x(\Omega^2-g/p)=0$. If $\Omega^2\neq g/p$ only $x=0$ (the vertex). $V_{eff}''(0)=m(g/p-\Omega^2)$ (Notes §4.6): a minimum (stable) for $\Omega^2<g/p$, a maximum (unstable) for $\Omega^2>g/p$.

Step 2. Small oscillations

Linearising (d) about $x=0$ ($q\to1$, the $x\dot x^2$ term is of third order): $\ddot x+\Big(\dfrac gp-\Omega^2\Big)x=0$, so $\omega=\sqrt{g/p-\Omega^2}$ for $\Omega^2<g/p$.

Step 3. The critical rate (the trap)

For $\Omega^2=g/p$ the linearised equation is $\ddot x=0$, which does not decide anything by itself. The full equation becomes $q^2\ddot x+\dfrac{x}{p^2}\dot x^2=0$, so $\ddot x=0$ whenever $\dot x=0$, at any $x$: every point is a relative equilibrium. $J$ reduces to $\tfrac12q^2\dot x^2=|\mathbf v_{rel}|^2/2=$ const: the bead slides with constant relative speed and never comes back (neutral, not stable). Geometric reason: at relative rest the resultant of gravity and centrifugal force must be normal to the wire, i.e. $dy/dx=\Omega^2x/g$; the parabola has $dy/dx=x/p$, which matches at every $x$ exactly when $\Omega^2=g/p$ (it is the shape of the free surface of a liquid spinning at that rate).

Key results
  • $x=0$ is an equilibrium for every $\Omega$: stable for $\Omega^2<g/p$ ($\omega^2=g/p-\Omega^2$), unstable for $\Omega^2>g/p$.
  • $\Omega^2=g/p$: every point of the wire is an equilibrium (neutral), and a bead with relative speed slides at constant speed along the wire: $q^2\dot x^2=$ const.

Part (g) · 1.5 points

Release at $\Omega^2=2g/p$: escape and side force

For $\Omega^2=2g/p$ and release from relative rest at $x_0=p/2$: show that the bead moves outwards, give $\dot x^2(x)$ and $N_z(x)$, their values at $x=p$ and their limits for $x\to\infty$.

Step 1. Direction

With $\dot x=0$ in (d): $q_0^2\ddot x=x_0\Big(\dfrac{2g}{p}-\dfrac gp\Big)=\dfrac{g x_0}{p}>0$. The bead starts outwards (the vertex is unstable, part (f)).

Step 2. Speed

$J(x,\dot x)=J(x_0,0)$ with $g/p-\Omega^2=-g/p$:

$$\frac12\Big(1+\frac{x^2}{p^2}\Big)\dot x^2-\frac{g}{2p}x^2=-\frac{g}{2p}x_0^2\ \Longrightarrow\ \dot x^2=\frac gp\,\frac{x^2-x_0^2}{1+x^2/p^2}$$

$\dot x^2\ge0$ only for $|x|\ge x_0$: the bead never returns towards the vertex, and $x$ grows monotonically. Consistency: $\dfrac{d(\dot x^2)}{dx}=2\ddot x$ reproduces (d) (checked with SymPy).

Step 3. Side force

$N_z=-2m\Omega\dot x$ with $\Omega=\sqrt{2g/p}$ and $\dot x>0$:

$$N_z=-2m\sqrt{\frac{2g}{p}}\sqrt{\frac gp\,\frac{x^2-x_0^2}{1+x^2/p^2}}=-\frac{2\sqrt2\,mg}{p}\sqrt{\frac{x^2-x_0^2}{1+x^2/p^2}}$$

Step 4. Values

At $x=p$ ($x_0=p/2$): $\dot x^2=\dfrac gp\cdot\dfrac{p^2-p^2/4}{2}=\dfrac38gp$, and $N_z=-2m\sqrt{\dfrac{2g}{p}}\sqrt{\dfrac{3gp}{8}}=-2mg\sqrt{\dfrac34}=-\sqrt3\,mg$. The in-plane reaction there: $\dfrac{\dot x^2}{p}+\dfrac{\Omega^2x^2}{p}+g=\dfrac38g+2g+g=\dfrac{27}{8}g$ and $q=\sqrt2$, so $N_n=\dfrac{27}{8\sqrt2}mg\approx2.386\,mg$. The relative speed is then $|\mathbf v_{rel}|^2=q^2\dot x^2=\tfrac34gp$.

Step 5. Limits

For $x\to\infty$, $\dfrac{x^2-x_0^2}{1+x^2/p^2}\to p^2$, so $\dot x^2\to gp$ and $|N_z|\to2\sqrt2\,mg$. The abscissa grows at a bounded rate, while the speed along the wire $q|\dot x|$ grows without bound because the wire becomes steep.

Key results
  • $\ddot x(x_0)=\dfrac{(g/p)\,x_0}{1+x_0^2/p^2}>0$; $\ \dot x^2=\dfrac gp\,\dfrac{x^2-x_0^2}{1+x^2/p^2}$.
  • $N_z=-\dfrac{2\sqrt2\,mg}{p}\sqrt{\dfrac{x^2-x_0^2}{1+x^2/p^2}}$; at $x=p$: $\dot x^2=\tfrac38gp$, $N_z=-\sqrt3\,mg\approx-1.732\,mg$, $N_n=\dfrac{27}{8\sqrt2}mg\approx2.386\,mg$.
  • $x\to\infty$: $\dot x^2\to gp$ and $|N_z|\to2\sqrt2\,mg\approx2.83\,mg$.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $g/p$ and $\Omega^2$ are s$^{-2}$; in (d) every term is m s$^{-2}$ ($x\dot x^2/p^2$ is m (m/s)$^2$/m$^2$); $N_z=2m\Omega\dot x$ is kg s$^{-1}$ m s$^{-1}$ = N; $\dot x^2\to gp$ is m$^2$ s$^{-2}$.
  • Limits: $\Omega=0$: a bead in a fixed parabolic bowl, $\omega=\sqrt{g/p}$ about the vertex and $N_z=0$; $p\to\infty$ (straight horizontal wire): always $\Omega^2>g/p$, and $x(t)$ grows as $\cosh\Omega t$, the classic rotating rod.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) forgetting $N_z$ (the reaction of a wire has two components; the Coriolis force needs one of them); (2) writing the Jacobi integral as the absolute energy (the motor does work in $S_0$); (3) at $\Omega^2=g/p$, concluding "stable" or "unstable" from $\ddot x=0$: every point is an equilibrium and a moving bead keeps a constant relative speed; (4) dropping the $(x/p^2)\dot x^2$ term, which comes from differentiating the constraint $y=x^2/(2p)$ twice.
Problem · Unit 5 · 10 points · 60 min · Standard exam level

P04 — Radial tube on a turntable with increasing rate: Euler force, three regimes and the motor torque

A horizontal turntable turns about the fixed vertical axis $O\mathbf k$ of the inertial frame $S_0=\{O;\mathbf i_0,\mathbf j_0,\mathbf k\}$ ($\mathbf k$ vertical upward, gravity $-g\,\mathbf k$). A motor imposes the angle $\theta(t)$ between $\mathbf i_0$ and a radial line of the table, with

$$\dot\theta=\Omega(t)=\Omega_0+\beta t,\qquad\theta(0)=0$$

($\Omega_0>0$ and $\beta$ constants). A smooth, thin, straight tube is fixed on the table along a diameter of the table, from the rim at $x=-L$ through $O$ to the rim at $x=L$. A bead $P$ of mass $m$ moves inside the tube and is joined to $O$ by a spring of stiffness $k$ and zero natural length. The frame $S_1=\{O;\mathbf i_1,\mathbf j_1,\mathbf k\}$ turns with the table, $\mathbf i_1$ along the tube and $\mathbf j_1=\mathbf k\times\mathbf i_1$; $x$ is the signed coordinate of $P$ along $\mathbf i_1$ (so $x<0$ means on the other side of $O$). The table and the tube have moment of inertia $I_T$ about the axis.

Figure 1. Top view: turntable with rate $\Omega(t)=\dot\theta=\Omega_0+\beta t$ about the vertical axis $O\mathbf k$ (out of the page); smooth tube along $\mathbf i_1$, bead $P$ at distance $x$ from $O$, spring of stiffness $k$ and zero natural length.

(The scalar $k$ is the stiffness; the bold $\mathbf k$ is the vertical unit vector.)

Setup

  • Data: $m$, $k$, $g$, $\Omega_0$, $\beta$, $I_T$, $L$.
  • Numerical parts (e), (f): $m=0.2$ kg, $k=20$ N/m (so $\omega_n=\sqrt{k/m}=10$ rad/s), $\beta=0$, $x(0)=x_0=0.1$ m, $\dot x(0)=v_0=0$, $L=0.5$ m, $g=9.81$ m s$^{-2}$, and $\Omega_0=6$, $10$ or $12.5$ rad/s.
  • Hypotheses: point particle, no friction in the tube, $S_0$ inertial, the motor delivers whatever torque is needed to keep $\Omega(t)$.

Part (a) · 1 point

Degrees of freedom and frames

Count the degrees of freedom of $P$. Give $[{}_0R_1]$, $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}$ and $\boldsymbol\alpha_{10}$.

Step 1. Count

In $S_1$ the tube is the line $y_1=0$, $z_1=0$: two holonomic constraints, so $3-2=1$ degree of freedom, $x$ (Notes §2.7). In $S_0$ the tube turns with the prescribed $\theta(t)$, so the constraints depend explicitly on time (rheonomic).

Step 2. Frames

$\mathbf i_1=\cos\theta\,\mathbf i_0+\sin\theta\,\mathbf j_0$, $\mathbf j_1=-\sin\theta\,\mathbf i_0+\cos\theta\,\mathbf j_0$: the columns of $[{}_0R_1]$ above, a simple rotation about $\mathbf k$ (Notes §1.5) with $\det=\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta=1$ and $RR^T=I$. Then $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\dot\theta\,\mathbf k=\Omega(t)\,\mathbf k$ and $\boldsymbol\alpha_{10}=\dot\Omega\,\mathbf k=\beta\,\mathbf k$.

Key results
  • 1 degree of freedom, $x$; constraints $y_1=0$, $z_1=0$ (rheonomic in $S_0$).
  • $[{}_0R_1]=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta&0\\\sin\theta&\cos\theta&0\\0&0&1\end{pmatrix}$, $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\Omega(t)\,\mathbf k$, $\boldsymbol\alpha_{10}=\beta\,\mathbf k$.

Part (b) · 1.5 points

Kinematics

Write the relative velocity and acceleration of $P$ and its absolute acceleration in $\mathcal B_1$, (i) with the Coriolis theorem and (ii) by differentiating $\mathbf r=x\,\mathbf i_1$ directly in $S_0$.

Step 1. Coriolis theorem

$O$ is fixed, so (Notes §3.2) $\mathbf a=\mathbf a_{rel}+\boldsymbol\alpha\times\mathbf r+\boldsymbol\omega\times(\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf r)+2\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf v_{rel}$, with $\mathbf r=x\,\mathbf i_1$ and $\mathbf k\times\mathbf i_1=\mathbf j_1$, $\mathbf k\times\mathbf j_1=-\mathbf i_1$:

$$\boldsymbol\alpha\times\mathbf r=\beta x\,\mathbf j_1,\qquad\boldsymbol\omega\times(\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf r)=\Omega\,\mathbf k\times\Omega x\,\mathbf j_1=-\Omega^2x\,\mathbf i_1,\qquad2\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf v_{rel}=2\Omega\dot x\,\mathbf j_1$$
$$\mathbf a=(\ddot x-\Omega^2x)\,\mathbf i_1+(2\Omega\dot x+\beta x)\,\mathbf j_1$$

Step 2. Direct differentiation

$\mathbf v=\dfrac{d}{dt}(x\,\mathbf i_1)=\dot x\,\mathbf i_1+x\Omega\,\mathbf j_1$ (product rule). Again, with $\dfrac{d}{dt}(x\Omega)=\dot x\Omega+x\dot\Omega=\dot x\Omega+x\beta$:

$$\mathbf a=\ddot x\,\mathbf i_1+\dot x\Omega\,\mathbf j_1+(\dot x\Omega+x\beta)\,\mathbf j_1+x\Omega(-\Omega\,\mathbf i_1)$$

which is the same result. The term $\beta x\,\mathbf j_1$ is the tangential acceleration of the point of the table under $P$.

Key results
  • $\mathbf v_{rel}=\dot x\,\mathbf i_1$, $\mathbf a_{rel}=\ddot x\,\mathbf i_1$.
  • $\mathbf a=(\ddot x-\Omega^2x)\,\mathbf i_1+(2\Omega\dot x+\beta x)\,\mathbf j_1$.

Part (c) · 1.5 points

Forces and inertia forces

Write all the forces on $P$ in $S_1$, including the three inertia forces.

Step 1. Applied forces and reaction

The spring has zero natural length, so its force is $-k\,\overrightarrow{OP}=-kx\,\mathbf i_1$. The weight is $-mg\,\mathbf k$. The tube is smooth: its reaction has no $\mathbf i_1$ component, $\mathbf N=N_y\,\mathbf j_1+N_z\,\mathbf k$.

Step 2. Inertia forces of $S_1$

From the terms of (b) multiplied by $-m$ (Notes §4.9):

$$\mathbf F_E=-m\beta x\,\mathbf j_1,\qquad\mathbf F_{cf}=m\Omega^2x\,\mathbf i_1,\qquad\mathbf F_{cor}=-2m\Omega\dot x\,\mathbf j_1$$

The Euler (azimuthal) force exists only because the rotation accelerates; it points against the sense of rotation for $\beta>0$.

Key results
  • $\mathbf F_k=-kx\,\mathbf i_1$, $\mathbf W=-mg\,\mathbf k$, $\mathbf N=N_y\,\mathbf j_1+N_z\,\mathbf k$.
  • $\mathbf F_{E}=-m\beta x\,\mathbf j_1$ (Euler), $\mathbf F_{cf}=m\Omega^2x\,\mathbf i_1$, $\mathbf F_{cor}=-2m\Omega\dot x\,\mathbf j_1$.

Part (d) · 1.5 points

Equation of motion and reactions

Obtain the equation of motion free of reactions, and $N_y$, $N_z$. Why does the equation contain $\Omega^2$ but not $\beta$?

Step 1. Projections

$$\mathbf i_1:\ m\ddot x=-kx+m\Omega^2x\qquad\mathbf j_1:\ 0=N_y-m\beta x-2m\Omega\dot x\qquad\mathbf k:\ 0=N_z-mg$$

Step 2. Result

$$\ddot x+\Big(\frac km-\Omega(t)^2\Big)x=0,\qquad N_y=m(\beta x+2\Omega\dot x),\qquad N_z=mg$$

Step 3. Why no $\beta$

The Euler force $-m\beta x\,\mathbf j_1$ and the Coriolis force are perpendicular to the tube: the wall absorbs them and they appear only in $N_y$. The angular acceleration still acts on $x$, but only through the time-dependent coefficient $\Omega(t)^2=(\Omega_0+\beta t)^2$. (In $S_0$ the same equation is the $\mathbf i_1$ component of $m\mathbf a$ of (b).)

Key results
  • $\ddot x+\Big(\dfrac km-\Omega(t)^2\Big)x=0$.
  • $N_y=m(\beta x+2\Omega\dot x)$, $\ N_z=mg$.

Part (e) · 2 points

Constant rate: the three regimes

Take $\beta=0$. Solve the equation of motion with $x(0)=x_0$, $\dot x(0)=v_0$ for $\Omega_0^2<k/m$, $\Omega_0^2=k/m$ and $\Omega_0^2>k/m$, write the Jacobi integral, and evaluate each case for the data of the Setup ($\Omega_0=6$, $10$, $12.5$ rad/s).

Step 1. $\Omega_0^2<k/m$

$\ddot x+\omega^2x=0$ with $\omega^2=k/m-\Omega_0^2>0$ (Notes Ch. 5): $x=A\cos\omega t+B\sin\omega t$. $x(0)=A=x_0$; $\dot x=-A\omega\sin\omega t+B\omega\cos\omega t$ gives $\dot x(0)=B\omega=v_0$. Data, $\Omega_0=6$: $\omega=\sqrt{100-36}=8$ rad/s, $x=0.1\cos8t$ m, period $2\pi/8=0.7854$ s. The spring wins over the centrifugal force.

Step 2. $\Omega_0^2=k/m$ (the trap)

$\ddot x=0$: integrating twice with Barrow's rule, $\dot x=v_0$ and $x=x_0+v_0t$. With $v_0=0$ the bead stays where it is, wherever that is: the spring force $kx$ is exactly the centrifugal force $m\Omega_0^2x$ at every $x$. With $v_0\neq0$ the bead drifts at constant relative speed. It is neither an oscillation of zero frequency nor an exponential escape. Data, $\Omega_0=10$: $x\equiv0.1$ m.

Step 3. $\Omega_0^2>k/m$

$\ddot x-\lambda^2x=0$ with $\lambda^2=\Omega_0^2-k/m$: $x=A\cosh\lambda t+B\sinh\lambda t$, with $x(0)=A=x_0$ and $\dot x(0)=B\lambda=v_0$. Writing it as $\tfrac12(x_0+v_0/\lambda)e^{\lambda t}+\tfrac12(x_0-v_0/\lambda)e^{-\lambda t}$, the growing exponential disappears only for $v_0=-\lambda x_0$, when $x=x_0e^{-\lambda t}$. Data, $\Omega_0=12.5$: $\lambda=\sqrt{156.25-100}=7.5$ s$^{-1}$, $x=0.1\cosh7.5t$ m.

Step 4. Jacobi integral

Multiplying the equation by $\dot x$: $\dfrac{d}{dt}\Big[\tfrac12\dot x^2+\tfrac12\Big(\dfrac km-\Omega_0^2\Big)x^2\Big]=0$, so

$$\tfrac12\dot x^2+\tfrac12\Big(\frac km-\Omega_0^2\Big)x^2=\text{const}$$

the energy in $S_1$ with the spring and centrifugal potentials. $\mathbf N$, $\mathbf F_{cor}$ and $\mathbf F_E$ do no work in $S_1$ because they are orthogonal to $\mathbf v_{rel}$.

Key results
  • $\Omega_0^2<\omega_n^2$: $x=x_0\cos\omega t+\dfrac{v_0}{\omega}\sin\omega t$, $\omega=\sqrt{\omega_n^2-\Omega_0^2}$.
  • $\Omega_0^2=\omega_n^2$: $x=x_0+v_0t$ (every position is a neutral relative equilibrium).
  • $\Omega_0^2>\omega_n^2$: $x=x_0\cosh\lambda t+\dfrac{v_0}{\lambda}\sinh\lambda t$, $\lambda=\sqrt{\Omega_0^2-\omega_n^2}$; it decays only if $v_0=-\lambda x_0$.
  • Data: $\omega=8$ rad/s, $x=0.1\cos8t$ m, period $0.7854$ s; $x\equiv0.1$ m; $\lambda=7.5$ s$^{-1}$, $x=0.1\cosh7.5t$ m.

Part (f) · 1 point

Reaching the end of the tube

For $\Omega_0=12.5$ rad/s, $\beta=0$ and the data of the Setup, find the time at which the bead reaches the rim ($x=L$), its relative speed then, and the reaction there.

Step 1. Time

$0.1\cosh7.5t_L=0.5\Rightarrow\cosh7.5t_L=5\Rightarrow t_L=\dfrac{\operatorname{arccosh}5}{7.5}=\dfrac{2.2924}{7.5}=0.3057$ s.

Step 2. Speed

$\dot x=x_0\lambda\sinh\lambda t$ with $\sinh\lambda t_L=\sqrt{25-1}=\sqrt{24}$: $\dot x=0.1\cdot7.5\cdot\sqrt{24}=3.674$ m/s. Check with the Jacobi integral: $\dot x^2=\lambda^2(L^2-x_0^2)=56.25\cdot0.24=13.5$ m$^2$ s$^{-2}$.

Step 3. Reaction

$N_y=m(0+2\Omega_0\dot x)=2\cdot0.2\cdot12.5\cdot3.674=18.37$ N, about nine times the weight $N_z=mg=1.962$ N.

Key results
  • $t_L=\dfrac{\operatorname{arccosh}5}{7.5}=0.3057$ s; $\dot x=0.75\sqrt{24}=3.674$ m/s.
  • $N_y=2m\Omega_0\dot x=18.37$ N, $N_z=mg=1.962$ N.

Part (g) · 1.5 points

Motor torque and power balance

With the angular-momentum theorem about the axis, find the torque $M$ the motor must deliver to keep $\Omega(t)$, and check it against $N_y$. Write the power balance of the bead in $S_0$. For the case of (f), give $M$ (beyond $I_T\beta$) and the power at $x=L$, and the work of the motor on the bead between $x_0$ and $L$.

Step 1. Angular momentum of the bead about the axis

$\mathbf r\times m\mathbf v=x\,\mathbf i_1\times m(\dot x\,\mathbf i_1+\Omega x\,\mathbf j_1)=mx^2\Omega\,\mathbf k$, so $H_z=mx^2\Omega$. By the product rule:

$$\frac{dH_z}{dt}=m\big(2x\dot x\,\Omega+x^2\dot\Omega\big)=m\big(2x\dot x\,\Omega+x^2\beta\big)$$

Step 2. Torque on the bead

Moments about the axis (Notes §4.8): the spring force passes through $O$; $\mathbf r\times\mathbf W=x\,\mathbf i_1\times(-mg\,\mathbf k)=mgx\,\mathbf j_1$ and $\mathbf r\times N_z\mathbf k$ are horizontal; only $\mathbf r\times N_y\mathbf j_1=xN_y\,\mathbf k$ counts. Hence $\dfrac{dH_z}{dt}=xN_y$, and with $N_y$ of (d): $xN_y=mx(\beta x+2\Omega\dot x)$, the same expression as Step 1. The two routes agree.

Step 3. Motor torque

The table receives the motor torque $M$ and the moment $-xN_y$ of the bead's push on the tube: $I_T\beta=M-xN_y$, so

$$M=I_T\beta+mx\big(\beta x+2\Omega\dot x\big)$$

Even at constant rate ($\beta=0$) the motor must supply $2m\Omega x\dot x$ whenever the bead moves radially.

Step 4. Power balance in $S_0$

$T=\tfrac12m(\dot x^2+\Omega^2x^2)$, $V=\tfrac12kx^2$. Differentiating, $\dfrac{d}{dt}(T+V)=m\dot x\ddot x+m\Omega\beta x^2+m\Omega^2x\dot x+kx\dot x$, and with $m\ddot x=-kx+m\Omega^2x$ from (d): $\dfrac{d}{dt}(T+V)=2m\Omega^2x\dot x+m\Omega\beta x^2=\Omega\,xN_y$. This is the power of $N_y\mathbf j_1$ on the absolute velocity, $N_y\,\Omega x$ (Notes §4.4): in $S_0$ the reaction of the moving tube works; in $S_1$ it does not.

Step 5. Numbers at $x=L$ (case (f), $\beta=0$)

$xN_y=0.5\cdot18.37=9.186$ N m and the power is $\Omega_0xN_y=12.5\cdot9.186=114.8$ W. Since $\beta=0$, the work of the motor on the bead is $\displaystyle\int M\Omega_0\,dt=\Omega_0\int\frac{dH_z}{dt}dt=\Omega_0\,[H_z]_{0}^{t_L}=\Omega_0\big(H_z(t_L)-H_z(0)\big)=m\Omega_0^2(L^2-x_0^2)=0.2\cdot156.25\cdot0.24=7.5$ J. Check: $T$ goes from $0.15625$ to $\tfrac12\cdot0.2\,(13.5+39.0625)=5.25625$ J and $V$ from $0.1$ to $2.5$ J, so $\Delta(T+V)=5.1+2.4=7.5$ J.

Key results
  • $H_z=mx^2\Omega$, $\ \dfrac{dH_z}{dt}=m(2x\dot x\Omega+x^2\beta)=xN_y$; $\ M=I_T\beta+mx(\beta x+2\Omega\dot x)$.
  • $\dfrac{d}{dt}(T+V)=\Omega\,xN_y=M_{bead}\,\Omega$ (the reaction works in $S_0$, not in $S_1$).
  • Case (f): $xN_y=9.186$ N m, power $114.8$ W, work $=\Omega_0\Delta H_z=m\Omega_0^2(L^2-x_0^2)=7.5$ J $=\Delta(T+V)$.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $k/m$ and $\Omega^2$ are s$^{-2}$; $N_y=m(\beta x+2\Omega\dot x)$ is kg (s$^{-2}$ m) = N; $H_z=mx^2\Omega$ is kg m$^2$ s$^{-1}$; $M$ is N m and $M\Omega$ is W.
  • Limits: $k=0$ gives the classic bead on a rotating rod, $x=x_0\cosh\Omega_0t$; $\Omega_0=0$, $\beta=0$ gives the plain oscillator $\omega=\omega_n$. With $\beta>0$ the coefficient $k/m-\Omega(t)^2$ eventually becomes negative: a bead that oscillates at first is pushed outwards once $\Omega(t)>\omega_n$.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) forgetting the Euler force $-m\boldsymbol\alpha\times\mathbf r$ (it changes $N_y$ and the torque, not the equation of motion); (2) at $\Omega_0^2=k/m$ writing an exponential or a harmonic solution instead of $x=x_0+v_0t$; (3) claiming that the energy $T+V$ of the bead is conserved because the tube is smooth: in $S_0$ the tube moves and its reaction works; (4) computing the motor torque without the $I_T\beta$ term of the table, or with the wrong sign of the bead's push.
Problem · Unit 5 · 10 points · 60 min · Standard exam level

P05 — Flight pendulum on an accelerating support: inertia forces, equilibrium and a change of regime

A flight-test pendulum hangs from a rail fixed to the ceiling of an aircraft cabin. The support $A$ slides along the rail with a constant acceleration $\mathbf a_A=a_0\,\mathbf i$ with respect to the inertial frame $S_0=\{O;\mathbf i,\mathbf j,\mathbf k\}$ ($x$ horizontal, forward; $y$ vertical, upward; uniform gravity $-g\,\mathbf j$), starting from rest at $t=0$. The bob $P$ of mass $m$ is joined to $A$ by a massless, inextensible string of length $\ell$ that stays taut. The angle $\theta$ is measured from the downward vertical, positive towards $+x$. At $t=0$ the bob hangs at rest with respect to the cabin ($\theta=0$, $\dot\theta=0$).

Figure 1. Flight pendulum: support $A$ with acceleration $\mathbf a_A=a_0\,\mathbf i$, string of length $\ell$, bob $P$ of mass $m$, angle $\theta$ from the downward vertical, positive towards $+x$.

Use the reference frame $S_1=\{A;\mathbf i,\mathbf j,\mathbf k\}$ that translates with $A$ (its axes stay parallel to those of $S_0$), and, only to project vectors, the unit vectors $\mathbf e_r=\sin\theta\,\mathbf i-\cos\theta\,\mathbf j$ and $\mathbf e_\theta=\cos\theta\,\mathbf i+\sin\theta\,\mathbf j$.

Setup

  • Data: $m$, $\ell$, $g$, $a_0>0$. In the numerical parts (f) and (g): $a_0=g/\sqrt3$.
  • Hypotheses: point particle, planar motion, string always taut (checked in (g)), no friction, $S_0$ inertial.
  • $S_1$ is a translating frame: $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\mathbf 0$, so the only inertia force is $-m\,\mathbf a_A$ (no Coriolis, no centrifugal force).

Part (a) · 1 point

Degrees of freedom and frames

How many effective degrees of freedom has $P$? State the angular velocity of $S_1$ with respect to $S_0$ and the kind of inertia force that appears in $S_1$.

Step 1. Count

A free particle in the plane has 2 degrees of freedom. The string gives the holonomic constraint $|\mathbf r_{AP}|=\ell$, which removes one: $\theta$ describes the configuration. The motion of $A$ is given ($\mathbf a_A=a_0\mathbf i$), so it adds no unknown: the constraint is rheonomic in $S_0$ and scleronomic in $S_1$.

Step 2. The frame

$S_1$ keeps its axes parallel to those of $S_0$, so $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\mathbf 0$. In the relative-motion law $\mathbf F+\mathbf F_{in}=m\mathbf a_{rel}$ (Notes §4.9) the Coriolis term $-2m\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf v_{rel}$ and the centrifugal term vanish, and only $\mathbf F_{in}=-m\mathbf a_A$ remains.

Key results
  • 1 degree of freedom, coordinate $\theta(t)$.
  • $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\mathbf 0$; only the translational inertia force $-m a_0\mathbf i$.

Part (b) · 1.5 points

Kinematics of $P$

Write the position, velocity and acceleration of $P$ relative to $S_1$, and the absolute velocity and acceleration of $P$ in $S_0$, in the basis $\{\mathbf e_r,\mathbf e_\theta\}$.

Change of basis

The projection basis $\{\mathbf e_r,\mathbf e_\theta\}$ is related to $\{\mathbf i,\mathbf j\}$ (planar, $\mathbf k$ common) by

$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf e_r\cr\mathbf e_\theta\end{pmatrix}=R\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf i\cr\mathbf j\end{pmatrix},\qquad R=\begin{pmatrix}\sin\theta&-\cos\theta\cr\cos\theta&\sin\theta\end{pmatrix}$$
$$\det R=\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1,\qquad RR^T=\begin{pmatrix}\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta&0\cr0&\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta\end{pmatrix}=I$$

so $R$ is orthonormal and right-handed ($\mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_\theta=\mathbf k$). The inverse is $R^T$: $\mathbf i=\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_r+\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$, used below.

Step 1. Derivatives of the unit vectors

From $\mathbf e_r=\sin\theta\,\mathbf i-\cos\theta\,\mathbf j$ and the chain rule $\frac{d}{dt}f(\theta(t))=\frac{df}{d\theta}\dot\theta$:

$$\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot\theta\,(\cos\theta\,\mathbf i+\sin\theta\,\mathbf j)=\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta,\qquad \dot{\mathbf e}_\theta=\dot\theta\,(-\sin\theta\,\mathbf i+\cos\theta\,\mathbf j)=-\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_r$$

Step 2. Relative motion

$$\mathbf r_{rel}=\ell\,\mathbf e_r,\qquad \mathbf v_{rel}=\ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta,\qquad \mathbf a_{rel}=\ell\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta+\ell\dot\theta\,\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta=\ell\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta-\ell\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_r$$

($\ell$ is constant, so the string produces no radial relative velocity.)

Step 3. Absolute motion

The support starts from rest with constant acceleration: $\mathbf v_A=a_0t\,\mathbf i$, $\mathbf r_A=\tfrac12a_0t^2\,\mathbf i$. Since $S_1$ does not rotate, $\mathbf v_P=\mathbf v_A+\mathbf v_{rel}$ and $\mathbf a_P=\mathbf a_A+\mathbf a_{rel}$:

$$\mathbf v_P=a_0t\,\mathbf i+\ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta,\qquad \mathbf a_P=a_0\,\mathbf i+\ell\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta-\ell\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_r$$

Projecting $\mathbf i=\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_r+\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$ (the dot products $\mathbf i\cdot\mathbf e_r=\sin\theta$, $\mathbf i\cdot\mathbf e_\theta=\cos\theta$):

$$\mathbf a_P=\big(a_0\sin\theta-\ell\dot\theta^2\big)\mathbf e_r+\big(a_0\cos\theta+\ell\ddot\theta\big)\mathbf e_\theta$$
Key results
  • $\mathbf a_{rel}=\ell\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta-\ell\dot\theta^2\mathbf e_r$.
  • $\mathbf a_P=a_0\mathbf i+\mathbf a_{rel}$ with $\mathbf i=\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_r+\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$.

Part (c) · 1.5 points

Forces on $P$ in $S_1$

Write every force acting on $P$ in $S_1$ in the basis $\{\mathbf e_r,\mathbf e_\theta\}$, including the inertia force. Which of them can do work in $S_1$?

Step 1. Weight

$\mathbf W=-mg\,\mathbf j$ with $\mathbf W\cdot\mathbf e_r=-mg(\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf e_r)=mg\cos\theta$ and $\mathbf W\cdot\mathbf e_\theta=-mg\sin\theta$:

$$\mathbf W=mg\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_r-mg\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$$

Step 2. Tension and inertia force

The string is massless and taut, so its force on $P$ points to $A$: $\mathbf T=-T\,\mathbf e_r$ with $T\ge0$ unknown. The inertia force of the translating frame is

$$\mathbf F_{in}=-m\,\mathbf a_A=-ma_0\,\mathbf i=-ma_0\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_r-ma_0\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$$

Step 3. Work in $S_1$

The power of a force is $\mathbf F\cdot\mathbf v_{rel}$ with $\mathbf v_{rel}=\ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$: only the $\mathbf e_\theta$ components work. $\mathbf T\cdot\mathbf v_{rel}=0$. $\mathbf W$ and $\mathbf F_{in}$ are uniform forces, hence conservative, with potentials $V_g=-mg\ell\cos\theta$ and $V_{in}=ma_0\ell\sin\theta$ (check: $-\partial V/\partial\theta/\ell$ gives their $\mathbf e_\theta$ components).

Key results
  • $\mathbf W=mg(\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_r-\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta)$, $\ \mathbf T=-T\mathbf e_r$, $\ \mathbf F_{in}=-ma_0(\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_r+\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta)$.
  • $\mathbf T$ does no work (it is orthogonal to $\mathbf v_{rel}$); $\mathbf W$ and $\mathbf F_{in}$ are conservative.

Part (d) · 2 points

Equation of motion and tension

Obtain the equation $\ddot\theta=f(\theta)$ that does not contain $T$, and the expression of $T$ as a function of $\theta$ and $\dot\theta$.

Step 1. Newton in $S_1$

$$m\big(\ell\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta-\ell\dot\theta^2\mathbf e_r\big)=\mathbf W+\mathbf T+\mathbf F_{in}$$

Step 2. Tangential projection

$$m\ell\ddot\theta=-mg\sin\theta-ma_0\cos\theta\ \Longrightarrow\ \ell\ddot\theta=-g\sin\theta-a_0\cos\theta$$

This equation has one unknown, $\theta(t)$, because the tension is orthogonal to $\mathbf e_\theta$.

Step 3. Radial projection

$$-m\ell\dot\theta^2=mg\cos\theta-T-ma_0\sin\theta\ \Longrightarrow\ T=m\big(\ell\dot\theta^2+g\cos\theta-a_0\sin\theta\big)$$

Step 4. Cross-check with the absolute frame

Newton in $S_0$ along $\mathbf e_r$: $m\,\mathbf a_P\cdot\mathbf e_r=m(a_0\sin\theta-\ell\dot\theta^2)=\mathbf W\cdot\mathbf e_r-T=mg\cos\theta-T$, which gives the same $T$.

Key results
  • $\ell\ddot\theta=-g\sin\theta-a_0\cos\theta$.
  • $T=m\big(\ell\dot\theta^2+g\cos\theta-a_0\sin\theta\big)$.

Part (e) · 1 point

Equilibrium and small oscillations

Find the equilibrium angle $\theta_e$ and the frequency of the small oscillations about it, as functions of $a_0$, $g$, $\ell$. What does the pendulum indicate?

Step 1. Equilibrium

Setting $\ddot\theta=0$: $g\sin\theta+a_0\cos\theta=0\Rightarrow\tan\theta_e=-a_0/g$. Two solutions differ by $\pi$; the stable one hangs below $A$: $\theta_e=-\arctan(a_0/g)$.

Step 2. Rewrite the equation

With $\cos\alpha=g/g_{eff}$, $\sin\alpha=a_0/g_{eff}$ ($\alpha=\arctan(a_0/g)$) and the identity $\sin(\theta+\alpha)=\sin\theta\cos\alpha+\cos\theta\sin\alpha$:

$$\ell\ddot\theta=-g_{eff}\big(\sin\theta\cos\alpha+\cos\theta\sin\alpha\big)=-g_{eff}\sin(\theta+\alpha)$$

so $\theta_e=-\alpha$. Define $\varphi=\theta-\theta_e=\theta+\alpha$: $\ \ell\ddot\varphi=-g_{eff}\sin\varphi$, an ordinary pendulum in a gravity $g_{eff}$.

Step 3. Linearisation

For $|\varphi|\ll1$, $\sin\varphi\approx\varphi$ and $\ddot\varphi+\dfrac{g_{eff}}{\ell}\varphi=0$:

$$\omega=\sqrt{\frac{g_{eff}}{\ell}}=\frac{\sqrt[4]{g^2+a_0^2}}{\sqrt\ell},\qquad \tau=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{\ell}{g_{eff}}}$$

The pendulum aligns with the apparent gravity $\mathbf g-\mathbf a_A$ (the specific force) and oscillates faster than at rest.

Key results
  • $\theta_e=-\arctan(a_0/g)$ (the bob lags behind), $g_{eff}=\sqrt{g^2+a_0^2}$.
  • $\omega^2=g_{eff}/\ell$, stable equilibrium.

Part (f) · 1.5 points

First integral and the swing from $\theta=0$

Write a first integral of the motion in $S_1$. For $a_0=g/\sqrt3$, with the initial conditions of the statement, find the extreme angles of the swing and $\ell\dot\theta^2$ when the bob passes $\theta_e$.

Step 1. Energy in $S_1$

In $S_1$ the work is done by $\mathbf W$ and $\mathbf F_{in}$ (conservative, part (c)); $\mathbf T$ does none. Hence $E=T_{kin}+V_g+V_{in}$ is constant:

$$\frac{E}{m}=\tfrac12\ell^2\dot\theta^2-g\ell\cos\theta+a_0\ell\sin\theta$$

Check: $\dfrac{d}{dt}\dfrac Em=\ell\dot\theta\big(\ell\ddot\theta+g\sin\theta+a_0\cos\theta\big)=0$ by part (d). With $-g\cos\theta+a_0\sin\theta=-g_{eff}\cos(\theta+\alpha)$:

$$\frac{E}{m}=\tfrac12\ell^2\dot\theta^2-g_{eff}\,\ell\cos\varphi$$

Step 2. Numbers

For $a_0=g/\sqrt3$: $\tan\alpha=1/\sqrt3$, so $\alpha=30^\circ$, $\theta_e=-30^\circ$ and $g_{eff}=g\sqrt{1+1/3}=2g/\sqrt3$. At $t=0$: $\theta=0\Rightarrow\varphi_0=30^\circ$, $\dot\theta=0$.

Step 3. Extremes and speed

Equating $E$ at $\varphi_0$ and at a generic $\varphi$:

$$\ell^2\dot\theta^2=2g_{eff}\,\ell\,\big(\cos\varphi-\cos30^\circ\big)$$

It vanishes at $\varphi=\pm30^\circ$, i.e. $\theta=0$ and $\theta=-60^\circ$. At $\varphi=0$ the factor is $\cos0-\cos30^\circ=1-\cos30^\circ$:

$$\ell\dot\theta^2\Big|_{\theta_e}=2g_{eff}\,(1-\cos30^\circ)=\frac{4g}{\sqrt3}\Big(1-\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\Big)=\Big(\frac4{\sqrt3}-2\Big)g\approx0.309\,g$$
Key results
  • $\tfrac12\ell^2\dot\theta^2-g\ell\cos\theta+a_0\ell\sin\theta=$ const, i.e. $\tfrac12\ell^2\dot\theta^2-g_{eff}\ell\cos\varphi=$ const.
  • $\theta_e=-30^\circ$, extremes $\theta=0$ and $\theta=-60^\circ$; $\ell\dot\theta^2(\theta_e)=2g_{eff}(1-\cos30^\circ)=(4/\sqrt3-2)\,g\approx0.309\,g$.

Part (g) · 1.5 points

Tension and a change of regime

For $a_0=g/\sqrt3$: (i) compute $T$ at $\theta_e$ and show that the string never goes slack. (ii) At the instant the bob passes $\theta_e$ for the first time (moving backwards, $\dot\theta<0$) the support stops accelerating ($a_0\to0$, $A$ keeps its velocity). Find the maximum angle of the following motion and the tension when the bob passes the vertical.

Step 1. Tension along the swing

From (d), $T=m(\ell\dot\theta^2+g\cos\theta-a_0\sin\theta)$ and $g\cos\theta-a_0\sin\theta=g_{eff}\cos\varphi$. With $\ell\dot\theta^2=2g_{eff}(\cos\varphi-\cos30^\circ)$ of (f):

$$T(\varphi)=mg_{eff}\big(3\cos\varphi-2\cos30^\circ\big)$$

At $\varphi=0$: $T=m\frac{2g}{\sqrt3}(3-\sqrt3)=mg(2\sqrt3-2)\approx1.464\,mg$. $T$ decreases with $|\varphi|$; its minimum is at $\varphi=\pm30^\circ$: $T=mg_{eff}\cos30^\circ=mg>0$. The string is taut during the whole swing.

Step 2. Initial conditions of phase 2

At the switch: $\theta_1=-30^\circ$, $\ell\dot\theta_1^2=(4/\sqrt3-2)g$. Afterwards $\ell\ddot\theta=-g\sin\theta$ (ordinary pendulum), with the energy per unit mass $\tfrac12\ell^2\dot\theta^2-g\ell\cos\theta=$ const. The bob has kinetic energy at $\theta_1$, which is not the equilibrium of the new motion.

Step 3. Maximum angle

Setting $\dot\theta=0$ at $\theta_m$:

$$-g\ell\cos\theta_m=\tfrac12\ell^2\dot\theta_1^2-g\ell\cos30^\circ\ \Longrightarrow\ \cos\theta_m=\frac{\sqrt3}{2}-\frac12\Big(\frac4{\sqrt3}-2\Big)=1-\frac{1}{2\sqrt3}=1-\frac{\sqrt3}{6}$$

so $\theta_m=\arccos(0.7113)=44.66^\circ$: the bob, which was at $-30^\circ$ moving backwards, first reaches $-44.66^\circ$ (beyond the old equilibrium), then swings through the vertical to $+44.66^\circ$: the new motion is symmetric, $\theta\in[-44.66^\circ,+44.66^\circ]$, about the vertical.

Step 4. Tension at the vertical

The energy $\tfrac12\ell^2\dot\theta^2-g\ell\cos\theta$ is the same at $\theta_1=-30^\circ$ and at $\theta=0$, so $\ell\dot\theta^2(0)=\ell\dot\theta_1^2+2g(\cos0-\cos30^\circ)$ with $\cos0-\cos30^\circ=1-\sqrt3/2$:

$$\ell\dot\theta^2(0)=\Big(\frac4{\sqrt3}-2\Big)g+2g\Big(1-\frac{\sqrt3}2\Big)=\Big(\frac4{\sqrt3}-\sqrt3\Big)g=\frac{g}{\sqrt3}$$

and with $a_0=0$: $T(0)=m\big(\ell\dot\theta^2+g\big)=mg\big(1+\tfrac1{\sqrt3}\big)\approx1.577\,mg$.

Key results
  • $T(\varphi)=mg_{eff}(3\cos\varphi-2\cos30^\circ)$; $T(\theta_e)=mg(2\sqrt3-2)\approx1.46\,mg$; $T_{min}=mg$ at the turning points.
  • Phase 2: $\cos\theta_{max}=1-\sqrt3/6$, $\theta_{max}\approx44.66^\circ$ (the swing is $\pm44.66^\circ$ about the vertical; the first turning point is behind the vertical, at $-44.66^\circ$); $T(0)=mg(1+1/\sqrt3)\approx1.58\,mg$.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $a_0$, $g$ and $\ell\dot\theta^2$ are accelerations (m s$^{-2}$); $\omega^2=g_{eff}/\ell$ is s$^{-2}$; $T$ is $m\times$ acceleration = N.
  • Limits: $a_0\to0$ gives $\theta_e=0$, $g_{eff}=g$ and the ordinary pendulum; $a_0\gg g$ gives $\theta_e\to-90^\circ$ (the string lies horizontal, behind the support) and $\omega^2\approx a_0/\ell$.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) writing the inertia force with the wrong sign (it is $-m\mathbf a_A$, opposite to the support's acceleration); (2) forgetting that $\mathbf T$ is orthogonal to $\mathbf v_{rel}$ and adding its work; (3) taking the old equilibrium as the centre of the new motion after the support stops: the bob keeps its speed and swings past the vertical; (4) using $g$ instead of $g_{eff}$ in the period.
Problem · Unit 4 · 10 points · 60 min · Standard exam level

P06 — Precessing orbit under an attractive inverse-cube perturbation: Binet equation, circular orbits and apsidal advance

A particle $P$ of mass $m$ moves about a fixed centre $O$ (inertial frame $S_0=\{O;\mathbf i,\mathbf j,\mathbf k\}$) under the single force

$$\mathbf F=-\Big(\frac{m\mu}{r^2}+\frac{m\varepsilon}{r^3}\Big)\mathbf e_r$$

where $r=|\mathbf{OP}|$, $\mathbf e_r=\mathbf{OP}/r$, $\mu>0$ is the gravitational parameter of the centre and $\varepsilon>0$ (units m$^4$ s$^{-2}$) measures a small attractive perturbation (a model perturbation added to the Newtonian attraction). Once the motion is shown to be planar, use polar coordinates $(r,\theta)$ in the plane of the orbit, with $\theta$ measured counter-clockwise from a fixed reference line through $O$, the unit vectors $\mathbf e_r$, $\mathbf e_\theta$ ($\mathbf e_\theta$ points towards increasing $\theta$) and $h=r^2\dot\theta$.

Figure 1. Orbit of $P$ about $O$ for $ arepsilon=0.19\,h^2$, $e=0.62$: one radial cycle from the perigee $\Pi_1$ through the apogee $A$ to the next perigee $\Pi_2$ (solid) and the start of the next cycle (dashed); $r$, $ heta$, the polar unit vectors at $P$ and the apsidal advance $\Delta\phi$.

A perigee is a point of minimum $r$ and an apogee a point of maximum $r$. In the numerical part the reference line $\theta=0$ passes through a perigee.

Setup

  • Data: $m$, $\mu$, $\varepsilon$ and the initial conditions. Numerical case (part (f)): $\mu=4.0\times10^{14}$ m$^3$ s$^{-2}$; at $t=0$ the particle is at the perigee $r_p=8.0\times10^{6}$ m with the speed $v_p=1.0\times10^{4}$ m/s perpendicular to $\mathbf{OP}$; $\varepsilon=0.19\,h^2$.
  • Hypotheses: point particle, $O$ fixed and $S_0$ inertial, no other force.
  • Frames: $S_0$ (inertial, Cartesian) and the polar basis $\{\mathbf e_r,\mathbf e_\theta,\mathbf k\}$ attached to $P$, with $\mathbf e_r=\cos\theta\,\mathbf i+\sin\theta\,\mathbf j$ and $\mathbf e_\theta=-\sin\theta\,\mathbf i+\cos\theta\,\mathbf j$ ($\mathbf i$, $\mathbf j$ in the plane of the orbit, $\mathbf k$ along $\mathbf H_O$, see (a)).
  • Change of basis:
$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf e_r\ \mathbf e_\theta\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix}=[R]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf i\ \mathbf j\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix},\qquad [R]=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta&0\ -\sin\theta&\cos\theta&0\ 0&0&1\end{pmatrix}$$

with rows of unit length ($\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta=1$) and mutually orthogonal ($-\cos\theta\sin\theta+\sin\theta\cos\theta=0$), so $[R][R]^T=I$, and $\det[R]=\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta=+1$: the polar basis is right-handed, $\mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_\theta=\mathbf k$.

Part (a) · 1.5 points

Planar motion and conservation of angular momentum

Show that the angular momentum $\mathbf H_O$ of $P$ about $O$ is constant, that the motion takes place in a fixed plane through $O$, and that $h=r^2\dot\theta$ is a constant of the motion. How many degrees of freedom remain?

Step 1. Torque of a central force

$\mathbf F$ is parallel to $\mathbf r=r\,\mathbf e_r$, so

$$\mathbf M_O=\mathbf r\times\mathbf F=-\Big(\frac{m\mu}{r^2}+\frac{m\varepsilon}{r^3}\Big)\,r\,(\mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_r)=\mathbf 0$$

By the angular momentum theorem about a fixed point (Notes §4.8), $\dot{\mathbf H}_O=\mathbf M_O=\mathbf 0$, so $\mathbf H_O=m\,\mathbf r\times\mathbf v$ is a constant vector.

Step 2. Plane of the motion

For every $t$, $\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf H_O=m\,\mathbf r\cdot(\mathbf r\times\mathbf v)=0$ (a triple product with a repeated vector). The position is always orthogonal to the fixed vector $\mathbf H_O$: $P$ moves in the fixed plane through $O$ orthogonal to $\mathbf H_O$ (if $\mathbf H_O=\mathbf 0$ the motion is radial, along a fixed line). Choose $\mathbf k$ along $\mathbf H_O$ (Notes §4.8.1).

Step 3. The constant $h$

In the plane, $\mathbf r=r\,\mathbf e_r$ and, with $\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$ (chain rule on $\mathbf e_r(\theta(t))$),

$$\mathbf v=\dot r\,\mathbf e_r+r\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta,\qquad \mathbf H_O=m\,r\,\mathbf e_r\times\big(\dot r\,\mathbf e_r+r\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta\big)=mr^2\dot\theta\,\mathbf k$$

So $h=|\mathbf H_O|/m=r^2\dot\theta$ is constant (twice the areal velocity). The particle has 3 degrees of freedom in space; the plane is fixed by the initial conditions, and in it 2 remain, $r$ and $\theta$, linked by the first integral $h$.

Key results
  • $\mathbf M_O=\mathbf r\times\mathbf F=\mathbf 0$, so $\mathbf H_O=m\,\mathbf r\times\mathbf v$ is constant.
  • $\mathbf r\cdot\mathbf H_O=0$: $P$ stays in the plane through $O$ orthogonal to $\mathbf H_O$; in it $\mathbf H_O=mr^2\dot\theta\,\mathbf k$, so $h=r^2\dot\theta=$ const.
  • 2 degrees of freedom, $(r,\theta)$, with one first integral ($h$).

Part (b) · 1.5 points

Equations of motion and energy
