Write the equations of motion in polar components. Show that $\mathbf F$ is conservative, find its potential energy, and write the energy integral as a one-dimensional problem in $r$ with an effective potential $U_{eff}(r)$.

Step 1. Acceleration in polar components

Differentiating $\mathbf v=\dot r\,\mathbf e_r+r\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$ with the product rule and $\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$, $\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta=-\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_r$:

$$\mathbf a=\ddot r\,\mathbf e_r+\dot r\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta+(\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta)\,\mathbf e_\theta-r\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_r=(\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2)\,\mathbf e_r+(2\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta)\,\mathbf e_\theta$$

and $2\dot r\dot\theta+r\ddot\theta=\frac1r\frac{d}{dt}(r^2\dot\theta)$ (product rule: $\frac{d}{dt}(r^2\dot\theta)=2r\dot r\dot\theta+r^2\ddot\theta$).

Step 2. Newton's second law

$m\mathbf a=\mathbf F$ projected on $\mathbf e_r$ and $\mathbf e_\theta$ (Notes §8.3):

$$\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2=-\frac{\mu}{r^2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{r^3},\qquad \frac1r\frac{d}{dt}(r^2\dot\theta)=0$$

The second equation is the conservation of $h$ of part (a).

Step 3. Potential energy

$\mathbf F=f(r)\,\mathbf e_r$ with $f(r)=-m\mu/r^2-m\varepsilon/r^3$ depends only on $r$, so $\mathbf F=-\nabla V$ with $V'(r)=-f(r)$ (Notes §4.4). Choosing $V\to0$ as $r\to\infty$, $V(r)=-\displaystyle\int_\infty^rf(r')\,dr'$. An antiderivative of $-f(r')=\dfrac{m\mu}{r'^2}+\dfrac{m\varepsilon}{r'^3}$ is $G(r')=-\dfrac{m\mu}{r'}-\dfrac{m\varepsilon}{2r'^2}$, and Barrow's rule with $G(r')\to0$ as $r'\to\infty$ gives

$$V(r)=\int_\infty^r\Big(\frac{m\mu}{r'^2}+\frac{m\varepsilon}{r'^3}\Big)dr'=\Big[-\frac{m\mu}{r'}-\frac{m\varepsilon}{2r'^2}\Big]_\infty^r=\Big(-\frac{m\mu}{r}-\frac{m\varepsilon}{2r^2}\Big)-0=-\frac{m\mu}{r}-\frac{m\varepsilon}{2r^2}$$

Check: $-V'(r)=-\frac{m\mu}{r^2}-\frac{m\varepsilon}{r^3}=f(r)$.

Step 4. Energy integral and effective potential

The only force is conservative, so $E=\tfrac12m|\mathbf v|^2+V$ is constant (Notes §4.5), with $|\mathbf v|^2=\dot r^2+r^2\dot\theta^2=\dot r^2+h^2/r^2$:

$$\frac Em=\tfrac12\dot r^2+\frac{h^2}{2r^2}-\frac{\mu}{r}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2r^2}=\tfrac12\dot r^2+U_{eff}(r),\qquad U_{eff}(r)=\frac{h^2-\varepsilon}{2r^2}-\frac{\mu}{r}$$

The perturbation enters exactly as a reduction of the centrifugal barrier, $h^2\to h^2-\varepsilon$.

Key results
  • $\ddot r-r\dot\theta^2=-\dfrac{\mu}{r^2}-\dfrac{\varepsilon}{r^3}$ and $\dfrac{d}{dt}(r^2\dot\theta)=0$.
  • $V=-\dfrac{m\mu}{r}-\dfrac{m\varepsilon}{2r^2}$.
  • $\dfrac Em=\tfrac12\dot r^2+U_{eff}(r)$, $\ U_{eff}=\dfrac{h^2-\varepsilon}{2r^2}-\dfrac{\mu}{r}$.

Part (c) · 1.5 points

Circular orbit and its stability

For a given $h$, find the radius $r_c$ and the speed of the circular orbit. Under what condition does it exist? Is it stable against small radial perturbations? Compare the frequency of the small radial oscillations with the angular rate on the circle.

Step 1. Equilibrium of the radial problem

$$U_{eff}'(r)=-\frac{h^2-\varepsilon}{r^3}+\frac{\mu}{r^2}=0\ \Longrightarrow\ r_c=\frac{h^2-\varepsilon}{\mu}$$

A positive radius requires $h^2>\varepsilon$. If $h^2\le\varepsilon$ the barrier term is non-positive, $U_{eff}$ decreases monotonically towards $r\to0$, and the particle with $\dot r<0$ falls into the centre: no circular orbit exists.

Step 2. Speed

On the circle $\dot r=0$, so $v_c=r_c\dot\theta=h/r_c$. Equivalently, the radial equation with $\ddot r=0$ gives $r_c\dot\theta^2=\mu/r_c^2+\varepsilon/r_c^3$, i.e. $v_c^2=\mu/r_c+\varepsilon/r_c^2$; with $h^2=\mu r_c+\varepsilon$ both forms agree.

Step 3. Stability

$$U_{eff}''(r)=\frac{3(h^2-\varepsilon)}{r^4}-\frac{2\mu}{r^3}\ \Longrightarrow\ U_{eff}''(r_c)=\frac{3\mu r_c}{r_c^4}-\frac{2\mu}{r_c^3}=\frac{\mu}{r_c^3}>0$$

$r_c$ is a minimum of $U_{eff}$: the circular orbit is stable. Linearising $\ddot r=-U_{eff}'(r)$ about $r_c$ with $\rho=r-r_c$ gives $\ddot\rho+U_{eff}''(r_c)\rho=0$, so the radial oscillations have $\omega_r^2=\mu/r_c^3$.

Step 4. Radial frequency versus angular rate

On the circle $\dot\theta_c=h/r_c^2$, so $\dot\theta_c^2=h^2/r_c^4$ and

$$\frac{\omega_r^2}{\dot\theta_c^2}=\frac{\mu r_c}{h^2}=\frac{h^2-\varepsilon}{h^2}=1-\frac{\varepsilon}{h^2}$$

For $\varepsilon=0$ (Kepler) the ratio is 1: $r$ returns to its minimum exactly once per turn and the slightly perturbed orbit closes (an ellipse). For $\varepsilon>0$ the radial oscillation is slower than the rotation, so the minimum of $r$ is reached after more than one turn: the perigee advances. Part (e) makes this exact for any amplitude.

Key results
  • $r_c=\dfrac{h^2-\varepsilon}{\mu}$, it exists only if $h^2>\varepsilon$; $v_c^2=\dfrac{h^2}{r_c^2}=\dfrac{\mu}{r_c}+\dfrac{\varepsilon}{r_c^2}$.
  • $U_{eff}''(r_c)=\mu/r_c^3>0$: stable. $\omega_r^2=\mu/r_c^3$, $\ \omega_r/\dot\theta_c=\sqrt{1-\varepsilon/h^2}<1$.

Part (d) · 2 points

Binet equation and the orbit

With $u=1/r$ and $\theta$ as the independent variable, derive the Binet equation of this force field, solve it with the perigee at $\theta=0$, and give the perigee and apogee radii.

Step 1. First derivative

$r=1/u$ with $u=u(\theta(t))$. Chain rule:

$$\dot r=\frac{d}{dt}\Big(\frac1u\Big)=-\frac{1}{u^2}\frac{du}{d\theta}\dot\theta=-\frac{1}{u^2}u'\,hu^2=-h\,u'$$

using $\dot\theta=h/r^2=hu^2$.

Step 2. Second derivative

$h$ is constant, so again by the chain rule

$$\ddot r=\frac{d}{dt}(-h\,u')=-h\frac{du'}{d\theta}\dot\theta=-h\,u''\,hu^2=-h^2u^2u''$$

Step 3. Radial equation in $u$

Substitute $\ddot r$, $r\dot\theta^2=\frac1u\,h^2u^4=h^2u^3$, $\mu/r^2=\mu u^2$ and $\varepsilon/r^3=\varepsilon u^3$ in the radial equation of (b):

$$-h^2u^2u''-h^2u^3=-\mu u^2-\varepsilon u^3$$

Dividing by $-h^2u^2$ (Notes §8.3, Eq. 8.12 with the extra term):

$$u''+\Big(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{h^2}\Big)u=\frac{\mu}{h^2}$$

Step 4. Solution

With the scalar $k^2=1-\varepsilon/h^2>0$ (not the unit vector $\mathbf k$; the condition $h^2>\varepsilon$ of (c)), this is a forced harmonic oscillator in $\theta$ with "frequency" $k$. A particular solution is the constant $u_0=\mu/(h^2k^2)$; the homogeneous solution is $C\cos(k\theta-\theta_0)$. Choosing $\theta=0$ at a perigee (maximum of $u$) gives $\theta_0=0$ and $C\ge0$; write $C=u_0e$:

$$u=\frac{\mu}{h^2k^2}\big(1+e\cos k\theta\big),\qquad r=\frac{r_c}{1+e\cos k\theta},\qquad r_c=\frac{h^2k^2}{\mu}=\frac{h^2-\varepsilon}{\mu}$$

Check: $u''=-k^2u_0e\cos k\theta$ and $u''+k^2u=k^2u_0=\mu/h^2$.

Step 5. Apsides

$r$ is minimum where $\cos k\theta=1$ ($\theta=0$) and maximum where $\cos k\theta=-1$ ($k\theta=\pi$):

$$r_p=\frac{r_c}{1+e},\qquad r_a=\frac{r_c}{1-e}\quad(0\le e<1)$$

The shape looks like a conic with semi-parameter $r_c$, but the angle that appears is $k\theta$, not $\theta$: the apogee is reached at $\theta=\pi/k>\pi$.

Key results
  • $u''+\Big(1-\dfrac{\varepsilon}{h^2}\Big)u=\dfrac{\mu}{h^2}$.
  • $u=\dfrac{\mu}{h^2k^2}\big(1+e\cos k\theta\big)$, i.e. $r=\dfrac{r_c}{1+e\cos k\theta}$ with $r_c=\dfrac{h^2-\varepsilon}{\mu}=\dfrac{h^2k^2}{\mu}$.
  • $r_p=\dfrac{r_c}{1+e}$ at $\theta=0$; $r_a=\dfrac{r_c}{1-e}$ at $k\theta=\pi$ (bounded orbit if $0\le e<1$).

Part (e) · 1.5 points

Apsidal precession

Find the angle swept between two consecutive perigees and the advance $\Delta\phi$ of the perigee per radial cycle; give its first-order form for $\varepsilon\ll h^2$. Is the orbit closed? Is the time between two perigees the time the particle needs to turn $360^\circ$?

Step 1. Angle between perigees

Perigees are at $\cos k\theta=1$, i.e. $k\theta=0,2\pi,4\pi,\dots$ Two consecutive ones are separated by $\theta=2\pi/k$, and since $k<1$ this is more than one turn. The advance per radial cycle is

$$\Delta\phi=\frac{2\pi}{k}-2\pi=2\pi\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\varepsilon/h^2}}-1\Big)$$

positive: the perigee advances in the sense of the motion.

Step 2. Small perturbation

With $x=\varepsilon/h^2\ll1$ and the binomial series $(1-x)^{-1/2}=1+\tfrac12x+O(x^2)$:

$$\Delta\phi=2\pi\Big(\tfrac12x+O(x^2)\Big)\approx\frac{\pi\varepsilon}{h^2}$$

Step 3. Closed or not

The orbit closes if after $q$ radial cycles the particle has made an integer number $p$ of turns: $q\,(2\pi/k)=2\pi p$, i.e. $k=q/p$ rational. Otherwise the perigee directions never repeat and the trajectory densely fills the annulus between $r_p$ and $r_a$.

Step 4. Time between perigees (the trap)

Substituting $\dot\theta=h/r^2$ in the radial equation of (b):

$$\ddot r=\frac{h^2}{r^3}-\frac{\mu}{r^2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{r^3}=\frac{\tilde h^2}{r^3}-\frac{\mu}{r^2},\qquad \tilde h^2=h^2-\varepsilon$$

This is the radial equation of a Kepler orbit with angular momentum $\tilde h$ and the same $r_p$, $r_a$, so $r(t)$ is periodic with the Kepler period of semi-major axis $a=(r_p+r_a)/2$ (Notes §8.4, Eq. 8.18):

$$T_r=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{a^3}{\mu}}$$

During $T_r$ the angle advances by $2\pi/k$, not $2\pi$: $\dot\theta=h/r^2=(h/\tilde h)\,(\tilde h/r^2)$ and $h/\tilde h=1/k$. The time to turn $360^\circ$ and the time between perigees are different quantities; equating them is the classic error. The energy also matches: at $r_p$, $\frac Em=U_{eff}(r_p)=-\frac{\mu}{2a}$.

Key results
  • Angle between perigees $2\pi/k$; $\ \Delta\phi=2\pi\Big(\dfrac{1}{\sqrt{1-\varepsilon/h^2}}-1\Big)\approx\dfrac{\pi\varepsilon}{h^2}$.
  • Closed only if $k$ is rational; for irrational $k$ the orbit fills the annulus $r_p\le r\le r_a$ (a rosette).
  • Time between perigees: the radial period $T_r=2\pi\sqrt{a^3/\mu}$, $2a=r_p+r_a$; in that time $\theta$ advances $2\pi/k$, not $2\pi$.

Part (f) · 2 points

Numerical case

With the data of the Setup ($\mu=4.0\times10^{14}$ m$^3$ s$^{-2}$, $r_p=8.0\times10^6$ m, $v_p=1.0\times10^4$ m/s perpendicular to $\mathbf{OP}$, $\varepsilon=0.19\,h^2$), compute $h$, $\varepsilon$, $k$, $r_c$, $e$, $r_a$, $E/m$, the apsidal advance (exact and first-order), and the time between two perigees. Comment on the value of $v_p$.

Step 1. Constants of the motion

$h=r_pv_p=8.0\times10^6\times1.0\times10^4=8.0\times10^{10}$ m$^2$/s, $h^2=6.4\times10^{21}$ m$^4$ s$^{-2}$, $\varepsilon=0.19\,h^2=1.216\times10^{21}$ m$^4$ s$^{-2}$ and $k=\sqrt{1-0.19}=\sqrt{0.81}=0.9$.

Step 2. Orbit

$$r_c=\frac{h^2-\varepsilon}{\mu}=\frac{0.81\times6.4\times10^{21}}{4.0\times10^{14}}=1.296\times10^{7}\ \text{m}$$

At $\theta=0$, $r=r_p$: $r_p=r_c/(1+e)\Rightarrow e=r_c/r_p-1=1.62-1=0.62$. Then $r_a=r_c/(1-e)=1.296\times10^7/0.38=3.411\times10^{7}$ m and $a=(r_p+r_a)/2=2.105\times10^{7}$ m.

Step 3. Energy

$$\frac Em=\frac{v_p^2}{2}-\frac{\mu}{r_p}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2r_p^2}=5.0\times10^{7}-5.0\times10^{7}-\frac{1.216\times10^{21}}{1.28\times10^{14}}=-9.50\times10^{6}\ \text{J/kg}$$

in agreement with $-\mu/(2a)=-4.0\times10^{14}/(4.211\times10^{7})=-9.50\times10^{6}$ J/kg. Note that $\tfrac12v_p^2=\mu/r_p$: $v_p$ is exactly the escape speed of the unperturbed problem. Without the perturbation the orbit would be a parabola; the extra attraction makes it bound.

Step 4. Precession and times

$$\Delta\phi=2\pi\Big(\frac{1}{0.9}-1\Big)=\frac{2\pi}{9}=40^\circ,\qquad \frac{\pi\varepsilon}{h^2}=0.19\,\pi=0.597\ \text{rad}=34.2^\circ$$

The first-order formula underestimates by 15 %: $\varepsilon/h^2=0.19$ is not small. The apogee is at $\theta=\pi/k=200^\circ$ and the next perigee at $\theta=400^\circ$. Since $k=9/10$, the orbit closes after 9 radial cycles and 10 turns.

$$T_r=2\pi\sqrt{\frac{(2.105\times10^{7})^3}{4.0\times10^{14}}}=3.035\times10^{4}\ \text{s}\approx8.43\ \text{h}$$

A numerical integration of the equations of (b) from the perigee confirms that $r$ is minimum again after $3.035\times10^4$ s with $\theta=400.0^\circ$.

Key results
  • $h=8.0\times10^{10}$ m$^2$/s, $\varepsilon=1.216\times10^{21}$ m$^4$ s$^{-2}$, $k=0.9$.
  • $r_c=1.296\times10^{7}$ m, $e=0.62$, $r_a=3.411\times10^{7}$ m, $a=2.105\times10^7$ m, $E/m=-9.50\times10^{6}$ J/kg.
  • $\Delta\phi=40^\circ$ exactly ($2\pi/9$); first-order estimate $34.2^\circ$; apogee at $\theta=200^\circ$; $T_r=3.035\times10^4$ s.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $\mu/r^2$ and $\varepsilon/r^3$ are accelerations (m$^3$ s$^{-2}$/m$^2$ and m$^4$ s$^{-2}$/m$^3$); $\varepsilon/h^2$ is dimensionless (m$^4$ s$^{-2}$ over m$^4$ s$^{-2}$), so $k$ and $\Delta\phi$ are pure numbers; $r_c=(h^2-\varepsilon)/\mu$ has m$^4$ s$^{-2}$/(m$^3$ s$^{-2}$) = m; $\sqrt{a^3/\mu}$ has s.
  • Limits: $\varepsilon\to0$ gives $k=1$, $\Delta\phi=0$, the conic $r=(h^2/\mu)/(1+e\cos\theta)$ of Notes Eq. 8.14 and $T_r$ equal to the orbital period. $\varepsilon\to h^2$ gives $k\to0$: the barrier disappears, $\Delta\phi\to\infty$ and $r_c\to0$ (the particle spirals in).
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) dropping the $\varepsilon u^3$ term when changing to $u$, or writing it on the wrong side, which changes the sign of the precession; (2) treating the orbit as a closed ellipse with $\theta$ in place of $k\theta$ (apogee at $180^\circ$); (3) taking the time between perigees as the time for one turn, or dividing $\Delta\phi$ by the period of a Kepler orbit with $h$ instead of $\tilde h$; (4) using the first-order $\pi\varepsilon/h^2$ when $\varepsilon/h^2$ is not small.
Problem · Unit 4 · 10 points · 60 min · Standard exam level

P07 — Insertion orbit and circularisation impulse: conic from the energy, apsides, apogee burn and time of flight

A probe $P$ of mass $m$ moves in the gravitational field of a planet of centre $O$, radius $R$ and gravitational parameter $\mu$: the only force is $\mathbf F=-\dfrac{m\mu}{r^2}\mathbf e_r$, with $r=|\mathbf{OP}|$, $\mathbf e_r=\mathbf{OP}/r$; $O$ is fixed and the frame $S_0$ attached to it is inertial. At $t=0$ the probe is at the distance $r_0$ from $O$ and its velocity $\mathbf v_0$ has the magnitude

$$v_0=\sqrt{\mu/r_0}$$

(the local circular speed) and makes the flight-path angle $\gamma=30^\circ$ with the local horizontal, pointing away from the planet. Use polar coordinates $(r,\theta)$ in the orbital plane, with $\theta$ (the true anomaly) measured from the perigee $\Pi$ of the conic in the sense of the motion ($\Pi$ is a virtual point: with the data below it lies inside the planet, so the probe never reaches it; the trajectory arc starts at the surface $r=R$), the unit vectors $\mathbf e_r$, $\mathbf e_\theta$ and $h=r^2\dot\theta>0$. The local horizontal at $P$ is the direction of $\mathbf e_\theta$, so $\mathbf v_0=v_0(\sin\gamma\,\mathbf e_r+\cos\gamma\,\mathbf e_\theta)$.

Figure 1. Insertion orbit: focus $O$ (planet of radius $R$), probe $P$ at $r_0$ with velocity $\mathbf v_0$ and flight-path angle $\gamma$ above the local horizontal $\mathbf e_\theta$; true anomaly $\theta_0$ from the perigee $\Pi$; apogee $A$, impulse $\Delta\mathbf v$ and the circular target orbit $r=r_a$ (dashed).

When the probe reaches the apogee $A$, its engine fires a short tangential impulse $\Delta\mathbf v$ that puts it on the circular orbit of radius $r_a=|\mathbf{OA}|$.

Setup

  • Data: $\mu$, $r_0$, $v_0=\sqrt{\mu/r_0}$, $\gamma=30^\circ$. Numerical part (f): $\mu=4.0\times10^{14}$ m$^3$ s$^{-2}$, $r_0=1.0\times10^{7}$ m, $R=6.0\times10^{6}$ m.
  • Hypotheses: point particle; planet's field spherically symmetric; no drag; the impulse is instantaneous (the position does not change during the burn) and the mass of propellant is irrelevant for the kinematics.
  • Frames: $S_0=\{O;\mathbf i,\mathbf j,\mathbf k\}$ with $\mathbf i$ towards $\Pi$, $\mathbf j$ in the orbital plane and $\mathbf k$ along $\mathbf H_O$; polar basis $\mathbf e_r=\cos\theta\,\mathbf i+\sin\theta\,\mathbf j$, $\mathbf e_\theta=-\sin\theta\,\mathbf i+\cos\theta\,\mathbf j$. Change of basis:
$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf e_r\ \mathbf e_\theta\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix}=[R]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf i\ \mathbf j\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix},\qquad [R]=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&\sin\theta&0\ -\sin\theta&\cos\theta&0\ 0&0&1\end{pmatrix}$$

with $[R][R]^T=I$ (rows of length $\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta=1$, rows 1 and 2 orthogonal: $-\cos\theta\sin\theta+\sin\theta\cos\theta=0$) and $\det[R]=\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta=+1$, so $\mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_\theta=\mathbf k$.

  • Results from Notes §8.4 used: orbit $r=p/(1+e\cos\theta)$ with $p=h^2/\mu$ (Eq. 8.14), $p=a(1-e^2)$ (Eq. 8.16), $r_p=a(1-e)$, $r_a=a(1+e)$, period $\tau=2\pi\sqrt{a^3/\mu}$ (Eq. 8.18); vis-viva $v^2=\mu(2/r-1/a)$ (Notes §8.5).

Part (a) · 1.5 points

Conserved quantities and type of conic

Explain why $h$ and the energy per unit mass $E/m$ are constant. Compute them from the initial conditions and decide the type of conic and its semi-major axis $a$.

Step 1. Why they are conserved

$\mathbf F$ is central, so its torque about $O$ is $\mathbf r\times\mathbf F=\mathbf 0$ and $\mathbf H_O$ is constant (Notes §4.8); the motion is planar and $h=r^2\dot\theta$ is constant (Notes §4.8.1). $\mathbf F=-\nabla V$ with $V=-m\mu/r$, and it is the only force, so the mechanical energy $E=\tfrac12mv^2-m\mu/r$ is constant (Notes §4.5).

Step 2. Angular momentum

With $\mathbf r=r_0\,\mathbf e_r$ and $\mathbf v_0=v_0(\sin\gamma\,\mathbf e_r+\cos\gamma\,\mathbf e_\theta)$:

$$\mathbf r\times\mathbf v_0=r_0v_0\cos\gamma\,(\mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_\theta)=r_0v_0\cos\gamma\,\mathbf k\ \Longrightarrow\ h=r_0\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{r_0}}\,\frac{\sqrt3}{2}=\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\sqrt{\mu r_0}$$

Step 3. Energy and conic

$$\frac Em=\frac12\,\frac{\mu}{r_0}-\frac{\mu}{r_0}=-\frac{\mu}{2r_0}<0$$

A negative energy means a bounded orbit: an ellipse. The vis-viva equation (Notes §8.5) gives $E/m=-\mu/(2a)$, so $a=r_0$. Note the trap: the probe has exactly the circular speed of its radius, yet the orbit is not a circle, because the velocity is not horizontal; the speed fixes $a$ only, the direction fixes the shape.

Key results
  • $h=r_0v_0\cos\gamma=\tfrac{\sqrt3}{2}\sqrt{\mu r_0}$, $\ h^2=\tfrac34\mu r_0$.
  • $E/m=\tfrac12v_0^2-\mu/r_0=-\dfrac{\mu}{2r_0}<0$: ellipse, $a=-\dfrac{\mu}{2E/m}=r_0$.

Part (b) · 2 points

Shape of the orbit and apsides

Find the semi-latus rectum $p$, the eccentricity $e$ and the perigee and apogee radii $r_p$, $r_a$. Verify the apsides independently from the energy equation.

Step 1. Semi-latus rectum

$$p=\frac{h^2}{\mu}=\frac{\tfrac34\mu r_0}{\mu}=\frac34r_0$$

Step 2. Eccentricity

From $p=a(1-e^2)$ (Notes Eq. 8.16): $e^2=1-p/a=1-\tfrac34=\tfrac14$, so $e=\tfrac12$. Substituting $a=-\mu/(2E/m)$ and $p=h^2/\mu$ gives the general formula $e^2=1+2(E/m)h^2/\mu^2=1+2\big(-\tfrac{\mu}{2r_0}\big)\tfrac{3\mu r_0}{4\mu^2}=1-\tfrac34$, the same value.

Step 3. Apsides

$$r_p=a(1-e)=\frac{r_0}{2},\qquad r_a=a(1+e)=\frac{3r_0}{2}$$

and indeed $r_p+r_a=2a$.

Step 4. Independent check

With $v^2=\dot r^2+h^2/r^2$, the energy at an apsis ($\dot r=0$) reads $\dfrac{h^2}{2r^2}-\dfrac{\mu}{r}=\dfrac Em$. Multiplying by $2r^2/\mu$ with $h^2=\tfrac34\mu r_0$ and $E/m=-\mu/(2r_0)$:

$$\frac34r_0-2r=-\frac{r^2}{r_0}\ \Longrightarrow\ r^2-2r_0r+\frac34r_0^2=0\ \Longrightarrow\ r=r_0\pm\sqrt{r_0^2-\tfrac34r_0^2}=r_0\pm\frac{r_0}{2}$$

the same $r_p=r_0/2$ and $r_a=3r_0/2$.

Key results
  • $p=\tfrac34r_0$, $\ e=\sqrt{1-p/a}=\tfrac12$ (equivalently $e^2=1+2(E/m)h^2/\mu^2$).
  • $r_p=a(1-e)=\tfrac12r_0$, $\ r_a=a(1+e)=\tfrac32r_0$.

Part (c) · 1.5 points

Position on the orbit, apsidal speeds and period

Find the true anomaly $\theta_0$ of the initial point (justify the sign), the speeds $v_p$, $v_a$ at the apsides of the conic ($v_p$ is the speed the conic would have at its virtual perigee) and the period $\tau$ of the full ellipse.

Step 1. True anomaly

$$r_0=\frac{p}{1+e\cos\theta_0}\ \Longrightarrow\ 1+\tfrac12\cos\theta_0=\frac{p}{r_0}=\frac34\ \Longrightarrow\ \cos\theta_0=-\frac12$$

so $\theta_0=\pm120^\circ$.

Step 2. Sign from the radial velocity

Differentiating $r=p\,(1+e\cos\theta)^{-1}$ with the chain rule and $\dot\theta=h/r^2$:

$$\dot r=\frac{p\,e\sin\theta\,\dot\theta}{(1+e\cos\theta)^2}=\frac{r^2}{p}\,e\sin\theta\,\frac{h}{r^2}=\frac{h}{p}e\sin\theta=\frac{\mu}{h}e\sin\theta$$

The velocity points away from the planet, $\dot r=v_0\sin\gamma>0$, so $\sin\theta_0>0$ and $\theta_0=+120^\circ$. Check: $\frac{\mu}{h}\,\frac12\,\frac{\sqrt3}2=\frac{\mu}{\sqrt3\sqrt{\mu r_0}}\frac{\sqrt3}{2}=\frac12\sqrt{\mu/r_0}=v_0\sin30^\circ$.

Step 3. Apsidal speeds

At the apsides the velocity is perpendicular to $\mathbf r$ ($\dot r=0$), so $v=h/r$:

$$v_p=\frac{h}{r_p}=\frac{\tfrac{\sqrt3}2\sqrt{\mu r_0}}{r_0/2}=\sqrt3\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{r_0}},\qquad v_a=\frac{h}{r_a}=\frac{\tfrac{\sqrt3}2\sqrt{\mu r_0}}{3r_0/2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt3}\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{r_0}}$$

Vis-viva check at the apogee: $\mu\big(\tfrac{2}{r_a}-\tfrac1a\big)=\tfrac{\mu}{r_0}\big(\tfrac43-1\big)=\tfrac{\mu}{3r_0}=v_a^2$.

Step 4. Period

Kepler's third law (Notes Eq. 8.18) with $a=r_0$: $\ \tau=2\pi\sqrt{a^3/\mu}=2\pi\sqrt{r_0^3/\mu}$.

Key results
  • $\cos\theta_0=-\tfrac12$ and $\dot r>0$, so $\theta_0=+120^\circ$ (outbound, between perigee and apogee).
  • $v_p=h/r_p=\sqrt3\sqrt{\mu/r_0}$, $\ v_a=h/r_a=\sqrt{\mu/r_0}/\sqrt3$; $\ \tau=2\pi\sqrt{r_0^3/\mu}$.

Part (d) · 2 points

Circularisation impulse at the apogee

Compute the impulse $\Delta\mathbf v$ (magnitude and direction) that makes the orbit circular at $r_a$, the change of energy per unit mass and the ratio of the new period to $\tau$. Why is the apogee the right place for a purely tangential burn?

Step 1. Why the apogee

A circular orbit has $\dot r=0$ everywhere, so its velocity is perpendicular to $\mathbf r$. At the apogee $\dot r=0$ already: the velocity $v_a\,\mathbf e_\theta$ is horizontal and only its magnitude has to change. At any other point the burn would also have to cancel $\dot r$, so it could not be purely tangential.

Step 2. Impulse

The circular speed at $r_a$ follows from $\mu/r_a^2=v^2/r_a$ (or from vis-viva with $a=r_a$):

$$v_{circ}=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{r_a}}=\sqrt{\frac{2\mu}{3r_0}},\qquad \Delta\mathbf v=(v_{circ}-v_a)\,\mathbf e_\theta=\Big(\sqrt{\tfrac23}-\tfrac{1}{\sqrt3}\Big)\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{r_0}}\,\mathbf e_\theta=\frac{\sqrt2-1}{\sqrt3}\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{r_0}}\,\mathbf e_\theta$$

positive: the engine pushes forward, in the direction of motion.

Step 3. Energy

The position does not change during the burn, so only the kinetic energy changes:

$$\frac{\Delta E}{m}=\frac12\big(v_{circ}^2-v_a^2\big)=\frac12\Big(\frac23-\frac13\Big)\frac{\mu}{r_0}=\frac{\mu}{6r_0}$$

Check with $E/m=-\mu/(2a)$: $-\dfrac{\mu}{2r_a}+\dfrac{\mu}{2a}=-\dfrac{\mu}{3r_0}+\dfrac{\mu}{2r_0}=\dfrac{\mu}{6r_0}$.

Step 4. New period

$\tau_{circ}=2\pi\sqrt{r_a^3/\mu}$, so $\tau_{circ}/\tau=(r_a/a)^{3/2}=(3/2)^{3/2}\approx1.837$.

Key results
  • $v_{circ}=\sqrt{\mu/r_a}=\sqrt{2/3}\,\sqrt{\mu/r_0}$; $\ \Delta v=v_{circ}-v_a=\dfrac{\sqrt2-1}{\sqrt3}\sqrt{\dfrac{\mu}{r_0}}\approx0.239\sqrt{\mu/r_0}$, along $+\mathbf e_\theta$ (prograde).
  • $\Delta E/m=\tfrac12(v_{circ}^2-v_a^2)=\dfrac{\mu}{6r_0}>0$; $\ \tau_{circ}/\tau=(3/2)^{3/2}\approx1.837$.

Part (e) · 1.5 points

Time of flight from $r_0$ to the apogee

Compute the time the probe needs to go from the initial point to the apogee. Show that it is not $\tau\cdot(60^\circ/360^\circ)$. (Extension: use the eccentric anomaly $\mathcal E$, defined by $r=a(1-e\cos\mathcal E)$.)

Step 1. Eccentric anomaly (extension)

Define $\mathcal E$ by $r=a(1-e\cos\mathcal E)$; it runs from $0$ at the perigee to $\pi$ at the apogee. Equating with $r=p/(1+e\cos\theta)$ and $p=a(1-e^2)$ and solving for $\cos\mathcal E$:

$$\cos\mathcal E=\frac1e\Big(1-\frac{1-e^2}{1+e\cos\theta}\Big)=\frac{e+\cos\theta}{1+e\cos\theta}$$

At $\theta_0=120^\circ$: $\cos\mathcal E_0=(\tfrac12-\tfrac12)/(1-\tfrac14)=0$, so $\mathcal E_0=\pi/2$ (outbound branch); check $r=a(1-0)=r_0$. At the apogee $\mathcal E=\pi$.

Step 2. Kepler's equation

The energy equation $\dot r^2+h^2/r^2=\mu(2/r-1/a)$ with $h^2=\mu a(1-e^2)$ gives

$$\dot r^2=\frac{\mu}{a\,r^2}\big[2ar-r^2-a^2(1-e^2)\big]$$

Substituting $r=a(1-e\cos\mathcal E)$: $2ar-r^2-a^2(1-e^2)=a^2\big[2-2e\cos\mathcal E-1+2e\cos\mathcal E-e^2\cos^2\mathcal E-1+e^2\big]=a^2e^2\sin^2\mathcal E$. Hence $\dot r=\pm\sqrt{\mu/a}\;a\,e\sin\mathcal E/r$. With $\mathcal E$ increasing along the motion, $\sin\mathcal E>0$ on the outbound branch ($0<\mathcal E<\pi$, $\dot r>0$) and $\sin\mathcal E<0$ on the inbound branch ($\pi<\mathcal E<2\pi$, $\dot r<0$), so the sign is $+$ on both branches: $\dot r=\sqrt{\mu/a}\;a\,e\sin\mathcal E/r$ for every $\mathcal E$. On the other hand, by the chain rule $\dot r=a\,e\sin\mathcal E\,\dot{\mathcal E}$, also valid on both branches. Equating (and, at the apses where $\sin\mathcal E=0$, by continuity):

$$\dot{\mathcal E}=\frac{\sqrt{\mu/a}}{r}=\frac{n}{1-e\cos\mathcal E}\quad\text{for every }\mathcal E\ \Longrightarrow\ (1-e\cos\mathcal E)\,d\mathcal E=n\,dt,\qquad n=\sqrt{\frac{\mu}{a^3}}$$

Step 3. Integration with Barrow's rule

The antiderivative of $1-e\cos\mathcal E$ is $\mathcal E-e\sin\mathcal E$, so

$$n\,t_{0\to A}=\big[\mathcal E-e\sin\mathcal E\big]_{\pi/2}^{\pi}=(\pi-0)-\Big(\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac12\Big)=\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac12$$

and with $1/n=\sqrt{r_0^3/\mu}$ (because $a=r_0$): $\ t_{0\to A}=\big(\tfrac{\pi}{2}+\tfrac12\big)\sqrt{r_0^3/\mu}\approx2.071\sqrt{r_0^3/\mu}$. As a fraction of the period, $(\pi/2+1/2)/(2\pi)\approx0.330$.

Step 4. The trap

The angle swept is $60^\circ$, one sixth of a turn, but the time is a third of the period: near the apogee the probe is slow ($\dot\theta=h/r^2$ is small), so equal angles do not take equal times. A direct numerical quadrature of $dt=r^2\,d\theta/h$ from $120^\circ$ to $180^\circ$ gives the same $\pi/2+1/2$.

Key results
  • $\cos\mathcal E=\dfrac{e+\cos\theta}{1+e\cos\theta}$: $\ \mathcal E_0=90^\circ$ at $\theta_0=120^\circ$, $\ \mathcal E=180^\circ$ at the apogee.
  • $t_{0\to A}=\dfrac{1}{n}\big[\mathcal E-e\sin\mathcal E\big]_{\pi/2}^{\pi}=\Big(\dfrac{\pi}{2}+\dfrac12\Big)\sqrt{\dfrac{r_0^3}{\mu}}\approx0.330\,\tau$, twice the naive $\tau/6\approx0.167\,\tau$.

Part (f) · 1.5 points

Numerical values and a failed burn

With $\mu=4.0\times10^{14}$ m$^3$ s$^{-2}$, $r_0=1.0\times10^7$ m: compute $v_0$, $h$, $v_p$, $v_a$, $\tau$, $\Delta v$, $\Delta E/m$ and $t_{0\to A}$. If the engine had failed at the apogee, would the probe have survived, given the planet radius $R=6.0\times10^6$ m? If not, at which true anomaly and how long after the apogee would it hit the surface?

Step 1. Numbers

$\sqrt{\mu/r_0}=\sqrt{4.0\times10^7}=6325$ m/s $=v_0$ and $\sqrt{r_0^3/\mu}=\sqrt{2.5\times10^6}=1581$ s. Then $h=\tfrac{\sqrt3}2r_0v_0=5.477\times10^{10}$ m$^2$/s, $v_p=\sqrt3\times6325=1.095\times10^4$ m/s, $v_a=6325/\sqrt3=3651$ m/s, $\tau=2\pi\times1581=9935$ s, $v_{circ}=5164$ m/s, $\Delta v=5164-3651=1512$ m/s, $\Delta E/m=\mu/(6r_0)=6.67\times10^6$ J/kg, $\tau_{circ}=1.825\times10^4$ s and $t_{0\to A}=2.071\times1581=3274$ s, against the naive $\tau/6=1656$ s.

Step 2. Failed burn

$r_p=r_0/2=5.0\times10^6$ m is smaller than $R=6.0\times10^6$ m: after the apogee the probe would descend into the planet. Impact: $7.5\times10^6/(1+\tfrac12\cos\theta)=6.0\times10^6\Rightarrow\cos\theta=\tfrac12$, and on the inbound branch ($\dot r<0$, $\sin\theta<0$) $\theta=300^\circ$. There $\cos\mathcal E=(\tfrac12+\tfrac12)/(1+\tfrac14)=\tfrac45$ with $\mathcal E$ in $(\pi,2\pi)$: $\mathcal E_i=2\pi-\arccos\tfrac45=5.640$ rad, $\sin\mathcal E_i=-\tfrac35$.

Step 3. Time to impact

$$n\,t_{A\to i}=\big[\mathcal E-e\sin\mathcal E\big]_{\pi}^{\mathcal E_i}=\Big(5.640+\frac12\cdot\frac35\Big)-\pi=2.798\ \Longrightarrow\ t_{A\to i}=2.798\times1581=4424\ \text{s}$$

The circularisation burn is therefore not optional: it also raises the perigee from $0.5\,r_0$ to $1.5\,r_0$.

Key results
  • $v_0=6325$ m/s, $h=5.477\times10^{10}$ m$^2$/s, $v_p=1.095\times10^4$ m/s, $v_a=3651$ m/s, $\tau=9935$ s, $\Delta v=1512$ m/s, $\Delta E/m=6.67\times10^6$ J/kg, $t_{0\to A}=3274$ s (naive: 1656 s).
  • $r_p=5.0\times10^6$ m $<R$: impact at $\theta=300^\circ$ ($\cos\theta=\tfrac12$, $\cos\mathcal E=\tfrac45$), $4424$ s after the apogee.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $h=r_0v_0$ in m$^2$/s; $h^2/\mu$ in m$^4$ s$^{-2}$/(m$^3$ s$^{-2}$) = m; $E/m$ and $\mu/r$ in m$^2$ s$^{-2}$ = J/kg; $\sqrt{a^3/\mu}$ in s; $\Delta v$ in m/s.
  • Limits: $\gamma\to0$ with $v_0=\sqrt{\mu/r_0}$ gives $h^2=\mu r_0$, $e=0$: the probe is already on the circle of radius $r_0$. $\gamma\to90^\circ$ gives $h=0$: a radial (degenerate) orbit, straight up and back down. For any $\gamma$, $a=r_0$, so the period does not depend on $\gamma$.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) concluding that the orbit is circular because $v_0=\sqrt{\mu/r_0}$; (2) using $h=r_0v_0$ without $\cos\gamma$; (3) taking $\theta_0=-120^\circ$ (the inbound point) without checking the sign of $\dot r$; (4) computing $\Delta v$ as $v_{circ}(r_0)-v_0$ or applying it at $r_0$; (5) converting angles into times with $\tau\,\Delta\theta/2\pi$, which is wrong for any $e\ne0$.
Problem · Unit 2 · 10 points · 60 min · Standard exam level

P08 — Seismic accelerometer on a vibrating base: inertia force, frequency response and the two operating regimes

A vibration sensor is a rigid case whose floor is bolted to a structure that moves vertically with the prescribed law $z(t)=Z\cos\nu t$ ($Z>0$, $\nu>0$), where $z$ is the height of the case floor above a fixed datum, measured along the upward vertical axis $y$ of the inertial frame $S_0$. Inside the case, a proof mass $m$ (a particle that can only move along the vertical) is joined to the case floor by a linear spring of stiffness $k$ and natural length $L_0$ and by a linear viscous damper of constant $c$ (force $-c$ times the velocity of the mass relative to the case). Uniform gravity $-g\,\mathbf j$ acts.

Figure 1. Seismic accelerometer: the case floor moves as $z(t)=Z\cos\nu t$ with respect to the fixed datum of $S_0$; the proof mass $m$ is held by the spring $k$ and the damper $c$; $x$ is measured in the case frame $S_1$ from the static equilibrium level (both $z$ and $x$ positive upwards).

Let $S_1$ be the frame attached to the case. The relative coordinate $x$ is the height of the mass in $S_1$ measured from its static equilibrium level (the level where it rests when $z\equiv0$), positive upwards. Write $\omega_0=\sqrt{k/m}$ and $\zeta=c/(2\sqrt{km})$ (the Notes' damping parameter is $\gamma=c/(2m)=\zeta\omega_0$), and assume $0<\zeta<1$.

Setup

  • Data: $m$, $k$, $c$, $L_0$, $g$, $Z$, $\nu$. Numerical part (f): $m=0.050$ kg, $k=500$ N/m, $c=2.0$ N s/m, $Z=2.0$ mm, $\nu=50$ rad/s, $g=9.81$ m/s$^2$.
  • Hypotheses: point mass with vertical motion only (lateral guides are smooth), massless spring and damper, linear laws, $S_0$ inertial.
  • Frames: $S_0=\{O;\mathbf i,\mathbf j\}$ fixed; $S_1$ attached to the case, a translating frame: $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\mathbf 0$, origin acceleration $\ddot z\,\mathbf j$.
  • Notation: $x$ is the vertical relative coordinate of the mass (along $\mathbf j$), not a coordinate along the horizontal axis $\mathbf i$ of $S_0$.

Part (a) · 1.5 points

Degrees of freedom, frames and kinematics

How many degrees of freedom does the mass have? Write the absolute position, velocity and acceleration of the mass in terms of $z$ and $x$, and identify the relative and the transport (dragging) acceleration.

Step 1. Degrees of freedom

The mass can only move along the vertical: 1 coordinate. The case is not a body of the problem but a prescribed motion, so it adds no unknown; the constraint "the mass moves on the vertical line of the case" is rheonomic in $S_0$ and fixed in $S_1$. The coordinate is $x$.

Step 2. Absolute kinematics

Let $\xi$ be the height of the mass above the case floor, so the absolute height is $y_P=z+\xi$, and $\xi=\xi_e+x$ with $\xi_e$ constant (found in (b)). Differentiating twice ($\xi_e$ is constant):

$$y_P=z+\xi_e+x,\qquad \dot y_P=\dot z+\dot x,\qquad \ddot y_P=\ddot z+\ddot x$$

Step 3. Relative-motion reading

$S_1$ translates, so $\mathbf a_P=\mathbf a_{O_1}+\mathbf a_{rel}$ (Notes §3.2 with $\boldsymbol\omega=\mathbf 0$): transport acceleration $\ddot z\,\mathbf j$ with, by the chain rule on $\cos(\nu t)$, $\dot z=-\nu Z\sin\nu t$ and $\ddot z=-\nu^2Z\cos\nu t$; relative acceleration $\ddot x\,\mathbf j$; the Coriolis term $2\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf v_{rel}$ vanishes.

Key results
  • 1 degree of freedom, $x(t)$; $z(t)$ is prescribed (rheonomic constraint).
  • $y_P=z+\xi_e+x$, $\ \mathbf a_P=(\ddot z+\ddot x)\,\mathbf j$; $\mathbf a_{rel}=\ddot x\,\mathbf j$, transport $\ddot z\,\mathbf j=-\nu^2Z\cos\nu t\,\mathbf j$, no Coriolis term.

Part (b) · 1.5 points

Forces in the case frame and static equilibrium

List the forces on the mass in $S_1$, including the inertia force, in terms of $\xi$ and $\dot\xi$. Find the static equilibrium height $\xi_e$ for $z\equiv0$ and the static deflection of the spring.

Step 1. Applied forces

  • Spring: elongation $\xi-L_0$; the force on the mass is $\mathbf F_s=-k(\xi-L_0)\,\mathbf j$.
  • Damper: opposes the velocity relative to the case, $\mathbf F_d=-c\dot\xi\,\mathbf j=-c\dot x\,\mathbf j$.
  • Weight: $\mathbf W=-mg\,\mathbf j$.
  • Guides: a smooth lateral reaction $\mathbf N$, horizontal; it has no vertical component and does no work.

Step 2. Inertia force

In the translating frame $S_1$ the relative-motion law is $m\mathbf a_{rel}=\sum\mathbf F-m\mathbf a_{O_1}$ (Notes §4.9), so

$$\mathbf F_{in}=-m\ddot z\,\mathbf j=m\nu^2Z\cos\nu t\,\mathbf j$$

When the case accelerates upwards ($\ddot z>0$) the mass feels pushed down, as in a lift.

Step 3. Static equilibrium

With $z\equiv0$ there is no inertia force and at rest $\dot\xi=0$:

$$-k(\xi_e-L_0)-mg=0\ \Longrightarrow\ \xi_e=L_0-\frac{mg}{k}$$

The spring is compressed by $\delta_{st}=mg/k$ and carries the weight.

Key results
  • $\mathbf F_s=-k(\xi-L_0)\,\mathbf j$, $\ \mathbf F_d=-c\dot\xi\,\mathbf j$, $\ \mathbf W=-mg\,\mathbf j$, $\ \mathbf F_{in}=-m\ddot z\,\mathbf j=m\nu^2Z\cos\nu t\,\mathbf j$; lateral reaction $\mathbf N\perp\mathbf j$.
  • $\xi_e=L_0-mg/k$; static deflection $\delta_{st}=mg/k$ (spring compressed).

Part (c) · 1.5 points

Equation of motion

Project the relative-motion law on $\mathbf j$ and obtain the equation of motion for $x$. Explain why $g$ does not appear, and write the equation in the normalised form with $\omega_0$ and $\zeta$.

Step 1. Projection

$$m\ddot\xi=-k(\xi-L_0)-c\dot\xi-mg-m\ddot z$$

Step 2. Change of variable

With $\xi=\xi_e+x$, $\dot\xi=\dot x$, $\ddot\xi=\ddot x$ and $-k(\xi_e+x-L_0)=-kx-k(\xi_e-L_0)=-kx+mg$ (from (b)):

$$m\ddot x=-kx+mg-c\dot x-mg-m\ddot z\ \Longrightarrow\ m\ddot x+c\dot x+kx=-m\ddot z=m\nu^2Z\cos\nu t$$

The weight is a constant force; measuring $x$ from the static equilibrium absorbs it into the spring preload, so it drops out of the dynamics. This is true only because the spring is linear: the same constant offset works at every $x$.

Step 3. Normal form

Dividing by $m$, with $k/m=\omega_0^2$ and $c/m=2\zeta\omega_0$:

$$\ddot x+2\zeta\omega_0\dot x+\omega_0^2x=\nu^2Z\cos\nu t$$

This is Notes Eq. 5.32 with the force amplitude $F_1=m\nu^2Z$: the base motion acts as a harmonic force whose amplitude grows with $\nu^2$. Extension (Lagrange equations are not in the course notes): cross-check by Lagrange in $S_0$: $T=\tfrac12m(\dot z+\dot\xi)^2$, $V=mg(z+\xi)+\tfrac12k(\xi-L_0)^2$ and the damper's generalised force $-c\dot\xi$ give the same equation.

Key results
  • $m\ddot x+c\dot x+kx=m\nu^2Z\cos\nu t$.
  • $\ddot x+2\zeta\omega_0\dot x+\omega_0^2x=\nu^2Z\cos\nu t$; $g$ is cancelled by the static preload of the spring.

Part (d) · 2 points

Steady-state response

Using the complex representation, find the amplitude $X$ and the phase $\varphi$ of the steady-state solution $x_p=X\cos(\nu t+\varphi)$, and write $X/Z$ as a function of $r=\nu/\omega_0$ and $\zeta$. What is the complete solution?

Step 1. Complex equation

The real forcing is $\nu^2Z\cos\nu t=\mathrm{Re}\big(\nu^2Ze^{i\nu t}\big)$ (Notes §5.2 and §5.4). With $\tilde x=Ae^{i\nu t}$: $\dot{\tilde x}=i\nu\tilde x$, $\ddot{\tilde x}=-\nu^2\tilde x$ (chain rule on the exponential), and

$$\big(-\nu^2+2i\zeta\omega_0\nu+\omega_0^2\big)A=\nu^2Z\ \Longrightarrow\ A=\frac{\nu^2Z}{\omega_0^2-\nu^2+2i\zeta\omega_0\nu}$$

Step 2. Amplitude and phase

Writing $A=Xe^{i\varphi}$, $|A|$ is the modulus of the numerator over that of the denominator:

$$X=\frac{\nu^2Z}{\sqrt{(\omega_0^2-\nu^2)^2+4\zeta^2\omega_0^2\nu^2}},\qquad \varphi=-\arg\big(\omega_0^2-\nu^2+2i\zeta\omega_0\nu\big)$$

so $\tan\varphi=-2\zeta\omega_0\nu/(\omega_0^2-\nu^2)$; the imaginary part of the denominator is positive, hence $-\pi<\varphi<0$ (the mass lags behind the forcing, Notes Eq. 5.42). The real part is $x_p=X\cos(\nu t+\varphi)$.

Step 3. Dimensionless form

Dividing numerator and denominator by $\omega_0^2$ with $r=\nu/\omega_0$:

$$\frac XZ=\frac{r^2}{\sqrt{(1-r^2)^2+(2\zeta r)^2}},\qquad \tan\varphi=-\frac{2\zeta r}{1-r^2}$$

Step 4. Complete solution

Add the general solution of the homogeneous equation (Notes Eq. 5.26, underdamped): $x=X\cos(\nu t+\varphi)+a\,e^{-\zeta\omega_0t}\cos(\omega_0\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}\,t+\beta)$, with $a$, $\beta$ fixed by the initial conditions. The second term is the transient.

Key results
  • $A=\dfrac{\nu^2Z}{\omega_0^2-\nu^2+2i\zeta\omega_0\nu}$; $\ X=|A|=\dfrac{\nu^2Z}{\sqrt{(\omega_0^2-\nu^2)^2+4\zeta^2\omega_0^2\nu^2}}$.
  • $\tan\varphi=-\dfrac{2\zeta\omega_0\nu}{\omega_0^2-\nu^2}$ with $-\pi<\varphi<0$; $\ \dfrac XZ=\dfrac{r^2}{\sqrt{(1-r^2)^2+(2\zeta r)^2}}$.
  • $x=X\cos(\nu t+\varphi)+a\,e^{-\zeta\omega_0t}\cos(\omega_0\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}\,t+\beta)$.

Part (e) · 2 points

Operating regimes and the resonant peak

Discuss the limits $\nu\ll\omega_0$ and $\nu\gg\omega_0$: what does $x$ measure in each case? Then find the forcing frequency at which $X$ is maximum and the maximum value of $X/Z$. Is it at $\nu=\omega_0$, or at $\omega_0\sqrt{1-2\zeta^2}$ as in Notes §5.4.1?

Step 1. Low frequency: accelerometer

For $r\to0$ the denominator tends to 1 and $\varphi\to0$: $X\approx r^2Z=\nu^2Z/\omega_0^2$ and

$$x\approx\frac{\nu^2Z\cos\nu t}{\omega_0^2}=-\frac{\ddot z}{\omega_0^2}$$

The relative displacement is proportional to the acceleration of the base: the spring force $kx$ supplies the force $-m\ddot z$ the mass needs to follow the case.

Step 2. High frequency: vibrometer

For $r\to\infty$: $X/Z\to r^2/r^2=1$ and $\tan\varphi=2\zeta r/(r^2-1)\to0^+$ with $\varphi$ in the third quadrant, so $\varphi\to-\pi$, so $x\approx Z\cos(\nu t-\pi)=-z$. The absolute height $y_P=z+\xi_e+x\approx\xi_e$ is constant: the mass cannot follow the fast motion and stays still in $S_0$, so $x$ measures the displacement of the base.

Step 3. Maximum of the response

$(X/Z)^2=f(s)=s^2/D$ with $D=(1-s)^2+4\zeta^2s$ and $D'=-2(1-s)+4\zeta^2$. By the quotient rule, $f'(s)=0$ requires $2sD-s^2D'=0$, i.e. $2D=sD'$:

$$2(1-2s+s^2)+8\zeta^2s=-2s+2s^2+4\zeta^2s\ \Longrightarrow\ 2-2s+4\zeta^2s=0\ \Longrightarrow\ s=\frac{1}{1-2\zeta^2}$$

which is admissible ($s>0$) for $\zeta<1/\sqrt2$. Then $1-s=-2\zeta^2/(1-2\zeta^2)$ and

$$D=\frac{4\zeta^4+4\zeta^2(1-2\zeta^2)}{(1-2\zeta^2)^2}=\frac{4\zeta^2(1-\zeta^2)}{(1-2\zeta^2)^2},\qquad f=\frac{s^2}{D}=\frac{1}{4\zeta^2(1-\zeta^2)}$$

so $\nu_{peak}=\omega_0/\sqrt{1-2\zeta^2}$ and $(X/Z)_{max}=1/\big(2\zeta\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}\big)$.

Step 4. The trap

The Notes' resonant frequency $\sqrt{\omega_0^2-2\gamma^2}=\omega_0\sqrt{1-2\zeta^2}$ (Eq. 5.46) is for a force of constant amplitude $F_1$. Here the forcing $m\nu^2Z$ grows with $\nu^2$, which shifts the maximum of the relative amplitude above $\omega_0$. Neither $\nu=\omega_0$ (where $X/Z=1/(2\zeta)$) nor $\omega_0\sqrt{1-2\zeta^2}$ is the peak.

Key results
  • $\nu\ll\omega_0$: $x\approx\nu^2Z\cos\nu t/\omega_0^2=-\ddot z/\omega_0^2$ (accelerometer). $\nu\gg\omega_0$: $X\to Z$, $\varphi\to-\pi$, $x\approx-z$ (the mass stays still in $S_0$: vibrometer).
  • Peak at $r^2=\dfrac{1}{1-2\zeta^2}$, i.e. $\nu_{peak}=\dfrac{\omega_0}{\sqrt{1-2\zeta^2}}>\omega_0$ (for $\zeta<1/\sqrt2$); $\ \Big(\dfrac XZ\Big)_{max}=\dfrac{1}{2\zeta\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}}$.

Part (f) · 1.5 points

Numerical case

With the data of the Setup, compute $\omega_0$, $\zeta$, the static deflection, $X$, $\varphi$, the energy dissipated per cycle, the decay of the transient, and the error of an accelerometer that reads $\omega_0^2X$ as the base acceleration amplitude. Locate the resonant peak.

Step 1. Parameters

$\omega_0=\sqrt{500/0.050}=100$ rad/s, $\zeta=2.0/(2\sqrt{500\times0.050})=2.0/10=0.2$, $r=50/100=0.5$, $\delta_{st}=mg/k=0.050\times9.81/500=9.81\times10^{-4}$ m $=0.981$ mm.

Step 2. Amplitude and phase

$$\frac XZ=\frac{0.25}{\sqrt{0.75^2+0.2^2}}=\frac{0.25}{\sqrt{0.6025}}=0.3221\ \Longrightarrow\ X=0.644\ \text{mm},\qquad \varphi=-\arctan\frac{0.2}{0.75}=-14.93^\circ$$

Step 3. Energy per cycle

With $\dot x=-\nu X\sin(\nu t+\varphi)$ and $\int_0^{2\pi/\nu}\sin^2(\nu t+\varphi)\,dt=\pi/\nu$ (the antiderivative of $\sin^2u$ is $\tfrac u2-\tfrac{\sin2u}{4}$, evaluated between $u=\varphi$ and $u=\varphi+2\pi$):

$$W=c\nu^2X^2\frac{\pi}{\nu}=\pi c\nu X^2=\pi\times2.0\times50\times(6.442\times10^{-4})^2=1.30\times10^{-4}\ \text{J}$$

In steady state this equals the work done per cycle by the inertia force $m\nu^2Z\cos\nu t$ on the relative motion.

Step 4. Transient

The homogeneous part decays as $e^{-\zeta\omega_0t}=e^{-20t}$: relaxation time $1/(\zeta\omega_0)=0.05$ s, and $e^{-20t}=0.01$ at $t=\ln100/20=0.230$ s. It oscillates at $\omega_d=\omega_0\sqrt{1-\zeta^2}=97.98$ rad/s.

Step 5. Accelerometer error and peak

The true acceleration amplitude is $\nu^2Z=2500\times0.002=5.0$ m/s$^2$; the instrument reads $\omega_0^2X=10^4\times6.442\times10^{-4}=6.44$ m/s$^2$, i.e. $+28.8$ %: at $r=0.5$ with $\zeta=0.2$ the sensor is too close to resonance. The peak is at $\nu_{peak}=100/\sqrt{0.92}=104.3$ rad/s with $X/Z=1/(0.4\sqrt{0.96})=2.552$, $X=5.10$ mm.

Key results
  • $\omega_0=100$ rad/s, $\zeta=0.2$, $r=0.5$, $\delta_{st}=0.981$ mm.
  • $X=0.644$ mm, $\varphi=-14.93^\circ$; $W=\pi c\nu X^2=1.30\times10^{-4}$ J per cycle.
  • Transient $\propto e^{-20t}$ (relaxation time 0.05 s, below 1 % after 0.230 s), $\omega_d=97.98$ rad/s.
  • $\omega_0^2X=6.44$ m/s$^2$ against $\nu^2Z=5.0$ m/s$^2$: $+28.8$ % error; peak at $\nu=104.3$ rad/s with $X=2.552\,Z=5.10$ mm.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $\omega_0^2=k/m$ in (N/m)/kg = s$^{-2}$; $\zeta=c/(2\sqrt{km})$ in (N s/m)/(kg s$^{-1}$) = 1; $\nu^2Z$ in m s$^{-2}$, the same as $\omega_0^2x$; $W=\pi c\nu X^2$ in (N s/m)(s$^{-1}$)(m$^2$) = J.
  • Limits: $\zeta\to0$ gives $X/Z=r^2/|1-r^2|$, infinite at $r=1$; $Z\to0$ leaves the free damped oscillator of Notes §5.3. With $\zeta\approx0.7$ the factor $1/\sqrt{(1-r^2)^2+(2\zeta r)^2}$ stays close to 1 over a wide band (at $r=0.5$ it is 0.975, a $-2.5$ % error), which is why real accelerometers are built with that damping.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) writing the inertia force as $+m\ddot z$, or forgetting it and writing the spring force with the absolute coordinate; (2) keeping $mg$ in the equation after measuring $x$ from the static equilibrium (or dropping it when $x$ is measured from the natural length); (3) taking the peak at $\nu=\omega_0$ or at $\omega_0\sqrt{1-2\zeta^2}$; (4) choosing $\varphi$ in the wrong quadrant with the arctangent above resonance.
Problem · Unit 2 · 10 points · 60 min · High difficulty / trap

P09 — Bead on a vertical hoop held by a spring: reaction, equilibria and a pitchfork bifurcation

A smooth circular hoop of radius $a$ and centre $C$ is fixed in a vertical plane. A bead $P$ of mass $m$ is threaded on it (it can neither leave the hoop nor the plane). A linear spring of stiffness $k$ and natural length $l_0$ joins $P$ to the fixed point $A$, which lies on the vertical through $C$ at the height $d=2a$ above $C$. The natural length is $l_0=d-a=a$, so the spring is unstretched when the bead is at the top $T$ of the hoop. Gravity $-g\,\mathbf j$ acts. The position of the bead is given by the angle $\theta$ between $\mathbf{CT}$ (upward vertical) and $\mathbf{CP}$, positive clockwise in the figure (towards $+x$).

Figure 1. Bead $P$ on the smooth hoop (centre $C$, radius $a$), spring of stiffness $k$ from the fixed point $A$ at height $d=2a$ above $C$, angle $\theta$ from the top; right: $V(\theta)/(mga)$ for $\kappa=ka/(mg)=1/2$ and $\kappa=1$.

Use the inertial frame $S_0=\{C;\mathbf i,\mathbf j\}$ ($\mathbf i$ horizontal, $\mathbf j$ upward) and the unit vectors $\mathbf e_r=\sin\theta\,\mathbf i+\cos\theta\,\mathbf j$ (along $\mathbf{CP}$) and $\mathbf e_\theta=\cos\theta\,\mathbf i-\sin\theta\,\mathbf j$ (tangent to the hoop, towards increasing $\theta$). The results are expressed with the dimensionless stiffness

$$\kappa=\frac{ka}{mg}$$

Setup

  • Data: $m$, $a$, $k$, $g$; $d=2a$, $l_0=a$. Numerical values in (e) and (f): $a=0.40$ m, $g=9.81$ m/s$^2$.
  • Hypotheses: point particle; smooth, rigid, fixed hoop (bilateral constraint); massless linear spring that can be stretched or compressed; $S_0$ inertial.
  • Spring length: $l=|\mathbf{AP}|$ with $\mathbf{AP}=a\,\mathbf e_r-d\,\mathbf j$.

Part (a) · 1.5 points

Degrees of freedom, geometry and potential energy

How many degrees of freedom has the bead? Find the spring length $l(\theta)$ and the total potential energy $V(\theta)$; check that the applied forces are conservative.

Step 1. Degrees of freedom

A free particle has 3 coordinates. The hoop is a curve: two holonomic, scleronomic constraints (the plane and the circle $|\mathbf{CP}|=a$), so 1 degree of freedom remains, described by $\theta$ (Notes §2.7).

Step 2. Spring length

$$l^2=\mathbf{AP}\cdot\mathbf{AP}=(a\,\mathbf e_r-d\,\mathbf j)\cdot(a\,\mathbf e_r-d\,\mathbf j)=a^2-2ad\,(\mathbf e_r\cdot\mathbf j)+d^2=a^2+d^2-2ad\cos\theta$$

With $d=2a$: $l=a\sqrt{5-4\cos\theta}$, from $l(0)=a$ at the top to $l(\pi)=3a$ at the bottom.

Step 3. Potentials

Weight: uniform, $V_g=mg\,y_P=mga\cos\theta$. Spring: $V_s=\tfrac12k(l-l_0)^2$, a function of the position only (Notes §4.4). Both are conservative, the reaction of the smooth fixed hoop does no work (part (b)), so

$$V(\theta)=mga\cos\theta+\tfrac12k\big(l(\theta)-l_0\big)^2,\qquad \frac{V}{mga}=\cos\theta+\frac{\kappa}{2}\Big(\sqrt{5-4\cos\theta}-1\Big)^2$$
Key results
  • 1 degree of freedom, $\theta$ (the hoop removes 2 of the 3 spatial coordinates).
  • $l^2=a^2+d^2-2ad\cos\theta=a^2(5-4\cos\theta)$; $\ l(0)=a=l_0$, $\ l(\pi)=3a$.
  • $V(\theta)=mga\cos\theta+\tfrac12k\big(l(\theta)-l_0\big)^2$, i.e. $\dfrac{V}{mga}=\cos\theta+\dfrac{\kappa}{2}\Big(\sqrt{5-4\cos\theta}-1\Big)^2$.

Part (b) · 2 points

Equation of motion and reaction of the hoop

Write the kinematics of $P$ in $\{\mathbf e_r,\mathbf e_\theta\}$, the forces, the equation of motion free of the reaction, and the reaction $\mathbf N$ as a function of $\theta$ and $\dot\theta$. Check the equation of motion against $V(\theta)$.

Step 1. Change of basis

The pair $\{\mathbf e_r,\mathbf e_\theta\}$ turns with $\theta$. Since $\theta$ grows clockwise (from $\mathbf j$ towards $\mathbf i$), the right-handed order in the plane is $\{\mathbf e_\theta,\mathbf e_r\}$: with $\mathbf k=\mathbf i\times\mathbf j$, $\mathbf e_\theta\times\mathbf e_r=(\cos\theta\cos\theta+\sin\theta\sin\theta)\,\mathbf k=\mathbf k$ (the order $\{\mathbf e_r,\mathbf e_\theta\}$ has determinant $-1$). Then

$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf e_\theta\ \mathbf e_r\end{pmatrix}=[R]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf i\ \mathbf j\end{pmatrix},\qquad [R]=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\theta&-\sin\theta\ \sin\theta&\cos\theta\end{pmatrix}$$

with $[R][R]^T=\begin{pmatrix}\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta&\cos\theta\sin\theta-\sin\theta\cos\theta\ \sin\theta\cos\theta-\cos\theta\sin\theta&\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta\end{pmatrix}=I$ and $\det[R]=\cos^2\theta+\sin^2\theta=+1$.

Step 2. Kinematics

$\mathbf r_{CP}=a\,\mathbf e_r$. By the chain rule, $\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot\theta(\cos\theta\,\mathbf i-\sin\theta\,\mathbf j)=\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$ and $\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta=\dot\theta(-\sin\theta\,\mathbf i-\cos\theta\,\mathbf j)=-\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_r$. With $a$ constant:

$$\mathbf v_P=a\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta,\qquad \mathbf a_P=a\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta+a\dot\theta\,\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta=a\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta-a\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_r$$

Step 3. Forces

  • Weight: $\mathbf W=-mg\,\mathbf j=-mg\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_r+mg\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$ (using $\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf e_r=\cos\theta$, $\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf e_\theta=-\sin\theta$).
  • Spring: $\mathbf F_s=-k\,\dfrac{l-l_0}{l}\,\mathbf{AP}=-k\Big(1-\dfrac{l_0}{l}\Big)\big[(a-d\cos\theta)\,\mathbf e_r+d\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta\big]$, since $\mathbf{AP}\cdot\mathbf e_r=a-d\cos\theta$ and $\mathbf{AP}\cdot\mathbf e_\theta=-d\,(\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf e_\theta)=d\sin\theta$.
  • Reaction: the hoop is smooth, so $\mathbf N$ is orthogonal to the tangent $\mathbf e_\theta$ (Notes §6.5); its component normal to the plane balances nothing (all other forces are in the plane), so $\mathbf N=N\,\mathbf e_r$.

Step 4. Equation of motion (tangential projection)

$$ma\ddot\theta=mg\sin\theta-k\Big(1-\frac{l_0}{l}\Big)d\sin\theta=\sin\theta\Big[mg-kd\Big(1-\frac{l_0}{l}\Big)\Big]$$

$N$ does not appear: this is the reaction-free equation.

Step 5. Reaction (radial projection)

$$-ma\dot\theta^2=-mg\cos\theta-k\Big(1-\frac{l_0}{l}\Big)(a-d\cos\theta)+N$$
$$N=mg\cos\theta+k\Big(1-\frac{l_0}{l}\Big)(a-d\cos\theta)-ma\dot\theta^2$$

Step 6. Check with $V$

By the chain rule, $\dfrac{dV}{d\theta}=-mga\sin\theta+k(l-l_0)\dfrac{dl}{d\theta}$ and, differentiating $l^2=a^2+d^2-2ad\cos\theta$ implicitly, $2l\,l'=2ad\sin\theta$, so $l'=ad\sin\theta/l$. Hence

$$V'(\theta)=\sin\theta\Big[-mga+kad\Big(1-\frac{l_0}{l}\Big)\Big]=-a\cdot ma\ddot\theta$$

which is the energy form $ma^2\ddot\theta=-V'(\theta)$ of Step 4 (the Lagrange equation, an extension beyond the course notes, gives the same).

Key results
  • $\mathbf a_P=a\ddot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta-a\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_r$; $\ \mathbf N=N\,\mathbf e_r$ (smooth hoop, planar forces).
  • $ma\ddot\theta=\sin\theta\Big[mg-kd\Big(1-\dfrac{l_0}{l}\Big)\Big]$, equal to $-V'(\theta)/a$.
  • $N=mg\cos\theta+k\Big(1-\dfrac{l_0}{l}\Big)(a-d\cos\theta)-ma\dot\theta^2$ (positive: outwards).

Part (c) · 2 points

Equilibrium positions

Find all the equilibrium positions as functions of $\kappa$. Show that besides $\theta=0$ and $\theta=\pi$ there is a symmetric pair $\theta=\pm\theta^*$ only above a critical value $\kappa_c$, and give $\cos\theta^*(\kappa)$.

Step 1. Factorisation

From (b), with $d=2a$, $l_0=a$ and $k=\kappa mg/a$:

$$\frac{V'(\theta)}{mga}=\sin\theta\Big[-1+2\kappa\Big(1-\frac{a}{l}\Big)\Big]$$

Equilibria are the zeros of $V'$ (Notes §4.6).

Step 2. $\sin\theta=0$

$\theta=0$ and $\theta=\pi$ are equilibria for every $\kappa$: by symmetry, the spring force and the weight are both along the vertical there, so they have no tangential component.

Step 3. The symmetric pair

$-1+2\kappa(1-a/l)=0\Rightarrow 1-a/l=1/(2\kappa)\Rightarrow l^*=\dfrac{a}{1-1/(2\kappa)}=\dfrac{2\kappa a}{2\kappa-1}$, which requires $\kappa>\tfrac12$ for $l^*>0$. Substituting into $l^2=a^2(5-4\cos\theta)$:

$$\cos\theta^*=\frac14\Big(5-\frac{l^{*2}}{a^2}\Big)=\frac14\Big(5-\frac{4\kappa^2}{(2\kappa-1)^2}\Big)$$

Step 4. Existence condition

A point of the hoop has $a\le l\le3a$; a new equilibrium needs $a<l^*<3a$. $l^*>a$ holds for every $\kappa>\tfrac12$. $l^*<3a\iff2\kappa<3(2\kappa-1)\iff\kappa>\tfrac34$. So the pair $\pm\theta^*$ exists only for $\kappa>\kappa_c=\tfrac34$; at $\kappa=\tfrac34$, $\cos\theta^*=\tfrac14(5-9)=-1$: the pair is born at the bottom. As $\kappa\to\infty$, $l^*\to a$ and $\theta^*\to0$.

Key results
  • $\theta=0$ (top) and $\theta=\pi$ (bottom) for every $\kappa$.
  • $G=0\iff 1-\dfrac{a}{l}=\dfrac{1}{2\kappa}\iff l^*=\dfrac{2\kappa a}{2\kappa-1}$; $\ \cos\theta^*=\dfrac14\Big(5-\dfrac{4\kappa^2}{(2\kappa-1)^2}\Big)$.
  • The pair exists only for $\kappa>\kappa_c=\tfrac34$ ($\theta^*\to\pi$ as $\kappa\to\tfrac34^+$, $\theta^*\to0$ as $\kappa\to\infty$).

Part (d) · 1.5 points

Stability and the bifurcation

Classify the stability of each equilibrium in the two regimes $\kappa<\tfrac34$ and $\kappa>\tfrac34$. Describe what happens at $\kappa=\tfrac34$. Is "the bottom of the hoop is always stable" true?

Step 1. Second derivative

From $V'=\sin\theta\,G(\theta)$ with $G=-mga+kad(1-l_0/l)$, the product rule gives $V''=\cos\theta\,G+\sin\theta\,G'$, and by the chain rule $G'=kad\,\dfrac{l_0}{l^2}\,l'=kad\,\dfrac{l_0}{l^2}\,\dfrac{ad\sin\theta}{l}$.

Step 2. Top and bottom

At $\theta=0$: $l=l_0$, so $G(0)=-mga$ and $V''(0)=-mga<0$: unstable for every $\kappa$ (the spring is slack there and cannot hold the bead up).

At $\theta=\pi$: $l=3a$, $G(\pi)=-mga+k\,a\,2a\,\tfrac23=-mga+\tfrac43ka^2$ and $V''(\pi)=\cos\pi\,G(\pi)=mga-\tfrac43ka^2=mga\big(1-\tfrac43\kappa\big)$.

Step 3. The pair

At $\theta^*$, $G=0$, so $V''(\theta^*)=\sin\theta^*G'(\theta^*)=\dfrac{ka^2d^2l_0\sin^2\theta^*}{l^{*3}}>0$: a minimum, stable.

Step 4. The two regimes (the trap)

  • $\kappa<\tfrac34$ (soft spring): two equilibria; top unstable, bottom stable.
  • $\kappa>\tfrac34$ (stiff spring): four equilibria; top unstable, bottom unstable, $\pm\theta^*$ stable. The stretched spring pulls the bead up harder than gravity pulls it down, and the bead settles on one side.

The bottom is not always stable. At $\kappa=\tfrac34$, $V''(\pi)=0$ and $V'''(\pi)=0$ (symmetry), and $V''''(\pi)=\tfrac13mga>0$: the bottom is still a (flat) minimum. As $\kappa$ crosses $\tfrac34$ the minimum at the bottom splits into two minima that move away symmetrically: a supercritical pitchfork bifurcation (see the energy curves of the figure).

Key results
  • Top: $V''(0)=-mga<0$, always unstable.
  • Bottom: $V''(\pi)=mga\big(1-\tfrac43\kappa\big)$: stable for $\kappa<\tfrac34$, unstable for $\kappa>\tfrac34$.
  • Pair: $V''(\theta^*)=\dfrac{ka^2d^2l_0\sin^2\theta^*}{l^{*3}}>0$: stable whenever it exists. Supercritical pitchfork at $\kappa=\tfrac34$ ($V''''(\pi)=\tfrac13mga>0$ there).

Part (e) · 1.5 points

Small oscillations

Compute the angular frequency of small oscillations (i) about the bottom for $\kappa=\tfrac12$ and (ii) about $\theta^*$ for $\kappa=1$ (give $\theta^*$). Numerical values for $a=0.40$ m.

Step 1. Linearisation

With $\theta=\theta_e+\eta$, $V'(\theta)=V'(\theta_e)+V''(\theta_e)\eta+O(\eta^2)$ and $V'(\theta_e)=0$, so $ma^2\ddot\eta+V''(\theta_e)\eta=0$ and $\omega^2=V''(\theta_e)/(ma^2)$ when $V''(\theta_e)>0$.

Step 2. Bottom, $\kappa=\tfrac12$

$V''(\pi)=mga(1-\tfrac43\cdot\tfrac12)=\tfrac13mga$, so $\omega^2=\dfrac{g}{3a}=\dfrac{9.81}{1.2}=8.175$ s$^{-2}$ and $\omega=2.859$ rad/s.

Step 3. Side equilibrium, $\kappa=1$

$l^*=2a/(2-1)=2a$, $\cos\theta^*=\tfrac14(5-4)=\tfrac14$, $\theta^*=\arccos\tfrac14=75.52^\circ$, $\sin^2\theta^*=\tfrac{15}{16}$. With $k=mg/a$, $d=2a$, $l_0=a$:

$$V''(\theta^*)=\frac{k\,a^2\,(4a^2)\,a\cdot\tfrac{15}{16}}{8a^3}=\frac{15}{32}ka^2=\frac{15}{32}mga,\qquad \omega^2=\frac{15}{32}\frac ga$$

Numerically $\omega^2=\tfrac{15}{32}\times\tfrac{9.81}{0.40}=11.50$ s$^{-2}$, $\omega=3.391$ rad/s.

Key results
  • (i) $\omega^2=\dfrac{g}{3a}$, $\ \omega=2.859$ rad/s.
  • (ii) $l^*=2a$, $\cos\theta^*=\tfrac14$, $\theta^*=75.52^\circ$; $\ \omega^2=\dfrac{15}{32}\dfrac ga$, $\ \omega=3.391$ rad/s.

Part (f) · 1.5 points

Energy integral and the bead at the bottom

Write the energy first integral. For $\kappa=\tfrac12$ the bead leaves the top with a vanishingly small speed. Find its speed at the bottom and the reaction of the hoop there. Would a bead released exactly at rest at the top move?

Step 1. First integral

The reaction is normal to $\mathbf v_P=a\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$, so its power $\mathbf N\cdot\mathbf v_P=0$ (Notes §6.4 to §6.5); the other forces derive from $V$. Hence (Notes §4.5)

$$\tfrac12ma^2\dot\theta^2+mga\cos\theta+\tfrac12k\big(l(\theta)-l_0\big)^2=E$$

Check: $\dfrac{dE}{dt}=\dot\theta\big(ma^2\ddot\theta+V'(\theta)\big)=0$ by (b).

Step 2. Top versus bottom

$V(0)=mga+0$ and $V(\pi)=-mga+\tfrac12k(3a-a)^2=-mga+2ka^2$, so $V(\pi)-V(0)=2ka^2-2mga=2mga(\kappa-1)$.

Step 3. Speed at the bottom

$\theta=0$ is an equilibrium: released exactly at rest there, the bead stays. With an infinitesimal push, $E=V(0)$. For $\kappa=\tfrac12<\tfrac34$ the only equilibria are $0$ and $\pi$, so $V$ decreases monotonically on $(0,\pi)$ and the bead reaches the bottom with

$$\tfrac12mv^2=V(0)-V(\pi)=2mga(1-\kappa)\ \Longrightarrow\ v^2=4ga(1-\kappa)=2ga,\qquad v=\sqrt{2\times9.81\times0.40}=2.801\ \text{m/s}$$

With no spring ($\kappa=0$) it would be $v^2=4ga$: the spring stores part of the energy.

Step 4. Reaction at the bottom

At $\theta=\pi$: $l=3a$, $1-l_0/l=\tfrac23$, $a-d\cos\pi=3a$, $a\dot\theta^2=v^2/a=2g$ and $k=mg/(2a)$:

$$N=mg\cos\pi+\frac{mg}{2a}\cdot\frac23\cdot3a-m\cdot2g=-mg+mg-2mg=-2mg$$

$N<0$ means the reaction points along $-\mathbf e_r$, towards $C$; at the bottom $-\mathbf e_r=\mathbf j$, so the hoop pushes the bead up with $2mg$. Check on the vertical: $N_{up}-mg+T_{spring}=mv^2/a$ with $T_{spring}=k(3a-a)=mg$ gives $N_{up}=2mg-mg+mg=2mg$.

Key results
  • $\tfrac12ma^2\dot\theta^2+mga\cos\theta+\tfrac12k(l-l_0)^2=E$; $\ V(\pi)-V(0)=2mga(\kappa-1)$.
  • $\kappa=\tfrac12$: $v_{bottom}^2=4ga(1-\kappa)=2ga$, $v=2.801$ m/s (against $4ga$ without spring).
  • $N(\pi)=-2mg$: the hoop pushes the bead towards $C$, i.e. upwards with $2mg$. At rest exactly at the top the bead stays (equilibrium); it needs a disturbance.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $\kappa=ka/(mg)$ is (N/m)(m)/N = 1; $V$ and $mga$ in J; $\omega^2=g/a$ in s$^{-2}$; $N$ in N; $v^2=ga$ in m$^2$ s$^{-2}$.
  • Limits: $\kappa\to0$ recovers the bead on a hoop under gravity alone (top unstable, bottom stable, $\omega^2=g/a$ at the bottom: our $V''(\pi)$ gives $mga$). $\kappa\to\infty$: the spring dominates and the stable positions approach the top, where the spring is unstretched. For $\kappa=1$, $V(\pi)=V(0)$: a bead leaving the top with a vanishing speed approaches the (now unstable) bottom only asymptotically, never reaching it in finite time.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) assuming the bottom is always the stable position; (2) missing the side equilibria by cancelling $\sin\theta$ without keeping the second factor; (3) using $l-l_0$ with the wrong natural length (the spring is unstretched at the top, not at the bottom); (4) forgetting that the reaction of a bead on a hoop can point either way (bilateral constraint), and assuming $N\ge0$; (5) "releasing from rest at the top" without noticing that it is an equilibrium.
Problem · Unit 5 · 10 points · 60 min · Standard exam level

P10 — Free fall from a tower on the rotating Earth: eastward deflection, the order of the north-south effect and an upward shot

A particle $P$ of mass $m$ is released from rest relative to the ground from the top of a tower of height $h$ standing at the point $A$ of the Earth's surface at latitude $\lambda$ (northern hemisphere). The Earth rotates with constant angular velocity of modulus $\Omega$ about its polar axis, whose unit vector $\mathbf k_E$ points to the north pole; the centre of the Earth is taken as fixed in an inertial frame $S_0$.

The local frame $S_1=\{A;\mathbf i,\mathbf j,\mathbf k\}$ is attached to the ground at the foot $A$ of the tower and rotates with the Earth: $x$ points east, $y$ points north, $z$ points up along the plumb line. In $S_1$ the particle feels the effective gravity $-g\,\mathbf k$, which already contains the centrifugal force of the point $A$ (gravitation plus $-m\boldsymbol\Omega\times(\boldsymbol\Omega\times\mathbf r_{CA})$, $C$ the centre of the Earth). Air drag is neglected and $h$ is small compared with the radius of the Earth.

Figure 1. Free fall on the rotating Earth: point $A$ at latitude $\lambda$, rotation $\boldsymbol\Omega$ along the Earth axis; local frame $S_1=\{A;x\ \text{east},\,y\ \text{north},\,z\ \text{up}\}$ (east points into the page), $\boldsymbol\Omega=\Omega\cos\lambda\,\mathbf j+\Omega\sin\lambda\,\mathbf k$, particle $P$ released from rest at height $h$.

Setup

  • Data: $m$, $h$, $g$, $\Omega$, $\lambda$. Numerical part: $h=100$ m, $\lambda=40^\circ$, $g=9.81$ m s$^{-2}$, $\Omega=7.292\times10^{-5}$ rad s$^{-1}$ ($2\pi$ per sidereal day of $86\,164$ s).
  • Hypotheses: point particle, uniform effective gravity along the height of the tower, $\Omega$ constant, $\Omega t\ll1$ during the fall (so a solution to first order in $\Omega$ is meaningful), no drag.
  • $S_1$ is a rotating frame: $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\Omega\,\mathbf k_E$ with $\dot{\boldsymbol\omega}_{10}=\mathbf 0$.

Part (a) · 1.5 points

Degrees of freedom, angular velocity of $S_1$ and inertia forces

How many degrees of freedom has $P$? Write $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}$ in the basis of $S_1$ and list the inertia forces that act on $P$ in $S_1$, saying which one is absorbed into $-g\,\mathbf k$ and which one is kept.

Step 1. Count

$P$ is a free particle in space: 3 degrees of freedom, the coordinates $(x,y,z)$ of $\mathbf r=\overrightarrow{AP}$ in $S_1$. Nothing constrains it between release and impact.

Step 2. The angular velocity in the local basis

The local vertical $\mathbf k$ is the plumb line at $A$ (statement), and the latitude $\lambda$ is measured from this plumb line: it is the angle between $\mathbf k$ and the equatorial plane. The angle between $\mathbf k$ and $\mathbf k_E$ is therefore $90^\circ-\lambda$, so $\mathbf k_E\cdot\mathbf k=\sin\lambda$ by definition of $\lambda$. The polar axis lies in the meridian plane through $A$, which contains $\mathbf j$ (north) and $\mathbf k$, so $\mathbf k_E\cdot\mathbf i=0$, and it leans towards the north: $\mathbf k_E\cdot\mathbf j=\cos\lambda$. Check: $\cos^2\lambda+\sin^2\lambda=1$. The ground turns with the Earth, so

$$\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\Omega\,\mathbf k_E=\Omega\cos\lambda\,\mathbf j+\Omega\sin\lambda\,\mathbf k,\qquad \dot{\boldsymbol\omega}_{10}=\mathbf 0$$

Step 3. Change of basis

The polar axis is best described with the cylindrical basis of the Earth at $A$, $\{\mathbf e_\rho,\mathbf e_\varphi,\mathbf k_E\}$: $\mathbf e_\rho$ perpendicular to the polar axis and pointing away from it, in the meridian plane of $A$, $\mathbf e_\varphi=\mathbf i$ (east), $\mathbf k_E$ the polar axis. This basis turns about $\mathbf k_E$ with angular velocity $\Omega$ relative to $S_0$, like the local one. Projecting the local unit vectors ($\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf e_\rho=-\sin\lambda$, $\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf k_E=\cos\lambda$, $\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf e_\rho=\cos\lambda$, $\mathbf k\cdot\mathbf k_E=\sin\lambda$):

$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf i\\ \mathbf j\\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix}=[R]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf e_\rho\\ \mathbf e_\varphi\\ \mathbf k_E\end{pmatrix},\qquad [R]=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0\\ -\sin\lambda&0&\cos\lambda\\ \cos\lambda&0&\sin\lambda\end{pmatrix}$$

Checks: the rows have unit length ($1$, $\sin^2\lambda+\cos^2\lambda$, $\cos^2\lambda+\sin^2\lambda$) and are mutually orthogonal (row 1 with rows 2 and 3: $0$; rows 2 and 3: $-\sin\lambda\cos\lambda+\cos\lambda\sin\lambda=0$), so $[R][R]^T=I$; expanding along the first row, $\det[R]=-1\cdot\big((-\sin\lambda)\sin\lambda-\cos\lambda\cos\lambda\big)=+1$. The third column of $[R]$ gives back $\mathbf k_E=\cos\lambda\,\mathbf j+\sin\lambda\,\mathbf k$.

Step 4. Inertia forces (Notes §3.2 and §4.9)

The acceleration of $P$ in $S_0$ is $\mathbf a=\mathbf a_{rel}+\mathbf a_{drag}+2\boldsymbol\omega_{10}\times\mathbf v_{rel}$. The point of $S_1$ that coincides with $P$ moves on a circle about the polar axis (the centre $C$ is fixed and $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}$ constant), so $\mathbf a_{drag}=\boldsymbol\Omega\times\big(\boldsymbol\Omega\times\mathbf r_{CP}\big)$ with $\mathbf r_{CP}=\mathbf r_{CA}+\mathbf r$. In $S_1$, $m\mathbf a_{rel}=\mathbf F+\mathbf F_{cf}+\mathbf F_{cor}$ with

$$\mathbf F_{cf}=-m\boldsymbol\Omega\times(\boldsymbol\Omega\times\mathbf r_{CA})-m\boldsymbol\Omega\times(\boldsymbol\Omega\times\mathbf r),\qquad \mathbf F_{cor}=-2m\boldsymbol\Omega\times\mathbf v_{rel}$$

The first term is the same at every point of the tower: added to the gravitational attraction it gives the effective weight $-mg\,\mathbf k$ that a plumb line at $A$ measures (this is how $\mathbf k$ is defined). The second term has modulus at most $m\Omega^2h\approx5.3\times10^{-7}\,m$ m s$^{-2}$ for $h=100$ m: it is of order $\Omega^2$ and is dropped. The Coriolis force is of order $\Omega$ and is the one that matters.

Key results
  • 3 degrees of freedom $(x,y,z)$, no constraint during the fall.
  • $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\Omega\cos\lambda\,\mathbf j+\Omega\sin\lambda\,\mathbf k$, constant.
  • Inertia forces: centrifugal $-m\boldsymbol\Omega\times\big(\boldsymbol\Omega\times(\mathbf r_{CA}+\mathbf r)\big)$ (the part with $\mathbf r_{CA}$ is in $g$; the part with $\mathbf r$ is $O(\Omega^2h)$ and is neglected) and Coriolis $-2m\boldsymbol\Omega\times\mathbf v_{rel}$ (kept). No Euler force.

Part (b) · 2 points

Equations of motion in $S_1$

Write the vector equation of motion of $P$ in $S_1$ and its three scalar components, with the initial conditions.

Step 1. Vector equation

With the results of (a), the only forces in $S_1$ are the effective weight and the Coriolis force:

$$m\,\ddot{\mathbf r}=-mg\,\mathbf k-2m\,\boldsymbol\Omega\times\dot{\mathbf r}$$

Step 2. The cross product

$$\boldsymbol\Omega\times\dot{\mathbf r}=\begin{vmatrix}\mathbf i&\mathbf j&\mathbf k\\0&\Omega\cos\lambda&\Omega\sin\lambda\\\dot x&\dot y&\dot z\end{vmatrix}=\Omega\big(\dot z\cos\lambda-\dot y\sin\lambda\big)\mathbf i+\Omega\dot x\sin\lambda\,\mathbf j-\Omega\dot x\cos\lambda\,\mathbf k$$

Step 3. Components

Dividing by $m$ and projecting on $\mathbf i$, $\mathbf j$, $\mathbf k$:

$$\ddot x=-2\Omega\big(\dot z\cos\lambda-\dot y\sin\lambda\big),\qquad \ddot y=-2\Omega\dot x\sin\lambda,\qquad \ddot z=-g+2\Omega\dot x\cos\lambda$$

The particle starts at the top of the tower with zero velocity relative to the ground (in $S_0$ it moves east with the tower top): $\mathbf r(0)=h\,\mathbf k$, $\dot{\mathbf r}(0)=\mathbf 0$.

Key results
  • $\ddot x=-2\Omega\big(\dot z\cos\lambda-\dot y\sin\lambda\big)$, $\ \ddot y=-2\Omega\dot x\sin\lambda$, $\ \ddot z=-g+2\Omega\dot x\cos\lambda$.
  • $x(0)=y(0)=0$, $z(0)=h$, $\dot x(0)=\dot y(0)=\dot z(0)=0$.

Part (c) · 2 points

Solution to first order in $\Omega$

Solve the equations to first order in $\Omega$ and show that the particle lands at $\Delta x=\dfrac{2\sqrt2}{3}\,\Omega\cos\lambda\sqrt{\dfrac{h^3}{g}}$ east of the foot of the tower. Does the time of fall change at this order?

Step 1. Zeroth order

With $\Omega=0$: $\ddot x_0=\ddot y_0=0$ and $\ddot z_0=-g$, with $\dot{\mathbf r}_0(0)=\mathbf 0$ and $\mathbf r_0(0)=h\,\mathbf k$. Integrating twice with the limits $0$ and $t$ (antiderivative, then Barrow's rule $[F]_0^t=F(t)-F(0)$):

$$\dot z_0(t)=\dot z_0(0)+\int_0^t(-g)\,d\tau=0+\big[-g\tau\big]_0^t=-gt-(-g\cdot0)=-gt$$
$$z_0(t)=z_0(0)+\int_0^t(-g\tau)\,d\tau=h+\Big[-\tfrac12g\tau^2\Big]_0^t=h-\tfrac12gt^2$$
$$\dot x_0(t)=\dot x_0(0)+\int_0^t0\,d\tau=0,\qquad x_0(t)=x_0(0)+\int_0^t0\,d\tau=0$$

and in the same way $\dot y_0=0$, $y_0=0$.

Step 2. First order

Every Coriolis term carries a factor $\Omega$, so to first order the velocity inside it is the zeroth-order one, $\dot{\mathbf r}_0=-gt\,\mathbf k$:

$$\ddot x_1=-2\Omega\big(\dot z_0\cos\lambda-\dot y_0\sin\lambda\big)=2\Omega gt\cos\lambda,\qquad \ddot y_1=-2\Omega\dot x_0\sin\lambda=0,\qquad \ddot z_1=2\Omega\dot x_0\cos\lambda=0$$

with zero initial conditions, so $y_1=z_1=0$ and

$$\dot x_1=\int_0^t2\Omega g\cos\lambda\,\tau\,d\tau=\big[\Omega g\cos\lambda\,\tau^2\big]_0^t=\Omega gt^2\cos\lambda$$
$$x_1=\int_0^t\Omega g\cos\lambda\,\tau^2\,d\tau=\Big[\tfrac13\Omega g\cos\lambda\,\tau^3\Big]_0^t=\tfrac13\Omega gt^3\cos\lambda$$

Step 3. Landing point

Since $z_1=0$, the height is $h-\tfrac12gt^2$ up to $O(\Omega^2)$ and the particle reaches $z=0$ at $t_f=\sqrt{2h/g}$, as without rotation. Then $t_f^3=(2h/g)^{3/2}=2\sqrt2\,h^{3/2}/g^{3/2}$ and

$$\Delta x=x_1(t_f)=\tfrac13\Omega g\cos\lambda\cdot\frac{2\sqrt2\,h^{3/2}}{g^{3/2}}=\frac{2\sqrt2}{3}\,\Omega\cos\lambda\sqrt{\frac{h^3}{g}}>0$$

The particle lands east of the foot $A$.

Step 4. Physical reading in $S_0$

At release the top of the tower is a distance $h\cos\lambda$ farther from the polar axis than the foot, so in $S_0$ it moves east $\Omega h\cos\lambda$ faster than the foot. The particle keeps that eastward speed while it falls, which is why it ends up ahead of the foot. But this lead speed $\Omega h\cos\lambda$ is measured against the foot, not against the rotating ground under the particle: relative to the ground ($S_1$) the particle starts at rest, and its eastward speed $\dot x_1=\Omega gt^2\cos\lambda$ is zero at release and grows like $t^2$, as the particle descends to points of the ground that move more slowly. The crude estimate "lead speed $\Omega h\cos\lambda$ during the whole fall", $\Omega h\cos\lambda\,t_f$, assumes the full lead from the start and is therefore $3/2$ of the exact first-order value: with $h=\tfrac12gt_f^2$, $\Omega h\cos\lambda\,t_f=\tfrac12\Omega g\cos\lambda\,t_f^3$, against $\Delta x=\tfrac13\Omega g\cos\lambda\,t_f^3$.

Key results
  • Order 0: $z_0=h-\tfrac12gt^2$, $\dot z_0=-gt$, $x_0=y_0=0$.
  • Order 1: $\ddot x_1=2\Omega g t\cos\lambda$, $\ \dot x_1=\Omega gt^2\cos\lambda$, $\ x_1=\tfrac13\Omega gt^3\cos\lambda$; $y_1=z_1=0$.
  • $t_f=\sqrt{2h/g}$ (unchanged at first order), $\Delta x=\tfrac{2\sqrt2}{3}\Omega\cos\lambda\sqrt{h^3/g}$, to the east.

Part (d) · 1 point

Numbers

For $h=100$ m, $\lambda=40^\circ$: compute the time of fall and the eastward deflection. Where is the particle (east of the plumb line) at $t_f/2$?

Step 1. Time and deflection

$t_f=\sqrt{2\cdot100/9.81}=\sqrt{20.39}=4.515$ s. With $\cos40^\circ=0.7660$ and $\sqrt{h^3/g}=\sqrt{10^6/9.81}=319.3$ s:

$$\Delta x=\frac{2\sqrt2}{3}\,(7.292\times10^{-5})(0.7660)(319.3)\ \text{m}=1.681\times10^{-2}\ \text{m}=1.681\ \text{cm}$$

A numerical integration of the exact equations of (b) (no expansion) gives the same value to four significant figures.

Step 2. Half-way

$x_1\propto t^3$, so at $t_f/2$ the deflection is $(1/2)^3\Delta x=0.210$ cm: most of the deflection builds up in the second half of the fall.

Key results
  • $t_f=\sqrt{200/9.81}=4.515$ s.
  • $\Delta x=1.681$ cm east; $x_1(t_f/2)=\Delta x/8=0.210$ cm.

Part (e) · 1.5 points

Is there a north-south deflection?

A student integrates the $y$ equation once more and reports a southward deflection $y_2(t_f)=-\tfrac23\Omega^2h^2\sin\lambda\cos\lambda/g$. Check the formula, and say whether it is a consistent result of the model of the statement.

Step 1. The second-order term

At second order the $y$ equation receives $\dot x_1$: $\ddot y_2=-2\Omega\dot x_1\sin\lambda=-2\Omega^2gt^2\sin\lambda\cos\lambda$. Integrating twice from rest:

$$\dot y_2(t)=\int_0^t\big(-2\Omega^2g\sin\lambda\cos\lambda\,\tau^2\big)\,d\tau=\Big[-\tfrac23\Omega^2g\sin\lambda\cos\lambda\,\tau^3\Big]_0^t=-\tfrac23\Omega^2gt^3\sin\lambda\cos\lambda$$
$$y_2(t)=\int_0^t\Big(-\tfrac23\Omega^2g\sin\lambda\cos\lambda\,\tau^3\Big)\,d\tau=\Big[-\tfrac16\Omega^2g\sin\lambda\cos\lambda\,\tau^4\Big]_0^t=-\tfrac16\Omega^2gt^4\sin\lambda\cos\lambda$$

(the lower limit contributes $0$ in both, since $\dot y_2(0)=y_2(0)=0$).

and with $t_f^4=4h^2/g^2$: $y_2(t_f)=-\tfrac23\Omega^2h^2\sin\lambda\cos\lambda/g$. With the data: $-1.78\times10^{-6}$ m, about $10^{-4}$ of $\Delta x$ (the ratio is $\tfrac12\Omega t_f\sin\lambda$).

Step 2. Why it is not a consistent answer

The equation of (b) is only exact to first order in $\Omega$: in (a) we dropped the residual centrifugal force $-m\boldsymbol\Omega\times(\boldsymbol\Omega\times\mathbf r)$, which is $O(\Omega^2)$. For $\mathbf r\approx z\,\mathbf k$ its $y$ component is $-m\Omega^2z\sin\lambda\cos\lambda$; with $z=z_0(t)$ it alone displaces the particle by

$$y_{cf}(t_f)=-\Omega^2\sin\lambda\cos\lambda\int_0^{t_f}\!\!\int_0^{\tau}z_0(s)\,ds\,d\tau=-\tfrac56\,\frac{\Omega^2h^2\sin\lambda\cos\lambda}{g}=-2.22\times10^{-6}\ \text{m}$$

which is larger than $y_2$. The direction of the gravitational attraction also changes along the vertical through $A$ by an amount of the same order. A second-order answer must collect all these terms; keeping only the Coriolis one is inconsistent. To first order in $\Omega$, the only deflection is the eastward one, and $y=O(\Omega^2)$ is at the micrometre level.

Key results
  • $\ddot y_2=-2\Omega\dot x_1\sin\lambda=-2\Omega^2gt^2\sin\lambda\cos\lambda$, $\ y_2=-\tfrac16\Omega^2gt^4\sin\lambda\cos\lambda$; at $t_f$: $-\tfrac23\Omega^2h^2\sin\lambda\cos\lambda/g=-1.78\times10^{-6}$ m.
  • The formula is right but not consistent: it is $O(\Omega^2)$, the same order as the residual centrifugal term $-m\boldsymbol\Omega\times(\boldsymbol\Omega\times\mathbf r)$ dropped in (a), whose own effect is $-\tfrac56\Omega^2h^2\sin\lambda\cos\lambda/g=-2.22\times10^{-6}$ m, and as the change of direction of gravitation along the fall. To first order in $\Omega$ there is no north-south deflection.

Part (f) · 2 points

A projectile fired vertically upward

From $A$ a projectile is fired vertically upward (along $\mathbf k$) with speed $v_0$ relative to the ground. To first order in $\Omega$, where does it land? Compute it for $v_0=50$ m s$^{-1}$ at $\lambda=40^\circ$, and compare with the drop of (c) from the same maximum height.

Step 1. Zeroth and first order

Order 0: $z_0=v_0t-\tfrac12gt^2$, $\dot z_0=v_0-gt$, back at $z=0$ at $T=2v_0/g$. Order 1, from the $x$ equation of (b) with $\dot y_0=0$:

$$\ddot x_1=-2\Omega\cos\lambda\,(v_0-gt)$$
$$\dot x_1=\int_0^t-2\Omega\cos\lambda\,(v_0-g\tau)\,d\tau=-2\Omega\cos\lambda\Big[v_0\tau-\tfrac12g\tau^2\Big]_0^t=-\Omega g\cos\lambda\;t\Big(\frac{2v_0}{g}-t\Big)$$
$$x_1=\int_0^t-2\Omega\cos\lambda\Big(v_0\tau-\tfrac12g\tau^2\Big)d\tau=-2\Omega\cos\lambda\Big[\tfrac12v_0\tau^2-\tfrac16g\tau^3\Big]_0^t=-\Omega\cos\lambda\Big(v_0t^2-\tfrac13gt^3\Big)$$

Step 2. Landing point

At $T=2v_0/g$: $v_0T^2=4v_0^3/g^2$ and $\tfrac13gT^3=\tfrac83v_0^3/g^2$, so

$$\Delta x=x_1(T)=-\Omega\cos\lambda\Big(4-\tfrac83\Big)\frac{v_0^3}{g^2}=-\frac43\,\Omega\cos\lambda\,\frac{v_0^3}{g^2}<0$$

The projectile lands west of the launch point. With $v_0=50$ m s$^{-1}$: $\Delta x=-\tfrac43(7.292\times10^{-5})(0.7660)(125\,000)/96.24$ m $=-9.674$ cm; the flight lasts $T=10.19$ s and the apex is at $v_0^2/2g=127.4$ m.

Step 3. Why it does not cancel

On the way up ($\dot z_0>0$) the Coriolis force pushes west; on the way down it pushes east. The symmetric-looking guess "the two halves cancel" confuses force with displacement: $\dot x_1=-\Omega g\cos\lambda\,t(T-t)$ is negative during the whole flight and only returns to zero at $t=T$. The westward velocity gained on the way up is removed on the way down, but the westward displacement accumulated meanwhile remains.

Step 4. Comparison with the drop

Setting $v_0=\sqrt{2gh}$ (apex at height $h$): $\Delta x_{up}=-\tfrac43\Omega\cos\lambda(2gh)^{3/2}/g^2=-4\cdot\tfrac{2\sqrt2}{3}\Omega\cos\lambda\sqrt{h^3/g}$, that is, $-4$ times the eastward deflection of (c): $-6.73$ cm for $h=100$ m. The descent of the projectile alone would push it $1.68$ cm east, but it starts the descent already moving west.

Key results
  • $\ddot x_1=-2\Omega\cos\lambda\,(v_0-gt)$, $\ \dot x_1=-\Omega g\cos\lambda\,t\,(T-t)\le0$, $\ x_1=-\Omega\cos\lambda\big(v_0t^2-\tfrac13gt^3\big)$, with $T=2v_0/g$.
  • $\Delta x=-\tfrac43\,\Omega\cos\lambda\,v_0^3/g^2$: it lands west of $A$; $-9.674$ cm for $v_0=50$ m s$^{-1}$ ($T=10.19$ s, apex $127.4$ m).
  • With $v_0=\sqrt{2gh}$ the westward offset is $4$ times the eastward deflection of the drop from $h$.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $[\Omega gt^3]=\text{s}^{-1}\cdot\text{m s}^{-2}\cdot\text{s}^3=\text{m}$; $[\Omega\sqrt{h^3/g}]=\text{s}^{-1}\sqrt{\text{m}^3/(\text{m s}^{-2})}=\text{s}^{-1}\cdot\text{m s}=\text{m}$; $[\Omega v_0^3/g^2]=\text{s}^{-1}\,\text{m}^3\text{s}^{-3}/(\text{m}^2\text{s}^{-4})=\text{m}$.
  • Limits: at the poles ($\lambda=90^\circ$) $\cos\lambda=0$: $\boldsymbol\Omega$ is vertical, parallel to the fall, and there is no deflection; it is largest at the equator. In the southern hemisphere ($\lambda<0$) $\cos\lambda>0$ still: the drop also lands east. The expansion requires $\Omega t_f\ll1$ (here $3.3\times10^{-4}$).
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) writing $\boldsymbol\Omega=\Omega\,\mathbf k$ (the local vertical) instead of $\Omega(\cos\lambda\,\mathbf j+\sin\lambda\,\mathbf k)$; (2) a sign error in the Coriolis force (it is $-2m\boldsymbol\Omega\times\mathbf v_{rel}$) that sends the particle west; (3) keeping $O(\Omega^2)$ Coriolis terms while dropping the centrifugal term of the same order; (4) assuming that the up and down halves of a vertical shot cancel.
Problem · Unit 3 · 10 points · 60 min · High difficulty / trap

P11 — Pendulum whose string is hauled through a ring: a rheonomic constraint, angular momentum and the work of the hauling force

A bob $P$ of mass $m$ hangs from a light inextensible string that passes through a small, smooth ring $O$ fixed to the ceiling. The other end of the string leaves the ring and is hauled in so that the length between $O$ and $P$ is

$$\ell(t)=\ell_0-u\,t,\qquad u>0\ \text{constant}$$

The motion is planar, in the vertical plane of the inertial frame $S_0=\{O;\mathbf i,\mathbf j,\mathbf k\}$ ($x$ horizontal, $y$ vertical upward, uniform gravity $-g\,\mathbf j$, $\mathbf k=\mathbf i\times\mathbf j$ out of the page). The angle $\theta$ is measured from the downward vertical, positive towards $+x$, and the string stays taut. Use the unit vectors $\mathbf e_r=\sin\theta\,\mathbf i-\cos\theta\,\mathbf j$ (from $O$ to $P$) and $\mathbf e_\theta=\cos\theta\,\mathbf i+\sin\theta\,\mathbf j$.

Figure 1. Pendulum hauled through a fixed smooth ring $O$: length $\ell(t)=\ell_0-ut$ between $O$ and the bob $P$, angle $\theta$ from the downward vertical (positive towards $+x$), unit vectors $\mathbf e_r$ (from $O$ to $P$) and $\mathbf e_\theta$; the free end is pulled with constant speed $u$.

Setup

  • Data: $m$, $g$, $\ell_0$, $u$. Numerical parts: (d) and (e) $m=0.5$ kg, $\ell_0=2$ m, $u=0.5$ m s$^{-1}$, $g=0$, initial transverse speed $\ell_0\dot\theta_0=1$ m s$^{-1}$; (f) $\ell_0=1$ m, $u=0.01$ m s$^{-1}$, $g=9.81$ m s$^{-2}$, $\theta(0)=0.05$ rad, $\dot\theta(0)=0$.
  • Hypotheses: point particle, massless inextensible string, smooth ring (the tension $T$ is the same on both sides), no air drag, $S_0$ inertial, $\ell>0$.

Part (a) · 1 point

Degrees of freedom and the constraint

How many degrees of freedom has $P$? Classify the constraint imposed by the string.

Step 1. Count

In the vertical plane a free particle has 2 degrees of freedom. The taut string between $O$ and $P$ imposes

$$f(\mathbf r,t)=|\mathbf r|-(\ell_0-ut)=0$$

one equation, so 1 degree of freedom remains: $\theta$. The length is prescribed by whoever pulls the string, so it is not a second coordinate (Notes §2.7).

Step 2. Classification

The constraint involves only positions and time: it is holonomic. Time appears explicitly, so it is rheonomic: the constraint moves, and its reaction (the tension) can do work on $P$, unlike the fixed string of the ordinary pendulum. A string can only pull, so the constraint is unilateral and holds while $T\ge0$.

Key results
  • $|\overrightarrow{OP}|-\ell_0+ut=0$: holonomic, rheonomic (depends on $t$), and unilateral ($T\ge0$).
  • $2-1=1$ degree of freedom, coordinate $\theta(t)$; $\ell(t)$ is a given function, not an unknown.

Part (b) · 1.5 points

Kinematics in polar coordinates
