Write the position, velocity and acceleration of $P$ in $\{\mathbf e_r,\mathbf e_\theta\}$, first for a general length $\ell(t)$ and then for $\ell=\ell_0-ut$.

Step 1. Change of basis and unit vectors

The working basis $\{\mathbf e_r,\mathbf e_\theta,\mathbf k\}$ turns with $P$; its relation to the inertial basis is

$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf e_r\\ \mathbf e_\theta\\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix}=[R]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf i\\ \mathbf j\\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix},\qquad [R]=\begin{pmatrix}\sin\theta&-\cos\theta&0\\ \cos\theta&\sin\theta&0\\ 0&0&1\end{pmatrix}$$

Checks: rows 1 and 2 have length $\sin^2\theta+\cos^2\theta=1$ and $\sin\theta\cos\theta-\cos\theta\sin\theta=0$, row 3 is $\mathbf k$, so $[R][R]^T=I$; $\det[R]=\sin\theta\cdot\sin\theta-(-\cos\theta)\cos\theta=+1$, so the basis is right-handed ($\mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_\theta=\mathbf k$). By the chain rule, $\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dfrac{d\mathbf e_r}{d\theta}\dot\theta=(\cos\theta\,\mathbf i+\sin\theta\,\mathbf j)\dot\theta=\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$ and $\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta=(-\sin\theta\,\mathbf i+\cos\theta\,\mathbf j)\dot\theta=-\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_r$.

Step 2. Position and velocity

$\mathbf r=\ell\,\mathbf e_r$. Product rule:

$$\mathbf v=\dot\ell\,\mathbf e_r+\ell\,\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot\ell\,\mathbf e_r+\ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$$

Step 3. Acceleration

Differentiating each product:

$$\mathbf a=\ddot\ell\,\mathbf e_r+\dot\ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta+\big(\dot\ell\dot\theta+\ell\ddot\theta\big)\mathbf e_\theta+\ell\dot\theta\,\dot{\mathbf e}_\theta=\big(\ddot\ell-\ell\dot\theta^2\big)\mathbf e_r+\big(\ell\ddot\theta+2\dot\ell\dot\theta\big)\mathbf e_\theta$$

Step 4. Prescribed length

$\ell=\ell_0-ut$ gives $\dot\ell=-u$, $\ddot\ell=0$:

$$\mathbf v=-u\,\mathbf e_r+\ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta,\qquad \mathbf a=-\ell\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_r+\big(\ell\ddot\theta-2u\dot\theta\big)\mathbf e_\theta$$
Key results
  • $\mathbf v=\dot\ell\,\mathbf e_r+\ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$, $\ \mathbf a=(\ddot\ell-\ell\dot\theta^2)\,\mathbf e_r+(\ell\ddot\theta+2\dot\ell\dot\theta)\,\mathbf e_\theta$.
  • With $\dot\ell=-u$, $\ddot\ell=0$: $\mathbf v=-u\,\mathbf e_r+\ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$, $\ \mathbf a=-\ell\dot\theta^2\,\mathbf e_r+(\ell\ddot\theta-2u\dot\theta)\,\mathbf e_\theta$.

Part (c) · 2 points

Forces and equations of motion

Write the forces on $P$ and obtain the equation of motion free of the tension and the tension itself.

Step 1. Forces

Weight: $\mathbf W=-mg\,\mathbf j$ with $\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf e_r=-\cos\theta$ and $\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf e_\theta=\sin\theta$, so $\mathbf W=mg\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_r-mg\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta$. Tension: the string pulls $P$ towards the ring, $\mathbf T=-T\,\mathbf e_r$, $T\ge0$.

Step 2. Transverse projection

$$m\big(\ell\ddot\theta+2\dot\ell\dot\theta\big)=-mg\sin\theta\ \Longrightarrow\ \ell\ddot\theta+2\dot\ell\dot\theta+g\sin\theta=0$$

With $\dot\ell=-u$: $(\ell_0-ut)\ddot\theta-2u\dot\theta+g\sin\theta=0$. The term $-2u\dot\theta$ has the form of a negative damping: hauling the string in feeds the swing.

Step 3. Radial projection

$$m\big(\ddot\ell-\ell\dot\theta^2\big)=mg\cos\theta-T\ \Longrightarrow\ T=m\big(g\cos\theta+\ell\dot\theta^2-\ddot\ell\big)=m\big(g\cos\theta+\ell\dot\theta^2\big)$$

because $\ddot\ell=0$: a constant hauling speed adds no radial inertia term. The equation of Step 2 is also what a Lagrange equation (extension, not in the course notes) with $L=\tfrac12m(\dot\ell^2+\ell^2\dot\theta^2)+mg\ell\cos\theta$ gives.

Key results
  • $\mathbf W=mg(\cos\theta\,\mathbf e_r-\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta)$, $\ \mathbf T=-T\,\mathbf e_r$.
  • $\ell\ddot\theta+2\dot\ell\dot\theta+g\sin\theta=0$, i.e. $(\ell_0-ut)\ddot\theta-2u\dot\theta+g\sin\theta=0$.
  • $T=m\big(g\cos\theta+\ell\dot\theta^2-\ddot\ell\big)=m\big(g\cos\theta+\ell\dot\theta^2\big)$.

Part (d) · 1.5 points

Angular momentum about $O$ and the gravity-free case

Compute $\mathbf H_O$ and $d\mathbf H_O/dt$. Is it conserved? Then remove gravity (a tether in orbit reeled in at constant speed, $g=0$): find $T(\ell)$ and, for the data of the Setup, $\dot\theta$, the speed and $T$ when $\ell=0.5$ m.

Step 1. Angular momentum

$\mathbf H_O=\mathbf r\times m\mathbf v=\ell\,\mathbf e_r\times m\big(\dot\ell\,\mathbf e_r+\ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta\big)=m\ell^2\dot\theta\,(\mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_\theta)$, and $\mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_\theta=\mathbf k$ (it is the cross product of $\mathbf i$ and $\mathbf j$ rotated by $\theta-90^\circ$):

$$\mathbf H_O=m\ell^2\dot\theta\,\mathbf k$$

Step 2. Its rate (Notes §4.8)

The tension passes through $O$, so its moment is zero; the weight gives $\mathbf r\times\mathbf W=\ell\,\mathbf e_r\times(-mg\sin\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta)=-mg\ell\sin\theta\,\mathbf k$. Product rule:

$$\frac{d}{dt}\big(m\ell^2\dot\theta\big)=m\big(2\ell\dot\ell\dot\theta+\ell^2\ddot\theta\big)=m\ell\big(\ell\ddot\theta+2\dot\ell\dot\theta\big)=-mg\ell\sin\theta$$

which is the equation of (c) multiplied by $m\ell$. With gravity $\mathbf H_O$ is not conserved.

Step 3. Gravity-free case

With $g=0$ the right-hand side vanishes: $\ell^2\dot\theta=h$ constant. In the tension, $\dot\theta=h/\ell^2$:

$$T=m\ell\dot\theta^2=m\ell\frac{h^2}{\ell^4}=\frac{mh^2}{\ell^3}$$

The transverse speed $\ell\dot\theta=h/\ell$ grows as the string shortens, and the tension grows like $\ell^{-3}$: whoever reels the tether in must pull harder and harder.

Step 4. Numbers

$h=\ell_0\cdot(\ell_0\dot\theta_0)=2\cdot1=2$ m$^2$ s$^{-1}$. $\ell=0.5$ m is reached at $t=(2-0.5)/0.5=3$ s. Then $\dot\theta=2/0.25=8$ rad s$^{-1}$, $\ell\dot\theta=4$ m s$^{-1}$, $|\mathbf v|=\sqrt{u^2+(\ell\dot\theta)^2}=\sqrt{0.25+16}=4.031$ m s$^{-1}$ and $T=0.5\cdot4/0.125=16$ N, compared with $T=0.5\cdot4/8=0.25$ N at the start.

Key results
  • $\mathbf H_O=m\ell^2\dot\theta\,\mathbf k$; $\ \dfrac{d\mathbf H_O}{dt}=\mathbf r\times\mathbf W=-mg\ell\sin\theta\,\mathbf k$: not conserved.
  • $g=0$: $\ell^2\dot\theta=h$, $T=m\ell\dot\theta^2=mh^2/\ell^3$, which grows without bound as $\ell\to0$.
  • Data: $h=2$ m$^2$ s$^{-1}$; at $\ell=0.5$ m ($t=3$ s): $\dot\theta=8$ rad s$^{-1}$, $\ell\dot\theta=4$ m s$^{-1}$, $|\mathbf v|=4.031$ m s$^{-1}$, $T=16$ N (it was $0.25$ N at $\ell_0$).

Part (e) · 2 points

Who supplies the kinetic energy?

Show that $\dfrac{d}{dt}\big(E_{kin}+V\big)=T\,u$ and explain why this is the power of the hauling force. For $g=0$ and the data of (d), compute the work done by the hauling force between $\ell_0=2$ m and $\ell=0.5$ m and check it against the change in kinetic energy.

Step 1. Power of the tension

$\mathbf T\cdot\mathbf v=-T\,\mathbf e_r\cdot\big(\dot\ell\,\mathbf e_r+\ell\dot\theta\,\mathbf e_\theta\big)=-T\dot\ell=Tu$. On a fixed string ($\dot\ell=0$) the tension is orthogonal to the velocity and does no work; here the constraint moves and it does.

Step 2. Energy balance (Notes §4.4)

The work-energy theorem gives $\dfrac{dE_{kin}}{dt}=\mathbf W\cdot\mathbf v+\mathbf T\cdot\mathbf v$, and the weight derives from $V=mgy_P=-mg\ell\cos\theta$, so $\mathbf W\cdot\mathbf v=-dV/dt$. Hence

$$\frac{d}{dt}\Big(\tfrac12m\big(\dot\ell^2+\ell^2\dot\theta^2\big)-mg\ell\cos\theta\Big)=Tu$$

The string is massless and the ring smooth, so the tension has the same value $T$ at the free end, which moves with speed $u$ along the pull: the hauler's power is $Tu$. The mechanical energy of $P$ is not a first integral. Integrating the balance from $0$ to $t$ (Barrow's rule) gives instead

$$E_{kin}(t)+V(t)-W_{haul}(t)=E_{kin}(0)+V(0),\qquad W_{haul}(t)=\int_0^tT\,u\,dt'$$

that is, the energy of $P$ grows by exactly the work done by the hauler. This is an energy balance, not a first integral in the strict sense: $W_{haul}(t)$ depends on the whole history $T(t')$, not on the state $(\theta,\dot\theta,t)$ alone.

Step 3. Work for $g=0$

With $T=mh^2/\ell^3$, change the variable to $\ell'=\ell_0-ut'$: $d\ell'=-u\,dt'$, so $dt'=-d\ell'/u$, and the limits become $t'=0\mapsto\ell'=\ell_0$, $t'=t\mapsto\ell'=\ell$:

$$W_{haul}=\int_0^{t}Tu\,dt'=\int_{\ell_0}^{\ell}\frac{mh^2}{\ell'^3}\,u\,\Big(-\frac{d\ell'}{u}\Big)=\int_{\ell}^{\ell_0}\frac{mh^2}{\ell'^3}\,d\ell'$$

An antiderivative of $mh^2\ell'^{-3}$ is $F(\ell')=-\dfrac{mh^2}{2\ell'^2}$ (check: $F'=-\tfrac{mh^2}{2}(-2)\ell'^{-3}=mh^2\ell'^{-3}$). Barrow's rule:

$$W_{haul}=F(\ell_0)-F(\ell)=-\frac{mh^2}{2\ell_0^2}-\Big(-\frac{mh^2}{2\ell^2}\Big)=\frac{mh^2}{2}\Big(\frac1{\ell^2}-\frac1{\ell_0^2}\Big)$$

With the data: $\tfrac{0.5\cdot4}{2}\big(4-0.25\big)=3.75$ J.

Step 4. Check

$E_{kin}=\tfrac12m\big(u^2+h^2/\ell^2\big)$: from $\tfrac12\cdot0.5\,(0.25+1)$ to $\tfrac12\cdot0.5\,(0.25+16)$, a change of $\tfrac12\cdot0.5\cdot15=3.75$ J. The radial part $u^2$ is the same at both ends, so all the work goes into the transverse motion.

Key results
  • $\mathbf T\cdot\mathbf v=-T\,\dot\ell=T\,u>0$: the tension does positive work although it is "a constraint force".
  • $W_{haul}=\dfrac{mh^2}{2}\Big(\dfrac1{\ell^2}-\dfrac1{\ell_0^2}\Big)=3.75$ J $=\Delta E_{kin}$.

Part (f) · 2 points

Extension: the amplitude of small swings while hauling

Extension

This part goes beyond the course (adiabatic invariants and Bessel functions are not in the Notes). It is included because the result is a classic trap: neither the energy nor the angular momentum is conserved, so neither gives the amplitude.

For small angles, rewrite the equation of (c) with $\ell$ as the independent variable. Show that, when the hauling is slow ($u\ll\sqrt{g\ell}$), the angular amplitude grows like $\ell^{-3/4}$, and check it with the data of the Setup between $\ell_0=1$ m and $\ell=0.25$ m. Grading: the 2.0 points are for the change of variable (chain rule) and the reduced equation in $\ell$; the solution with Bessel functions and the $\ell^{-3/4}$ law are an extension, shown for completeness and not expected in the exam.

Step 1. Change of variable

For $\theta(t)=\Theta(\ell(t))$, the chain rule gives $\dot\theta=\Theta'\dot\ell=-u\Theta'$ and $\ddot\theta=\dfrac{d}{dt}(-u\Theta')=-u\Theta''\dot\ell=u^2\Theta''$. With $\sin\theta\approx\theta$ the equation of (c) becomes

$$\ell u^2\Theta''+2(-u)(-u\Theta')+g\Theta=0\ \Longrightarrow\ \ell\,\Theta''+2\Theta'+\frac{g}{u^2}\Theta=0$$

and since $\dfrac{d}{d\ell}\big(\ell^2\Theta'\big)=\ell^2\Theta''+2\ell\Theta'$, it is $\dfrac1\ell\big(\ell^2\Theta'\big)'+\dfrac{g}{u^2}\Theta=0$.

Step 2. Exact solution

With $x=g\ell/u^2$ the equation is $x\,y''+2y'+y=0$, whose solutions are $x^{-1/2}J_1(2\sqrt x)$ and $x^{-1/2}Y_1(2\sqrt x)$ (Bessel functions of order 1). So

$$\theta=\ell^{-1/2}\Big[A\,J_1\Big(\frac{2\sqrt{g\ell}}{u}\Big)+B\,Y_1\Big(\frac{2\sqrt{g\ell}}{u}\Big)\Big]$$

Step 3. Slow hauling

For large argument $J_1(z)\approx\sqrt{2/(\pi z)}\cos(z-3\pi/4)$ (and $Y_1$ with a sine). With $z\propto\ell^{1/2}$ the envelope is $\ell^{-1/2}z^{-1/2}\propto\ell^{-3/4}$:

$$\theta_a\propto\ell^{-3/4}$$

The condition $z\gg1$ is $u\ll\sqrt{g\ell}$: the length changes little during one swing. The same law follows from the adiabatic invariant $E_{osc}/\omega$ with $E_{osc}\approx\tfrac12mg\ell\theta_a^2$ and $\omega=\sqrt{g/\ell}$: $\ell^{3/2}\theta_a^2$ stays constant.

Step 4. Numbers

At $\ell=0.25$ m, $z=2\sqrt{9.81\cdot0.25}/0.01=313\gg1$ and $u/\sqrt{g\ell}=0.0064$. The law predicts $\theta_a=0.05\,(0.25)^{-3/4}=0.05\cdot2.828=0.1414$ rad: the swing angle almost triples, while the horizontal excursion $\ell\theta_a\propto\ell^{1/4}$ shrinks by the factor $0.707$. A Runge-Kutta integration of $(\ell_0-ut)\ddot\theta-2u\dot\theta+g\sin\theta=0$ gives $0.1401$ rad at the last turning point ($\ell=0.253$ m), where the law gives $0.1400$ rad.

Key results
  • Graded: $\dot\theta=-u\,\theta'$, $\ddot\theta=u^2\theta''$ (chain rule) and the reduced equation below. Everything after it (Bessel solution, $\ell^{-3/4}$ law, numbers) is an extension, not expected in the exam.
  • $\ell\,\theta''+2\theta'+\dfrac{g}{u^2}\,\theta=0$, i.e. $\dfrac1\ell\dfrac{d}{d\ell}\big(\ell^2\theta'\big)+\dfrac{g}{u^2}\theta=0$.
  • $\theta=\ell^{-1/2}\big[A\,J_1(z)+B\,Y_1(z)\big]$, $z=2\sqrt{g\ell}/u$; for $z\gg1$, $J_1,Y_1\sim\sqrt{2/(\pi z)}$ times a cosine, so the amplitude is $\propto\ell^{-1/2}\ell^{-1/4}=\ell^{-3/4}$.
  • From $1$ m to $0.25$ m: $\theta_a=0.05\cdot4^{3/4}=0.1414$ rad; a numerical integration of the full equation agrees within $0.2\,\%$.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: every term of $\ell\ddot\theta+2\dot\ell\dot\theta+g\sin\theta$ is m s$^{-2}$; $[mh^2/\ell^3]=\text{kg}\,\text{m}^4\text{s}^{-2}/\text{m}^3=$ N; $[Tu]=$ W; $g\ell/u^2$ is dimensionless.
  • Limits: $u=0$ gives the simple pendulum with $T=m(g\cos\theta+\ell\dot\theta^2)$ and energy conservation; $u<0$ (paying the string out) turns $-2u\dot\theta$ into a true damping and the amplitude decreases like $\ell^{-3/4}$.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) forgetting the Coriolis-like term $2\dot\ell\dot\theta$ when $\ell$ varies; (2) claiming that the tension does no work because "it is a constraint force" (it does, because the constraint is rheonomic); (3) writing energy conservation for $P$; (4) using $\ell^2\dot\theta=$ const with gravity (the weight has a moment about $O$); (5) believing that $T$ stays bounded in the gravity-free case.
Problem · Unit 3 · 10 points · 60 min · Standard exam level

P12 — Block on an accelerating turntable: the friction cone, the instant of slip and the direction in which the block starts to slide

A horizontal turntable rotates about the fixed vertical axis through its centre $O$. It starts from rest at $t=0$ with constant angular acceleration $\beta>0$, so its angular rate is $\Omega(t)=\beta t$ (counter-clockwise seen from above). A small block $P$ of mass $m$ rests on the table at the distance $r_0$ from the axis; at $t=0$ it lies on the fixed $x$ axis. The contact is rough, with static Coulomb coefficient $\mu$ and kinetic coefficient $\mu_k\le\mu$; gravity is $-g\,\mathbf k$.

Use the inertial frame $S_0=\{O;\mathbf i,\mathbf j,\mathbf k\}$ ($\mathbf k$ vertical, upward), the turntable frame $S_1$ (rotating with the table), the angle $\varphi$ of $\overrightarrow{OP}$ from the $x$ axis and the unit vectors $\mathbf e_r=\cos\varphi\,\mathbf i+\sin\varphi\,\mathbf j$, $\mathbf e_\varphi=\cos\varphi\,\mathbf j-\sin\varphi\,\mathbf i$.

Figure 1. Turntable seen from above: fixed axes $x,y$ and $\mathbf k$ (out of the page, upwards) at the axis $O$, rate $\Omega=\beta t$ and angular acceleration $\beta$ (counter-clockwise), block $P$ at distance $r_0$; while it sticks, the friction $\mathbf F$ has a component $F_r<0$ towards $O$ and a component $F_\varphi>0$ along $\mathbf e_\varphi$.

Setup

  • Data: $m$, $g$, $\mu$, $\mu_k$, $r_0$, $\beta$. Numbers: $\mu=0.4$, $\mu_k=0.3$, $r_0=0.5$ m, $\beta=0.2$ rad s$^{-2}$, $g=9.81$ m s$^{-2}$.
  • Hypotheses: point particle (the block does not tip), rigid horizontal table, Coulomb friction, no air drag.
  • In (e) and (f) the friction force on $P$ is written $\mathbf F=F_r\,\mathbf e_r+F_\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi$ and $\psi$ is its angle from the inward radius $-\mathbf e_r$, positive towards $+\mathbf e_\varphi$ (figure).

Part (a) · 1 point

Degrees of freedom and frames

How many degrees of freedom has $P$ while it sticks to the table, and while it slides on it? Give the angular velocity and the angular acceleration of $S_1$ relative to $S_0$.

Step 1. Count

A free particle has 3 degrees of freedom. While it touches the table, $z=0$ (bilateral as long as $N\ge0$): 2 remain, the polar coordinates $(r,\varphi)$. While it does not slip, it moves with the table: $r=r_0$ and $\varphi$ is the angle turned by the table, $\varphi=\int_0^t\beta\tau\,d\tau=\big[\tfrac12\beta\tau^2\big]_0^t=\tfrac12\beta t^2$. Nothing is left free: 0 degrees of freedom, and the friction force is an unknown reaction that adjusts to keep the block in place, as long as it can (Notes §6.6).

Step 2. Frames

$S_1$ turns about $\mathbf k$ with $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\Omega\,\mathbf k=\beta t\,\mathbf k$; its angular acceleration $\dot{\boldsymbol\omega}_{10}=\beta\,\mathbf k$ is not zero, so in $S_1$ the block feels an Euler force as well as the centrifugal and Coriolis forces.

Key results
  • Sliding: 2 degrees of freedom $(r,\varphi)$ on the plane $z=0$. Sticking: 0 degrees of freedom, $r=r_0$, $\varphi=\tfrac12\beta t^2$ prescribed.
  • $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\beta t\,\mathbf k$, $\dot{\boldsymbol\omega}_{10}=\beta\,\mathbf k$.

Part (b) · 1.5 points

Kinematics while the block sticks

Compute the velocity and acceleration of $P$ in $S_0$ in the basis $\{\mathbf e_r,\mathbf e_\varphi\}$.

Step 1. Change of basis and unit vectors

The polar basis $\{\mathbf e_r,\mathbf e_\varphi,\mathbf k\}$ turns with the table; its relation to the inertial basis is

$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf e_r\\ \mathbf e_\varphi\\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix}=[R]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf i\\ \mathbf j\\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix},\qquad [R]=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\varphi&\sin\varphi&0\\ -\sin\varphi&\cos\varphi&0\\ 0&0&1\end{pmatrix}$$

Checks: rows 1 and 2 have length $\cos^2\varphi+\sin^2\varphi=1$ and $-\cos\varphi\sin\varphi+\sin\varphi\cos\varphi=0$, row 3 is $\mathbf k$, so $[R][R]^T=I$; $\det[R]=\cos^2\varphi+\sin^2\varphi=+1$ (right-handed, $\mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_\varphi=\mathbf k$). Then $\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot\varphi\,(-\sin\varphi\,\mathbf i+\cos\varphi\,\mathbf j)=\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi$ and $\dot{\mathbf e}_\varphi=-\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_r$ (chain rule through $\varphi(t)$).

Step 2. Velocity and acceleration

$\mathbf r=r_0\,\mathbf e_r$ with $r_0$ constant:

$$\mathbf v=r_0\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi=r_0\Omega\,\mathbf e_\varphi$$
$$\mathbf a=r_0\ddot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi+r_0\dot\varphi\,\dot{\mathbf e}_\varphi=r_0\beta\,\mathbf e_\varphi-r_0\Omega^2\,\mathbf e_r$$

The general polar formula $\mathbf a=(\ddot r-r\dot\varphi^2)\mathbf e_r+(r\ddot\varphi+2\dot r\dot\varphi)\mathbf e_\varphi$ gives the same with $\dot r=\ddot r=0$.

Key results
  • $\mathbf v=r_0\Omega\,\mathbf e_\varphi$.
  • $\mathbf a=-r_0\Omega^2\,\mathbf e_r+r_0\beta\,\mathbf e_\varphi$: centripetal and tangential parts.

Part (c) · 2 points

Forces and the friction needed to stick

List the forces on $P$ and find the normal reaction and the friction force needed for the block to move with the table. Check the result in $S_1$.

Step 1. Forces

Weight $-mg\,\mathbf k$; normal reaction $N\,\mathbf k$ ($N\ge0$); friction $\mathbf F=F_r\,\mathbf e_r+F_\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi$ in the plane of contact, unknown while the block sticks.

Step 2. Newton in $S_0$

$m\mathbf a=-mg\,\mathbf k+N\,\mathbf k+\mathbf F$. Projecting:

$$\mathbf k:\ 0=N-mg\Rightarrow N=mg;\qquad \mathbf e_r:\ -mr_0\Omega^2=F_r;\qquad \mathbf e_\varphi:\ mr_0\beta=F_\varphi$$

Friction must pull inward (to bend the path) and forward (to speed the block up along with the table).

Step 3. Check in $S_1$ (Notes §4.9)

In $S_1$ the block is at rest, $\mathbf v_{rel}=\mathbf 0$, so the Coriolis force vanishes. Centrifugal: $-m\boldsymbol\omega\times(\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf r)=-m\Omega^2\,\mathbf k\times(\mathbf k\times r_0\mathbf e_r)=-m\Omega^2\,\mathbf k\times r_0\mathbf e_\varphi=mr_0\Omega^2\,\mathbf e_r$. Euler: $-m\dot{\boldsymbol\omega}\times\mathbf r=-m\beta\,\mathbf k\times r_0\mathbf e_r=-mr_0\beta\,\mathbf e_\varphi$. Balance in the plane: $\mathbf F+mr_0\Omega^2\mathbf e_r-mr_0\beta\,\mathbf e_\varphi=\mathbf 0$, the same $F_r$ and $F_\varphi$.

Key results
  • $N=mg$; $\ F_r=-mr_0\Omega^2=-mr_0\beta^2t^2$; $\ F_\varphi=mr_0\beta$.
  • $|\mathbf F|=mr_0\sqrt{\Omega^4+\beta^2}$, increasing in time.
  • In $S_1$: $\mathbf F+mr_0\Omega^2\,\mathbf e_r-mr_0\beta\,\mathbf e_\varphi=\mathbf 0$ (centrifugal and Euler forces, no Coriolis).

Part (d) · 2 points

When does the block slip?

Using the Coulomb condition, find the instant $t^*$ at which the block starts to slide. What condition must $\beta$ satisfy for the block to stick at all?

Step 1. The friction cone

Static friction holds while $|\mathbf F|\le\mu N$ (Notes §6.6). With (c):

$$m^2r_0^2\big(\Omega^4+\beta^2\big)\le\mu^2m^2g^2\ \Longleftrightarrow\ \Omega^4\le\Big(\frac{\mu g}{r_0}\Big)^2-\beta^2$$

Step 2. Slip instant

The left side grows with $t$ ($\Omega=\beta t$), so the inequality fails for the first time when it becomes an equality:

$$\Omega^*=\Big[\Big(\frac{\mu g}{r_0}\Big)^2-\beta^2\Big]^{1/4},\qquad t^*=\frac{\Omega^*}{\beta}$$

Step 3. Existence

A real $\Omega^*\ge0$ needs $\beta\le\mu g/r_0$. In the equality case $\beta=\mu g/r_0$ the formula gives $\Omega^*=0$, $t^*=0$: the friction is already at the limit of the cone at $t=0$ and the block slides as soon as $\Omega>0$, so it never sticks for a finite time. Sticking therefore requires the strict condition $\beta<\mu g/r_0$. If $\beta>\mu g/r_0$, already at $t=0$ (where $\Omega=0$) the tangential friction $mr_0\beta$ needed exceeds $\mu mg$: the table slides under the block from the start. Dropping $\beta^2$ in the cone (the usual shortcut $r_0\Omega^2=\mu g$) hides exactly this case.

Key results
  • $r_0^2\big(\Omega^4+\beta^2\big)\le\mu^2g^2$; slip when $\Omega^{*4}=\Big(\dfrac{\mu g}{r_0}\Big)^2-\beta^2$.
  • $t^*=\dfrac{\Omega^*}{\beta}=\dfrac1\beta\Big[\Big(\dfrac{\mu g}{r_0}\Big)^2-\beta^2\Big]^{1/4}$; requires $\beta<\mu g/r_0$ (at $\beta=\mu g/r_0$, $t^*=0$), otherwise it slips at $t=0$.

Part (e) · 1.5 points

Numbers and the direction of the friction at the slip instant

For the data of the Setup compute $\Omega^*$, $t^*$, the number of turns before slip and the speed of the block, and the angle $\psi$ of the friction force at $t^*$.

Step 1. Instant of slip

$\mu g/r_0=0.4\cdot9.81/0.5=7.848$ s$^{-2}$, so $(\mu g/r_0)^2=61.591$ s$^{-4}$ and $\Omega^{*4}=61.591-0.04=61.551$ s$^{-4}$. Then $\Omega^{*2}=7.8454$ s$^{-2}$, $\Omega^*=2.801$ rad s$^{-1}$ and $t^*=2.801/0.2=14.00$ s. Here $\beta^2/(\mu g/r_0)^2=6.5\times10^{-4}$: the tangential part barely changes $t^*$.

Step 2. Turns and speed

$\varphi^*=\tfrac12\beta t^{*2}=\Omega^{*2}/(2\beta)=7.8454/0.4=19.61$ rad, i.e. $19.61/2\pi=3.12$ turns. The speed is $r_0\Omega^*=1.400$ m s$^{-1}$, along $\mathbf e_\varphi$.

Step 3. Direction of the friction

At $t^*$: $|F_r|=mr_0\Omega^{*2}=3.923\,m$ N and $F_\varphi=mr_0\beta=0.1\,m$ N, so $\tan\psi=\beta/\Omega^{*2}=0.2/7.8454=0.0255$ and $\psi=1.460^\circ$. Check: $\cos\psi=r_0\Omega^{*2}/(\mu g)$, and $|\mathbf F|=\mu mg=3.924\,m$ N. The friction points to the axis, tilted $1.46^\circ$ forward.

Key results
  • $\mu g/r_0=7.848$ s$^{-2}$, $\Omega^{*4}=61.551$ s$^{-4}$, $\Omega^*=2.801$ rad s$^{-1}$, $t^*=14.00$ s.
  • $\varphi^*=\Omega^{*2}/2\beta=19.61$ rad $=3.12$ turns; $\ |\mathbf v|=r_0\Omega^*=1.400$ m s$^{-1}$.
  • $\tan\psi=\beta/\Omega^{*2}=0.0255$, $\psi=1.460^\circ$, inward and slightly forward.

Part (f) · 2 points

How does the block start to slide?

Just after $t^*$ the friction is kinetic, of modulus $\mu_kmg$. Find the initial acceleration of $P$ relative to the table, its modulus and its direction. Does the block leave along the radius? Describe its motion in $S_0$ at that instant.

Step 1. Inertia forces at $t^*$

In $S_1$, with $\mathbf v_{rel}=\mathbf 0$, the inertia forces are those of (c): $\mathbf F_{in}=mr_0\Omega^{*2}\,\mathbf e_r-mr_0\beta\,\mathbf e_\varphi$. By (c) and (d), $\mathbf F_{in}=-\mathbf F_{needed}$ and $|\mathbf F_{in}|=\mu mg$. It points outward and backward (against the rotation), at the angle $\psi$ from $+\mathbf e_r$.

Step 2. Direction of sliding

Once static friction fails, the block starts to move relative to the table along the unit vector $\hat{\mathbf u}$ of its relative acceleration, and kinetic friction opposes that motion: $\mathbf F_k=-\mu_kmg\,\hat{\mathbf u}$. In $S_1$, $m\mathbf a_{rel}=\mathbf F_{in}-\mu_kmg\,\hat{\mathbf u}$ must be parallel to $\hat{\mathbf u}$, which is possible only if $\hat{\mathbf u}$ is along $\mathbf F_{in}$:

$$\mathbf a_{rel}(t^{*+})=(\mu-\mu_k)\,g\;\frac{\mathbf F_{in}}{|\mathbf F_{in}|},\qquad |\mathbf a_{rel}|=(0.4-0.3)\cdot9.81=0.981\ \text{m s}^{-2}$$

Components: $0.981\cos\psi=0.9807$ m s$^{-2}$ outward and $-0.981\sin\psi=-0.0250$ m s$^{-2}$ along $\mathbf e_\varphi$: the block lags behind the table as it drifts out. It does not start along the radius.

Step 3. Cross-check in $S_0$

In $S_0$, $\mathbf a=\mathbf F_k/m=\mu_kg\,\hat{\mathbf F}_{needed}$ (inward and forward, $2.943$ m s$^{-2}$), while the point of the table under the block has $\mathbf a_{drag}=\mu g\,\hat{\mathbf F}_{needed}$ ($3.924$ m s$^{-2}$, part (c)). With $\mathbf v_{rel}=\mathbf 0$ the Coriolis term vanishes and $\mathbf a_{rel}=\mathbf a-\mathbf a_{drag}=(\mu_k-\mu)g\,\hat{\mathbf F}_{needed}$: the same result. In $S_0$ the block moves at $1.400$ m s$^{-1}$ tangentially; it is not "flung out along the radius". It simply cannot be pulled inward hard enough to stay on its circle, so its path opens out.

Step 4. Equal coefficients

If $\mu_k=\mu$, $\mathbf a_{rel}(t^{*+})=\mathbf 0$: the sliding starts smoothly, driven by the growth of $\Omega$ after $t^*$, but in the same direction.

Key results
  • In $S_1$: centrifugal $+$ Euler $=-\mathbf F_{needed}$, of modulus $\mu mg$, pointing outward and backward (at $\psi$ behind the outward radius).
  • $\mathbf a_{rel}(t^{*+})=(\mu-\mu_k)\,g$ in that direction: $0.981$ m s$^{-2}$, components $0.9807$ m s$^{-2}$ along $\mathbf e_r$ and $-0.0250$ m s$^{-2}$ along $\mathbf e_\varphi$.
  • In $S_0$ the velocity is tangential ($1.400$ m s$^{-1}$, no radial launch) and the acceleration $\mu_kg=2.943$ m s$^{-2}$ is smaller than the $\mu g=3.924$ m s$^{-2}$ needed to follow the table.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $\mu g/r_0$ and $\beta$ are s$^{-2}$, so $\Omega^{*4}=(\mu g/r_0)^2-\beta^2$ is s$^{-4}$ and $t^*=\Omega^*/\beta$ is s; $r_0\Omega^2$, $r_0\beta$, $\mu g$ are m s$^{-2}$.
  • Limits: $\beta\to0$ (very slow spin-up) gives $r_0\Omega^{*2}=\mu g$, the classic result for uniform rotation, with $\psi\to0$ and sliding along the outward radius in $S_1$; $\beta\to\mu g/r_0$ gives $t^*\to0$ and $\psi\to90^\circ$: the block slides straight backward.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) writing $r_0\Omega^2\le\mu g$ and forgetting the tangential friction $mr_0\beta$; (2) forgetting the Euler force $-m\dot{\boldsymbol\omega}\times\mathbf r$ in $S_1$; (3) putting $|\mathbf F|=\mu N$ before slip (static friction is only bounded by $\mu N$); (4) claiming the block flies off radially in $S_0$.
Problem · Unit 2 · 10 points · 60 min · Standard exam level

P13 — Capsule descent under quadratic drag with two parachute stages: terminal speed, quadratures and the work of drag

In a drop test, a capsule of mass $m$ is released from rest from a hovering helicopter at the point $O$ and falls vertically. Its drogue parachute is open from the release: the air exerts on the capsule the drag $-c_1v^2$ along the motion, where $v=\dot y\ge0$ is the speed and $y$ the distance fallen, measured downward from $O$. When the speed reaches $v_1$, the main parachute opens instantaneously and the drag becomes $-c_2v^2$, with $c_2\gg c_1$. Gravity $g$ is uniform and points down; buoyancy and the variation of air density with height are neglected.

Figure 1. Two-stage descent: coordinate $y$ downward from the release point $O$, velocity $v=\dot y$; forces on the capsule: weight $mg$ (down) and drag $cv^2$ (up), with $c=c_1$ under the drogue (stage 1) and $c=c_2\gg c_1$ under the main parachute (stage 2).

Setup

  • Data: $m=1000$ kg, $g=9.81$ m s$^{-2}$. The parachutes are given by their terminal speeds: $v_{t1}=50$ m s$^{-1}$ (drogue) and $v_{t2}=8$ m s$^{-1}$ (main). The main parachute opens at $v_1=40$ m s$^{-1}$.
  • Hypotheses: point particle, vertical motion (released with no horizontal velocity, no wind), constant drag coefficients within each stage, instantaneous opening.
  • The time of stage 2 is counted from the opening, and $\Delta y$ is the distance fallen since the opening.

Part (a) · 1 point

Degrees of freedom, forces and equation of motion

How many degrees of freedom has the capsule? Write the forces and the equation of motion for $v$.

Step 1. Count

With no horizontal force and no horizontal initial velocity the motion is along the vertical: 1 degree of freedom, the coordinate $y$, with unit vector $\mathbf e_y$ pointing down.

Step 2. Forces and Newton

Weight $mg\,\mathbf e_y$ (positive: it points down). Drag $-c|\mathbf v|\mathbf v=-cv^2\,\mathbf e_y$ for $v\ge0$ (upward). Newton (Notes §4.3), with $\mathbf a=\dot v\,\mathbf e_y$:

$$m\dot v=mg-cv^2$$
Key results
  • 1 degree of freedom, $y(t)$, positive downward.
  • $m\dot v=mg-cv^2$ (for $v\ge0$).

Part (b) · 2 points

Terminal speed and $v(t)$ in stage 1

Define the terminal speed $v_t$ and integrate the equation for the drogue stage, from rest at $t=0$, to get $v(t)$ and $y(t)$.

Step 1. Terminal speed

The acceleration vanishes when $mg=cv^2$: $v_t=\sqrt{mg/c}$. With it the equation reads $\dot v=g\big(1-v^2/v_t^2\big)$. For the drogue, $c_1=mg/v_{t1}^2=9810/2500=3.924$ kg m$^{-1}$.

Step 2. Separation of variables

For $0\le v<v_t$:

$$\frac{dv}{1-v^2/v_t^2}=g\,dt$$

The antiderivative of the left side is $F(v)=v_t\,\text{artanh}(v/v_t)$. Check with the chain rule: $\dfrac{dF}{dv}=v_t\cdot\dfrac{1}{1-(v/v_t)^2}\cdot\dfrac{1}{v_t}=\dfrac{1}{1-v^2/v_t^2}$.

Step 3. Limits (Barrow's rule)

Integrating from $(t=0,v=0)$ to $(t,v)$:

$$\Big[v_t\,\text{artanh}\frac{v'}{v_t}\Big]_0^{v}=\big[g\,t'\big]_0^{t}\ \Longrightarrow\ v_t\,\text{artanh}\frac{v}{v_t}-0=gt\ \Longrightarrow\ v=v_t\tanh\frac{gt}{v_t}$$

Since $\tanh<1$, $v$ approaches $v_t$ but never reaches it in finite time.

Step 4. Position

$y=\int_0^tv\,dt'$. With $w=\cosh(gt'/v_t)$, $dw=\dfrac{g}{v_t}\sinh(gt'/v_t)\,dt'$, so $\tanh(gt'/v_t)\,dt'=\dfrac{v_t}{g}\dfrac{dw}{w}$ and

$$y=v_t\cdot\frac{v_t}{g}\big[\ln w\big]_{w=1}^{w=\cosh(gt/v_t)}=\frac{v_t^2}{g}\ln\cosh\frac{gt}{v_t}$$
Key results
  • $v_t=\sqrt{mg/c}$, i.e. $c=mg/v_t^2$; $\ c_1=3.924$ kg m$^{-1}$.
  • $\displaystyle\int\frac{dv}{1-v^2/v_t^2}=v_t\,\text{artanh}\frac{v}{v_t}$ for $v<v_t$.
  • $v=v_t\tanh\dfrac{gt}{v_t}$, $\ y=\dfrac{v_t^2}{g}\ln\cosh\dfrac{gt}{v_t}$.

Part (c) · 1.5 points

Speed as a function of the distance fallen

Obtain $v(y)$ for stage 1 directly from the equation of motion, without using $t$.

Step 1. Chain rule

With $v=v(y(t))$: $\dot v=\dfrac{dv}{dy}\dot y=v\dfrac{dv}{dy}$, so $v\dfrac{dv}{dy}=g\big(1-v^2/v_t^2\big)$ and, separating, $\dfrac{v\,dv}{1-v^2/v_t^2}=g\,dy$.

Step 2. Substitution

$w=1-v^2/v_t^2$, $dw=-\dfrac{2v}{v_t^2}dv$, so $v\,dv=-\dfrac{v_t^2}{2}dw$. Limits: $v=0\mapsto w=1$, $v\mapsto w=1-v^2/v_t^2$:

$$-\frac{v_t^2}{2}\int_1^{1-v^2/v_t^2}\frac{dw}{w}=-\frac{v_t^2}{2}\Big[\ln w\Big]_1^{1-v^2/v_t^2}=-\frac{v_t^2}{2}\ln\Big(1-\frac{v^2}{v_t^2}\Big)=g\,y$$

Step 3. Result

$$v^2=v_t^2\Big(1-e^{-2gy/v_t^2}\Big)$$

Cross-check: inserting $y(t)$ of (b), $e^{-2gy/v_t^2}=\cosh^{-2}(gt/v_t)$ and $1-\cosh^{-2}=\tanh^2$, which is $v(t)^2/v_t^2$ of (b).

Key results
  • $v\dfrac{dv}{dy}=g\Big(1-\dfrac{v^2}{v_t^2}\Big)$.
  • $v^2=v_t^2\Big(1-e^{-2gy/v_t^2}\Big)$.

Part (d) · 1.5 points

Numbers for the drogue stage

If the main parachute never opened, how long and how far would the capsule fall before reaching $95\,\%$ of $v_{t1}$? When and where does it actually reach $v_1=40$ m s$^{-1}$?

Step 1. $95\,\%$ of the terminal speed

$v_{t1}/g=50/9.81=5.097$ s and $\text{artanh}\,0.95=\tfrac12\ln(1.95/0.05)=1.8318$, so $t_{95}=9.336$ s. From (c), $y=-\dfrac{v_t^2}{2g}\ln\big(1-v^2/v_t^2\big)$ with $v_t^2/2g=127.4$ m and $\ln(1/0.0975)=2.3279$: $y_{95}=296.6$ m.

Step 2. Opening of the main parachute

$\text{artanh}\,0.8=\tfrac12\ln9=1.0986$, so $t_1=5.097\cdot1.0986=5.600$ s, and $y_1=127.4\cdot\ln(1/0.36)=127.4\cdot1.0217=130.2$ m. A numerical integration of the equation of motion gives the same values.

Key results
  • $t_{95}=\dfrac{v_{t1}}{g}\text{artanh}\,0.95=9.336$ s; $\ y_{95}=\dfrac{v_{t1}^2}{2g}\ln\dfrac{1}{1-0.95^2}=296.6$ m.
  • $v_1=0.8\,v_{t1}$ is reached at $t_1=5.600$ s, $y_1=130.2$ m.

Part (e) · 2 points

Stage 2: the main parachute

At the opening $v_1>v_{t2}$. Find the acceleration just after the opening, solve for $v(t)$ in stage 2, and compute the time and the distance needed to come down to $1.05\,v_{t2}$. Why can the formula of (b) not be used?

Step 1. Acceleration at the opening

$c_2=mg/v_{t2}^2=9810/64=153.3$ kg m$^{-1}$ ($c_2/c_1=39.06$). Just after the opening $v=40$ m s$^{-1}$, so $\dot v=g\big(1-(40/8)^2\big)=-24g=-235.4$ m s$^{-2}$: the drag $c_2v_1^2=245.3$ kN is 25 times the weight.

Step 2. Why not $\tanh$

The formula $v=v_t\tanh(\dots)$ is bounded by $v_t$, so it can never start at $v_1=5v_{t2}$; equivalently the constant of integration would be $\text{artanh}\,5$, which is not real. For $v>v_t$ the antiderivative of $1/(1-v^2/v_t^2)$ is $v_t\,\text{arcoth}(v/v_t)$: $\dfrac{d}{dv}\big[v_t\,\text{arcoth}(v/v_t)\big]=\dfrac{1}{1-v^2/v_t^2}$, the same derivative as $\text{artanh}$ on the other branch.

Step 3. Integration

From $(t=0,v=v_1)$ to $(t,v)$:

$$\Big[v_{t2}\,\text{arcoth}\frac{v'}{v_{t2}}\Big]_{v_1}^{v}=\big[g\,t'\big]_0^t\ \Longrightarrow\ v=v_{t2}\coth\Big(\frac{gt}{v_{t2}}+\text{arcoth}\frac{v_1}{v_{t2}}\Big)$$

$\coth>1$ and decreases, so $v$ decreases monotonically to $v_{t2}$ from above.

Step 4. Time to $1.05\,v_{t2}$

$t=\dfrac{v_{t2}}{g}\Big(\text{arcoth}\,1.05-\text{arcoth}\,5\Big)$, with $\text{arcoth}\,1.05=\tfrac12\ln41=1.8568$ and $\text{arcoth}\,5=\tfrac12\ln1.5=0.2027$: $t=0.8155\cdot1.6541=1.349$ s.

Step 5. Distance

As in (c), $\dfrac{v\,dv}{1-v^2/v_{t2}^2}=g\,dy$, now integrated from $(\Delta y=0,\ v=v_1)$ to $(\Delta y,\ v)$. Substitute $w=1-v'^2/v_{t2}^2$, $dw=-\dfrac{2v'}{v_{t2}^2}dv'$, so $v'\,dv'=-\dfrac{v_{t2}^2}{2}dw$; the limits become $w_1=1-v_1^2/v_{t2}^2=-24$ and $w=1-v^2/v_{t2}^2$. In stage 2 $v'>v_{t2}$, so $w<0$ on the whole interval and the antiderivative of $1/w$ is $\ln|w|$ (not $\ln w$):

$$g\,\Delta y=-\frac{v_{t2}^2}{2}\int_{w_1}^{w}\frac{dw'}{w'}=-\frac{v_{t2}^2}{2}\Big[\ln|w'|\Big]_{w_1}^{w}=-\frac{v_{t2}^2}{2}\big(\ln|w|-\ln|w_1|\big)=\frac{v_{t2}^2}{2}\ln\frac{|w_1|}{|w|}$$

With $|w_1|=(v_1^2-v_{t2}^2)/v_{t2}^2$ and $|w|=(v^2-v_{t2}^2)/v_{t2}^2$ the factors $v_{t2}^2$ cancel in the quotient; solving for $\Delta y$ and, inversely, for $v^2$:

$$v^2=v_{t2}^2+\big(v_1^2-v_{t2}^2\big)e^{-2g\Delta y/v_{t2}^2},\qquad \Delta y=\frac{v_{t2}^2}{2g}\ln\frac{v_1^2-v_{t2}^2}{v^2-v_{t2}^2}$$

With $v=8.4$ m s$^{-1}$: $\Delta y=3.262\cdot\ln(1536/6.56)=3.262\cdot5.456=17.80$ m.

Key results
  • $c_2=153.3$ kg m$^{-1}$; $\dot v(0^+)=g\big(1-v_1^2/v_{t2}^2\big)=-24\,g=-235.4$ m s$^{-2}$ (a deceleration; drag $245.3$ kN against a weight of $9.81$ kN).
  • $v=v_{t2}\coth\Big(\dfrac{gt}{v_{t2}}+\text{arcoth}\dfrac{v_1}{v_{t2}}\Big)$, decreasing towards $v_{t2}$ from above.
  • To $8.4$ m s$^{-1}$: $t=1.349$ s, $\Delta y=17.80$ m.

Part (f) · 2 points

Work of the drag and energy balance

Is the mechanical energy conserved? Compute the work done by the drag (i) in stage 1, from the release to the opening, and (ii) in stage 2, from the opening to $v=8.4$ m s$^{-1}$, from the energy balance; then check (ii) by integrating the drag force along the path.

Step 1. Energy is not conserved

The power of the drag is $(-cv^2\,\mathbf e_y)\cdot(v\,\mathbf e_y)=-cv^3<0$ (Notes §4.4): it removes mechanical energy as long as the capsule moves. Only the weight is conservative ($V=-mgy$ with $y$ downward), so $\dfrac{d}{dt}\big(\tfrac12mv^2-mgy\big)=-cv^3$. At the opening the dissipated power is $c_2v_1^3=153.3\cdot64\,000$ W $=9.81$ MW.

Step 2. Stage 1

From $v=0$, $y=0$ to $v_1=40$ m s$^{-1}$, $y_1=130.2$ m: $\Delta E_{kin}=\tfrac12\cdot1000\cdot1600=800.0$ kJ and $W_{weight}=mgy_1=1277.1$ kJ, so $W_{drag,1}=800.0-1277.1=-477.1$ kJ.

Step 3. Stage 2 from the energy balance

$\Delta E_{kin}=\tfrac12\cdot1000\,(70.56-1600)=-764.7$ kJ and $W_{weight}=9810\cdot17.80=174.6$ kJ, so $W_{drag,2}=-764.7-174.6=-939.3$ kJ: in under $18$ m the main parachute absorbs almost twice what the drogue absorbed in $130$ m.

Step 4. Direct integral

$W_{drag,2}=-\int_0^{\Delta y}c_2v^2\,ds$ with $v^2=v_{t2}^2+(v_1^2-v_{t2}^2)e^{-2gs/v_{t2}^2}$ and $c_2v_{t2}^2=mg$:

$$W_{drag,2}=-mg\,\Delta y-c_2\big(v_1^2-v_{t2}^2\big)\Big[-\frac{v_{t2}^2}{2g}e^{-2gs/v_{t2}^2}\Big]_0^{\Delta y}=-mg\,\Delta y-\frac m2\big(v_1^2-v_{t2}^2\big)\Big(1-e^{-2g\Delta y/v_{t2}^2}\Big)$$

and by (e), $\big(v_1^2-v_{t2}^2\big)\big(1-e^{-2g\Delta y/v_{t2}^2}\big)=v_1^2-v^2$, so $W_{drag,2}=-\big(mg\Delta y+\tfrac12m(v_1^2-v^2)\big)=-939.3$ kJ, the value of Step 3.

Key results
  • No: the drag power $-cv^3<0$ dissipates energy ($9.81$ MW just after the opening).
  • (i) $W_{drag,1}=\tfrac12mv_1^2-mgy_1=800.0-1277.1=-477.1$ kJ.
  • (ii) $W_{drag,2}=\tfrac12m(8.4^2-40^2)-mg\Delta y=-764.7-174.6=-939.3$ kJ; the direct integral gives $-\big(mg\Delta y+\tfrac12m(v_1^2-v^2)\big)$, the same.

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $[c]=$ N/(m s$^{-1}$)$^2=$ kg m$^{-1}$, so $\sqrt{mg/c}$ is m s$^{-1}$; $gt/v_t$ and $gy/v_t^2$ are dimensionless (arguments of $\tanh$ and $\exp$); $[cv^3]=$ W.
  • Limits: for $gt\ll v_t$, $\tanh x\approx x$ gives $v\approx gt$ and $y\approx\tfrac12gt^2$ (free fall); for $t\to\infty$ both $\tanh$ and $\coth$ tend to 1 and $v\to v_t$, from below in stage 1 and from above in stage 2; $y\approx v_t t-\tfrac{v_t^2}{g}\ln2$ for large $t$ in stage 1.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) writing the drag as $+cv^2$ or $-cv$ (wrong sign or wrong law); (2) using $v=v_t\tanh(\dots)$ after the main parachute opens, which would need $\text{artanh}$ of a number larger than 1; (3) skipping the chain rule $\dot v=v\,dv/dy$; (4) assuming the terminal speed is reached in a finite time; (5) writing energy conservation with drag acting.
Problem · Unit 1 · 10 points · 60 min · Standard exam level

P14 — A spiral trajectory flown at constant speed: polar and intrinsic kinematics, a non-central force and zero power

A drone $P$ of mass $m$ flies in a horizontal plane (its weight is balanced by the vertical lift, which is not studied). In this plane take the inertial frame $S_0=\{O;\mathbf i,\mathbf j\}$ and the polar coordinates $(r,\varphi)$ of $P$ about $O$, with $\varphi$ measured from $\mathbf i$ counter-clockwise and the polar basis $\mathbf e_r=\cos\varphi\,\mathbf i+\sin\varphi\,\mathbf j$, $\mathbf e_\varphi=-\sin\varphi\,\mathbf i+\cos\varphi\,\mathbf j$. The autopilot keeps $P$ on the logarithmic spiral

$$r=r_0\,e^{b\varphi}\qquad(r_0>0,\ b>0)$$

with a constant speed $|\mathbf v|=v_0$ and $\varphi$ increasing. At $t=0$ the drone is at $\varphi=0$, $r=r_0$. The resultant of the horizontal forces on the drone (thrust and drag) is called $\mathbf F$. Let $\mathbf e_t$, $\mathbf e_n$ be the unit tangent and the principal normal of the trajectory, $\rho$ its radius of curvature and $\alpha$ the angle between $\mathbf e_r$ and $\mathbf e_t$.

Figure 1. Drone $P$ on the spiral $r=r_0e^{b\varphi}$ flown with constant speed $v_0$ ($\varphi$ increasing): polar coordinates $(r,\varphi)$ about $O$, unit tangent $\mathbf e_t$ and principal normal $\mathbf e_n$, constant angle $\alpha$ between $\mathbf e_r$ and $\mathbf e_t$ (drawn for $b=3/4$).

Setup

  • Data: $r_0$, $b$, $v_0$, $m$. In the numerical parts: $r_0=20$ m, $b=3/4$, $v_0=10$ m/s, $m=2$ kg, and the drone flies from $r_1=r_0=20$ m to $r_2=50$ m.
  • Hypotheses: point particle, planar motion in the horizontal plane, $S_0$ inertial; the trajectory and the speed are imposed by the autopilot (the force $\mathbf F$ is whatever is needed to keep them).
  • Useful constant: $k\equiv\sqrt{1+b^2}$; for $b=3/4$, $k=\sqrt{1+9/16}=5/4$.

Part (a) · 2 points

Velocity in polar coordinates and the constant angle

How many parameters fix the position of $P$ on the trajectory? Write $\mathbf v$ in the polar basis and obtain $\dot r$ and $\dot\varphi$ as functions of $r$ from the two conditions of the statement. Show that the angle $\alpha$ between the velocity and the radius is constant. Give the numbers for the data of the Setup.

Step 1. Parameters

A free point of the plane has two coordinates $(r,\varphi)$. The trajectory $r=r_0e^{b\varphi}$ is one relation between them, so one parameter (for instance $\varphi$, or $r$, or the arc length $s$) fixes the position on the curve. The speed law $|\mathbf v|=v_0$ then fixes how that parameter changes with time: nothing is left free, which is why this is a pure kinematics problem and $\mathbf F$ is an output.

Step 2. Velocity in the polar basis

The polar basis turns with $\varphi$; its relation to the inertial basis is

$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf e_r\ \mathbf e_\varphi\end{pmatrix}=[R]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf i\ \mathbf j\end{pmatrix},\qquad [R]=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\varphi&\sin\varphi\ -\sin\varphi&\cos\varphi\end{pmatrix}$$

with $[R][R]^T=\begin{pmatrix}\cos^2\varphi+\sin^2\varphi&-\cos\varphi\sin\varphi+\sin\varphi\cos\varphi\ -\sin\varphi\cos\varphi+\cos\varphi\sin\varphi&\sin^2\varphi+\cos^2\varphi\end{pmatrix}=I$ and $\det[R]=\cos^2\varphi+\sin^2\varphi=+1$ (same orientation as $\{\mathbf i,\mathbf j\}$). Differentiating the rows with the chain rule gives $\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi$ and $\dot{\mathbf e}_\varphi=-\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_r$.

With $\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi$ and $\dot{\mathbf e}_\varphi=-\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_r$ (Notes §2.6), differentiating $\mathbf r=r\,\mathbf e_r$ with the product rule:

$$\mathbf v=\dot r\,\mathbf e_r+r\,\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot r\,\mathbf e_r+r\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi$$

Step 3. The trajectory relates $\dot r$ and $\dot\varphi$

By the chain rule $\frac{d}{dt}r(\varphi(t))=\frac{dr}{d\varphi}\dot\varphi$, with $\frac{dr}{d\varphi}=b\,r_0e^{b\varphi}=b\,r$:

$$\dot r=b\,r\,\dot\varphi$$

Step 4. The speed fixes $\dot\varphi$

$$|\mathbf v|^2=\dot r^2+r^2\dot\varphi^2=b^2r^2\dot\varphi^2+r^2\dot\varphi^2=(1+b^2)\,r^2\dot\varphi^2=v_0^2$$

With $\dot\varphi>0$ (the statement):

$$\dot\varphi=\frac{v_0}{r\sqrt{1+b^2}},\qquad \dot r=b\,r\dot\varphi=\frac{b\,v_0}{\sqrt{1+b^2}}$$

The radial velocity is constant although $\dot\varphi$ is not: $\dot\varphi$ decreases as $1/r$.

Step 5. The angle with the radius

$\mathbf v=v_0\,\mathbf e_t$, so $\cos\alpha=\mathbf e_r\cdot\mathbf e_t=\dot r/v_0$ and $\sin\alpha=\mathbf e_\varphi\cdot\mathbf e_t=r\dot\varphi/v_0$:

$$\tan\alpha=\frac{r\dot\varphi}{\dot r}=\frac{r\dot\varphi}{b\,r\dot\varphi}=\frac1b$$

independent of the point: the logarithmic spiral cuts every radius at the same angle (it is the equiangular spiral).

Step 6. Numbers

$k=5/4$: $\dot r=\frac{(3/4)\cdot10}{5/4}=6$ m/s, $\ r\dot\varphi=\frac{v_0}{k}=8$ m/s (check: $6^2+8^2=10^2$), $\ \tan\alpha=4/3$, $\ \alpha=53.13^\circ$.

Key results
  • One parameter ($\varphi$, or $r$, or $s$); with the speed imposed, the motion is fully determined.
  • $\dot r=b\,r\dot\varphi$, $\ \dot\varphi=\dfrac{v_0}{r\sqrt{1+b^2}}$, $\ \dot r=\dfrac{b\,v_0}{\sqrt{1+b^2}}$ constant.
  • $\tan\alpha=\dfrac{r\dot\varphi}{\dot r}=\dfrac1b$; numbers: $\dot r=6$ m/s, $r\dot\varphi=8$ m/s, $\alpha=\arctan(4/3)=53.13^\circ$.

Part (b) · 1.5 points

Time law: $r(t)$, $\varphi(t)$ and the flight time

Integrate the results of (a) to obtain $r(t)$ and $\varphi(t)$, and check that they lie on the spiral. Compute the time needed to go from $r_1=20$ m to $r_2=50$ m and the angle turned.

Step 1. Radial law

$\dot r=c$ with $c=bv_0/k$ constant. Integrating from $0$ to $t$ with Barrow's rule, $r(t)-r(0)=\int_0^tc\,d\tau=c\,t$:

$$r(t)=r_0+\frac{b\,v_0}{\sqrt{1+b^2}}\,t$$

Step 2. Angular law (quadrature)

$$\varphi(t)=\varphi(0)+\int_0^t\frac{v_0}{k\,(r_0+c\tau)}\,d\tau$$

Substitution $u=r_0+c\tau$, $du=c\,d\tau$; the limits $\tau=0\mapsto u=r_0$ and $\tau=t\mapsto u=r(t)$:

$$\varphi(t)=\frac{v_0}{k\,c}\int_{r_0}^{r(t)}\frac{du}{u}=\frac{v_0}{k\,c}\Big[\ln u\Big]_{r_0}^{r(t)}=\frac{v_0}{k\,c}\big(\ln r(t)-\ln r_0\big)=\frac1b\ln\frac{r(t)}{r_0}$$

since $\frac{v_0}{kc}=\frac{v_0}{k}\cdot\frac{k}{bv_0}=\frac1b$. Inverting, $r(t)=r_0e^{b\varphi(t)}$: the time law lies on the spiral, as it must.

Step 3. Flight time and angle turned

$r_2-r_1=c\,t_{12}$:

$$t_{12}=\frac{(r_2-r_1)\sqrt{1+b^2}}{b\,v_0}=\frac{30\cdot(5/4)}{(3/4)\cdot10}=5\ \text{s}$$
$$\Delta\varphi=\frac1b\ln\frac{r_2}{r_1}=\frac43\ln2.5=1.2217\ \text{rad}=70.00^\circ$$
Key results
  • $r(t)=r_0+\dfrac{b\,v_0}{\sqrt{1+b^2}}\,t$, $\ \varphi(t)=\dfrac1b\ln\dfrac{r(t)}{r_0}$.
  • $t_{12}=\dfrac{(r_2-r_1)\sqrt{1+b^2}}{b\,v_0}=5$ s; $\ \Delta\varphi=\dfrac{\ln(5/2)}{3/4}=1.2217$ rad $=70.00^\circ$.

Part (c) · 1.5 points

Arc length and the intrinsic basis

Obtain the arc length $s(r)$ measured from $r_0$, and check it against $v_0\,t_{12}$. Write $\mathbf e_t$ and the principal normal $\mathbf e_n$ in the polar basis.

Step 1. Arc length

From $\varphi(r)=\frac1b\ln\frac r{r_0}$, $\frac{d\varphi}{dr}=\frac1{br}$, so $r\,d\varphi=\frac{dr}{b}$ and

$$ds=\sqrt{dr^2+r^2d\varphi^2}=\sqrt{1+\frac1{b^2}}\,dr=\frac{\sqrt{1+b^2}}{b}\,dr$$

Integrating from $r_0$ to $r$ (Barrow):

$$s(r)=\int_{r_0}^{r}\frac{k}{b}\,dr'=\frac kb\Big[r'\Big]_{r_0}^{r}=\frac{\sqrt{1+b^2}}{b}\,(r-r_0)$$

From 20 m to 50 m: $s=\frac{5/4}{3/4}\cdot30=50$ m, equal to $v_0t_{12}=10\cdot5=50$ m, as it must at constant speed.

Step 2. Unit tangent

$\mathbf e_t=\mathbf v/v_0$ with the components of (a):

$$\mathbf e_t=\frac{\dot r}{v_0}\,\mathbf e_r+\frac{r\dot\varphi}{v_0}\,\mathbf e_\varphi=\frac{b\,\mathbf e_r+\mathbf e_\varphi}{\sqrt{1+b^2}}=\cos\alpha\,\mathbf e_r+\sin\alpha\,\mathbf e_\varphi$$

Step 3. Principal normal

The components of $\mathbf e_t$ are constant, so only the basis vectors change. With the product rule and $\dot{\mathbf e}_r=\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi$, $\dot{\mathbf e}_\varphi=-\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_r$:

$$\dot{\mathbf e}_t=\frac{b\,\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi-\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_r}{k}=\dot\varphi\,\frac{-\mathbf e_r+b\,\mathbf e_\varphi}{k}$$

By the chain rule $\frac{d\mathbf e_t}{ds}=\frac{\dot{\mathbf e}_t}{\dot s}$ with $\dot s=v_0$, and the principal normal is the unit vector of $d\mathbf e_t/ds$ (Notes §2.4). The vector $(-\mathbf e_r+b\,\mathbf e_\varphi)/k$ has modulus $\sqrt{1+b^2}/k=1$, so

$$\mathbf e_n=\frac{-\mathbf e_r+b\,\mathbf e_\varphi}{\sqrt{1+b^2}},\qquad \Big|\frac{d\mathbf e_t}{ds}\Big|=\frac{\dot\varphi}{v_0}$$

Check: $\mathbf e_t\cdot\mathbf e_n=(-b+b)/k^2=0$, and $\mathbf e_n$ is $\mathbf e_t$ turned $+90^\circ$ (to the left of the motion, towards the concave side and partly towards $O$).

Key results
  • $s(r)=\dfrac{\sqrt{1+b^2}}{b}\,(r-r_0)$; from 20 m to 50 m: $s=50$ m $=v_0t_{12}$.
  • $\mathbf e_t=\dfrac{b\,\mathbf e_r+\mathbf e_\varphi}{\sqrt{1+b^2}}$, $\ \mathbf e_n=\dfrac{-\mathbf e_r+b\,\mathbf e_\varphi}{\sqrt{1+b^2}}$ (towards the concave side, to the left of the motion).

Part (d) · 2 points

Acceleration, radius of curvature

Compute the acceleration in polar components. Obtain the radius of curvature $\rho$ and show that $\mathbf a=\dfrac{v_0^2}{\rho}\,\mathbf e_n$. Numbers at $r=20$ m and at $r=50$ m.

Step 1. Polar acceleration

Differentiating $\mathbf v=\dot r\,\mathbf e_r+r\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi$ with the product rule (Notes §2.6):

$$\mathbf a=(\ddot r-r\dot\varphi^2)\,\mathbf e_r+(r\ddot\varphi+2\dot r\dot\varphi)\,\mathbf e_\varphi$$

Step 2. The derivatives needed

$\ddot r=0$ because $\dot r$ is constant. From $\dot\varphi=\frac{v_0}{k}\,r^{-1}$, with the chain rule $\frac{d}{dt}r^{-1}=-r^{-2}\dot r$:

$$\ddot\varphi=-\frac{v_0}{k}\,\frac{\dot r}{r^2}=-\frac{\dot r\,\dot\varphi}{r}$$

Step 3. Components

$$a_r=0-r\dot\varphi^2=-r\,\frac{v_0^2}{r^2k^2}=-\frac{v_0^2}{r(1+b^2)}$$
$$a_\varphi=r\Big(-\frac{\dot r\dot\varphi}{r}\Big)+2\dot r\dot\varphi=\dot r\dot\varphi=\frac{bv_0}{k}\cdot\frac{v_0}{rk}=\frac{b\,v_0^2}{r(1+b^2)}$$

Step 4. Intrinsic form and radius of curvature

The curvature is $1/\rho=|d\mathbf e_t/ds|=\dot\varphi/v_0=\frac1{rk}$ (part (c)), so $\rho=r\sqrt{1+b^2}$. In the intrinsic basis (Notes §2.5) $\mathbf a=\dot v\,\mathbf e_t+\frac{v^2}{\rho}\,\mathbf e_n$ with $\dot v=0$:

$$\mathbf a=\frac{v_0^2}{r\sqrt{1+b^2}}\,\mathbf e_n=\frac{v_0^2}{r\sqrt{1+b^2}}\cdot\frac{-\mathbf e_r+b\,\mathbf e_\varphi}{\sqrt{1+b^2}}=-\frac{v_0^2}{r(1+b^2)}\,\mathbf e_r+\frac{b\,v_0^2}{r(1+b^2)}\,\mathbf e_\varphi$$

which coincides with Step 3: the two routes agree. Check: $\sqrt{a_r^2+a_\varphi^2}=\frac{v_0^2\sqrt{1+b^2}}{r(1+b^2)}=\frac{v_0^2}{\rho}$.

Step 5. Numbers

At $r=20$ m: $\rho=20\cdot\frac54=25$ m, $|\mathbf a|=\frac{100}{25}=4$ m/s$^2$, $a_r=-\frac{100}{20\cdot25/16}=-3.2$ m/s$^2$, $a_\varphi=\frac34\cdot3.2=2.4$ m/s$^2$ (check: $3.2^2+2.4^2=16$). At $r=50$ m: $\rho=62.5$ m and $|\mathbf a|=1.6$ m/s$^2$: the spiral opens up and the turn becomes gentler.

Key results
  • $a_r=-\dfrac{v_0^2}{r(1+b^2)}$, $\ a_\varphi=\dfrac{b\,v_0^2}{r(1+b^2)}$, $\ \rho=r\sqrt{1+b^2}$, $\ |\mathbf a|=\dfrac{v_0^2}{r\sqrt{1+b^2}}$.
  • At $r=20$ m: $\rho=25$ m, $a_r=-3.2$ m/s$^2$, $a_\varphi=2.4$ m/s$^2$, $|\mathbf a|=4$ m/s$^2$; at $r=50$ m: $|\mathbf a|=1.6$ m/s$^2$.

Part (e) · 1.5 points

Force, torque and angular momentum about $O$

Find the horizontal force $\mathbf F$ needed at $r=20$ m. Is it a central force about $O$? Compute the torque $\mathbf M_O$ and the angular momentum $\mathbf H_O(t)$, and verify the angular momentum theorem between $r_1$ and $r_2$.

Step 1. The force

Newton's second law in the inertial frame, $\mathbf F=m\mathbf a$, with $m=2$ kg and the numbers of (d):

$$\mathbf F=2\,(-3.2\,\mathbf e_r+2.4\,\mathbf e_\varphi)=-6.4\,\mathbf e_r+4.8\,\mathbf e_\varphi\ \text{N},\qquad|\mathbf F|=8\ \text{N}$$

In general $\mathbf F=\frac{mv_0^2}{\rho}\mathbf e_n$. Since $\mathbf e_n\cdot(-\mathbf e_r)=1/k$, the angle $\gamma$ between $\mathbf F$ and $-\mathbf e_r$ has $\cos\gamma=1/\sqrt{1+b^2}$, i.e. $\tan\gamma=b$: $\gamma=\arctan(3/4)=36.87^\circ$. The force is not central: $F_\varphi\ne0$ at every point.

Step 2. Torque

$\mathbf M_O=\mathbf r\times\mathbf F=r\,\mathbf e_r\times(F_r\mathbf e_r+F_\varphi\mathbf e_\varphi)=r\,F_\varphi\,\mathbf k$ (since $\mathbf e_r\times\mathbf e_\varphi=\mathbf k$):

$$\mathbf M_O=m\,r\,a_\varphi\,\mathbf k=m\,r\,\frac{b\,v_0^2}{r(1+b^2)}\,\mathbf k=\frac{m\,b\,v_0^2}{1+b^2}\,\mathbf k=\frac{2\cdot0.75\cdot100}{1.5625}\,\mathbf k=96\ \mathbf k\ \text{N m}$$

constant along the whole flight, although $\mathbf F$ changes.

Step 3. Angular momentum

$\mathbf r\times\mathbf v=r\,\mathbf e_r\times(\dot r\,\mathbf e_r+r\dot\varphi\,\mathbf e_\varphi)=r^2\dot\varphi\,\mathbf k$, so with $\dot\varphi$ of (a):

$$\mathbf H_O=m\,r^2\dot\varphi\,\mathbf k=\frac{m\,r(t)\,v_0}{\sqrt{1+b^2}}\,\mathbf k$$

At $r=20$ m: $H_O=\frac{2\cdot20\cdot10}{1.25}=320$ kg m$^2$/s; at $r=50$ m: $800$ kg m$^2$/s.

Step 4. Angular momentum theorem

About the fixed point $O$, $\dot{\mathbf H}_O=\mathbf M_O$ (Notes §4.8). By the chain rule $\frac{d}{dt}H_O=\frac{mv_0}{k}\dot r=\frac{mv_0}{k}\cdot\frac{bv_0}{k}=\frac{m\,b\,v_0^2}{1+b^2}=M_O$. Integrating from $0$ to $t_{12}$ (Barrow): $H_O(t_{12})-H_O(0)=\int_0^{t_{12}}M_O\,dt=96\cdot5=480$ kg m$^2$/s, and indeed $800-320=480$. The angular momentum is not conserved, and the areal velocity $\frac12r^2\dot\varphi$ grows: Kepler's second law does not hold for this force.

Key results
  • At $r=20$ m: $F_r=-6.4$ N, $F_\varphi=4.8$ N, $|\mathbf F|=8$ N; it makes $\arctan b=36.87^\circ$ with $-\mathbf e_r$: not central.
  • $\mathbf M_O=m\,r\,a_\varphi\,\mathbf k=\dfrac{m\,b\,v_0^2}{1+b^2}\,\mathbf k=96$ N m (constant); $\ \mathbf H_O=\dfrac{m\,r(t)\,v_0}{\sqrt{1+b^2}}\,\mathbf k$: 320 kg m$^2$/s at 20 m, 800 kg m$^2$/s at 50 m; $\Delta H_O=96\cdot5=480$ kg m$^2$/s.

Part (f) · 1.5 points

Power, kinetic energy and the trap

Compute the power of $\mathbf F$ and the work it does between $r_1$ and $r_2$, and check the result with the kinetic energy theorem. A student argues: "the speed is constant, so the drone is in equilibrium and the net force is zero; and since $\mathbf F$ does no work, it must be a central (or conservative) force". Discuss.

Step 1. Power and work

The power of $\mathbf F$ is $P=\mathbf F\cdot\mathbf v$ (Notes §4.5). With $\mathbf F=\frac{mv_0^2}{\rho}\mathbf e_n$ and $\mathbf v=v_0\mathbf e_t$:

$$P=\frac{m\,v_0^3}{\rho}\,(\mathbf e_n\cdot\mathbf e_t)=0$$

In polar components the same result is $m(a_r\dot r+a_\varphi r\dot\varphi)=m\frac{v_0^2}{rk^2}\big(-\frac{bv_0}{k}+b\,\frac{v_0}{k}\big)=0$. The work between $r_1$ and $r_2$ is $W_{12}=\int_0^{t_{12}}P\,dt=0$.

Step 2. Kinetic energy theorem

$\frac{d}{dt}\big(\tfrac12m\,\mathbf v\cdot\mathbf v\big)=m\,\mathbf a\cdot\mathbf v=P=0$, consistent with $|\mathbf v|=v_0$: $E_{kin}=\tfrac12\cdot2\cdot10^2=100$ J at every instant.

Step 3. The trap

  • "Constant speed means zero force" confuses the speed $|\mathbf v|$ with the velocity vector. The direction of $\mathbf v$ turns with angular rate $\dot\varphi$ (since $\alpha$ is constant, $\mathbf e_t$ turns exactly as $\mathbf e_r$), so $\mathbf a=\frac{v_0^2}{\rho}\mathbf e_n\neq\mathbf0$: at 20 m the autopilot must supply 8 N.
  • "No work, so central" is false: zero power only means $\mathbf F\perp\mathbf v$ at each instant. Here $\mathbf F$ is perpendicular to $\mathbf v$ but makes $36.87^\circ$ with the radius, so it has a moment about $O$ and $\mathbf H_O$ grows by 480 kg m$^2$/s.
  • A force perpendicular to the velocity is not conservative in general either: being workless along this path says nothing about the work along other paths. What matters for the energy budget is that it does no work along the actual motion.
Key results
  • $P=\mathbf F\cdot\mathbf v=\frac{mv_0^2}{\rho}\,v_0\,(\mathbf e_n\cdot\mathbf e_t)=0$; $W_{12}=0$; $E_{kin}=\tfrac12mv_0^2=100$ J at every instant.
  • Constant speed is not constant velocity: $|\mathbf F|=mv_0^2/\rho\neq0$. Zero power only says $\mathbf F\perp\mathbf v$; the force is neither zero nor central ($\mathbf M_O\ne\mathbf0$).

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $\dot r=bv_0/k$ and $r\dot\varphi$ are m/s; $\rho=rk$ is m; $a=v_0^2/\rho$ is m$^2$s$^{-2}$/m = m s$^{-2}$; $M_O=mbv_0^2/k^2$ is kg m$^2$s$^{-2}$ = N m; $H_O=mrv_0/k$ is kg m$^2$/s; $t_{12}=(r_2-r_1)k/(bv_0)$ is s.
  • Limiting cases: $b\to0$ gives $r=r_0$, $\dot r=0$, $\alpha=90^\circ$, $\rho=r_0$, $a_\varphi=0$: uniform circular motion with a central force $-mv_0^2/r_0\,\mathbf e_r$ and $\mathbf H_O$ constant. $b\to\infty$ gives $\alpha\to0$: an almost radial straight flight with $\rho\to\infty$ and $\mathbf a\to\mathbf0$.
  • Flying inwards: with $\varphi$ decreasing, $r$ decreases linearly and reaches $O$ after the finite time $r_0k/(bv_0)=10/3$ s and the finite length $r_0k/b=100/3$ m, while $\varphi=\frac1b\ln(r/r_0)\to-\infty$: infinitely many turns in a finite time, with $\dot\varphi$ and $|\mathbf a|$ growing without bound. A real autopilot cannot follow the spiral to the end.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) setting $a_r=0$ because $\dot r$ is constant (the centripetal term $-r\dot\varphi^2$ remains); (2) taking $\dot\varphi$ constant and dropping $r\ddot\varphi$ in $a_\varphi$; (3) writing $\rho=r$ as for a circle; (4) concluding from $P=0$ that $\mathbf F=\mathbf0$ or that $\mathbf H_O$ is conserved.
Problem · Unit 5 · 10 points · 60 min · High difficulty / trap

P15 — Relative orbital motion near a target on a circular orbit: Clohessy-Wiltshire equations, drift and a counter-intuitive rendezvous

A target $T$ moves on a circular orbit of radius $R_T$ around the Earth, whose centre $O$ is the origin of the inertial frame $S_0$ and whose gravitational parameter is $\mu$ (the Earth's gravity on a mass $m$ at $\mathbf r$ is $-m\mu\,\mathbf r/|\mathbf r|^3$). The target's angular velocity is constant, $n\,\mathbf k$, with the mean motion $n=\sqrt{\mu/R_T^3}$ ($\mathbf k$ normal to the orbital plane). A chaser $P$ of mass $m$, subject only to the Earth's gravity, moves in the same plane close to $T$.

Use the frame $S_1=\{T;\mathbf e_x,\mathbf e_y,\mathbf k\}$ attached to the target: $\mathbf e_x$ radial, pointing away from $O$; $\mathbf e_y=\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_x$ along the target's velocity (along-track); $S_1$ rotates with $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=n\,\mathbf k$. The relative position of the chaser is $\boldsymbol\rho=x\,\mathbf e_x+y\,\mathbf e_y$ with $|\boldsymbol\rho|\ll R_T$. Initial conditions at $t=0$: $x_0$, $y_0$, $\dot x_0$, $\dot y_0$ (relative to $S_1$).

Figure 1. Target $T$ on a circular orbit of radius $R_T$ with mean motion $n$; rotating frame $S_1=\{T;\mathbf e_x,\mathbf e_y\}$ ($x$ radial outwards, $y$ along-track); chaser $P$ at $\boldsymbol\rho=x\,\mathbf e_x+y\,\mathbf e_y$ (distance exaggerated). Inset: bounded relative orbit ($\dot y_0=-2nx_0$), a $2{:}1$ ellipse about its centre $C$, run in the sense of the arrow.

Setup

  • Data: $\mu$, $R_T$, $n=\sqrt{\mu/R_T^3}$, the initial conditions. Numerical part (f): $\mu=3.986\times10^{14}$ m$^3$/s$^2$, $R_T=6778$ km (low Earth orbit), chaser 1 km behind the target ($x_0=0$, $y_0=-1000$ m).
  • Hypotheses: point masses, planar motion in the orbital plane, $S_0$ inertial, no drag, no other bodies; the target's orbit is not affected by the chaser; terms of order $|\boldsymbol\rho|^2/R_T^2$ are neglected in the gravity difference.
  • The frame $S_1$ has a uniformly rotating basis ($\dot{\boldsymbol\omega}_{10}=\mathbf 0$) and an accelerated origin ($\mathbf a_T=-n^2R_T\,\mathbf e_x$).

Part (a) · 1.5 points

Degrees of freedom, frame and relative kinematics

How many degrees of freedom has $P$ in this planar problem? Write the absolute acceleration of $P$ in the basis of $S_1$ as the sum of the acceleration of $T$, the relative acceleration, the Coriolis and the centripetal terms, and list the inertia forces that act on $P$ in $S_1$.

Step 1. Count

$P$ is free in the orbital plane: 2 degrees of freedom, the coordinates $(x,y)$ in $S_1$. There are no constraints and no reactions; the only force is gravity.

Step 2. Motion of the frame

$T$ moves on a circle of radius $R_T$ with constant angular velocity $n\mathbf k$, so its acceleration is purely centripetal: $\mathbf a_T=-n^2R_T\,\mathbf e_x$. The basis of $S_1$ turns with $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=n\mathbf k$, and $\dot{\boldsymbol\omega}_{10}=\mathbf 0$.

Change of basis: take the inertial basis $\{\mathbf i,\mathbf j,\mathbf k\}$ of $S_0$ with $\mathbf i$ along $\overrightarrow{OT}$ at $t=0$. Then $\mathbf e_x=\cos nt\,\mathbf i+\sin nt\,\mathbf j$, $\mathbf e_y=\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_x=-\sin nt\,\mathbf i+\cos nt\,\mathbf j$, and

$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf e_x\\ \mathbf e_y\\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix}=[R]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf i\\ \mathbf j\\ \mathbf k\end{pmatrix},\qquad [R]=\begin{pmatrix}\cos nt&\sin nt&0\\ -\sin nt&\cos nt&0\\ 0&0&1\end{pmatrix}$$

The rows have length $\cos^2nt+\sin^2nt=1$, rows 1 and 2 are orthogonal ($-\cos nt\sin nt+\sin nt\cos nt=0$) and row 3 is $\mathbf k$, so $[R][R]^T=I$; $\det[R]=\cos^2nt+\sin^2nt=+1$. Differentiating, $\dot{\mathbf e}_x=n\,\mathbf e_y=n\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_x$ and $\dot{\mathbf e}_y=-n\,\mathbf e_x=n\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_y$ (Poisson), which confirms $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=n\,\mathbf k$.

Step 3. Relative kinematics

In $S_1$: $\mathbf v_{rel}=\dot x\,\mathbf e_x+\dot y\,\mathbf e_y$, $\mathbf a_{rel}=\ddot x\,\mathbf e_x+\ddot y\,\mathbf e_y$. With $\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_x=\mathbf e_y$, $\mathbf k\times\mathbf e_y=-\mathbf e_x$:

$$2\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf v_{rel}=2n\big(\dot x\,\mathbf e_y-\dot y\,\mathbf e_x\big),\qquad \boldsymbol\omega\times\boldsymbol\rho=n\big(x\,\mathbf e_y-y\,\mathbf e_x\big),\qquad \boldsymbol\omega\times(\boldsymbol\omega\times\boldsymbol\rho)=-n^2\big(x\,\mathbf e_x+y\,\mathbf e_y\big)$$

The composition of accelerations (Notes §3.2), with the Euler term zero, gives

$$\mathbf a_P=\big(-n^2R_T+\ddot x-2n\dot y-n^2x\big)\,\mathbf e_x+\big(\ddot y+2n\dot x-n^2y\big)\,\mathbf e_y$$

Step 4. Inertia forces

Written as $m\mathbf a_{rel}=\mathbf F+\mathbf F_{in}$ (Notes §4.9), the inertia force is $\mathbf F_{in}=-m\mathbf a_T-2m\boldsymbol\omega\times\mathbf v_{rel}-m\boldsymbol\omega\times(\boldsymbol\omega\times\boldsymbol\rho)$: the dragging force of the origin $mn^2R_T\,\mathbf e_x$, the centrifugal force $mn^2\boldsymbol\rho$ (about $T$) and the Coriolis force $-2mn\,\mathbf k\times\mathbf v_{rel}$. The Coriolis force is perpendicular to $\mathbf v_{rel}$ and does no work in $S_1$.

Key results
  • 2 degrees of freedom, $(x,y)$; $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=n\mathbf k$ constant, $\mathbf a_T=-n^2R_T\,\mathbf e_x$.
  • $\mathbf a_P=\big(-n^2R_T+\ddot x-2n\dot y-n^2x\big)\mathbf e_x+\big(\ddot y+2n\dot x-n^2y\big)\mathbf e_y$.
  • Inertia forces in $S_1$: $-m\mathbf a_T=mn^2R_T\,\mathbf e_x$, centrifugal $mn^2\boldsymbol\rho$, Coriolis $-2mn\mathbf k\times\mathbf v_{rel}$; no Euler force.

Part (b) · 2 points

Linearised gravity and the equations of motion

Expand the gravity on $P$ to first order in $|\boldsymbol\rho|/R_T$ and obtain the two equations of relative motion. Explain why the terms in $R_T$ disappear.

Step 1. The exact gravity

$\mathbf r_P=\overrightarrow{OT}+\boldsymbol\rho=(R_T+x)\,\mathbf e_x+y\,\mathbf e_y$ and $|\mathbf r_P|^2=R_T^2\big(1+\tfrac{2x}{R_T}+\tfrac{x^2+y^2}{R_T^2}\big)$.

Step 2. First-order expansion

Keeping only first-order terms, $u=2x/R_T$ and $(1+u)^{-3/2}=1-\tfrac32u+O(u^2)$:

$$\frac1{|\mathbf r_P|^3}\approx\frac1{R_T^3}\Big(1-\frac{3x}{R_T}\Big)$$
$$-\frac{\mu\,\mathbf r_P}{|\mathbf r_P|^3}\approx-\frac{\mu}{R_T^3}\Big(1-\frac{3x}{R_T}\Big)\big[(R_T+x)\,\mathbf e_x+y\,\mathbf e_y\big]\approx-\frac{\mu}{R_T^2}\,\mathbf e_x+\frac{\mu}{R_T^3}\big(2x\,\mathbf e_x-y\,\mathbf e_y\big)$$

(the product $x\cdot3x/R_T$ and $y\cdot3x/R_T$ are second order and dropped). The circular orbit of $T$ gives $n^2R_T=\mu/R_T^2$ (Notes §8.3), i.e. $\mu/R_T^3=n^2$.

Step 3. Newton in $S_0$, projected on the basis of $S_1$

$m\mathbf a_P=-m\mu\mathbf r_P/|\mathbf r_P|^3$ with $\mathbf a_P$ of (a):

$$\mathbf e_x:\quad -n^2R_T+\ddot x-2n\dot y-n^2x=-n^2R_T+2n^2x$$
$$\mathbf e_y:\quad \ddot y+2n\dot x-n^2y=-n^2y$$

The terms $-n^2R_T$ cancel: the gravity at $T$ is exactly what keeps $T$ (and $S_1$) on its orbit, so only the difference of gravity between $P$ and $T$ (the tidal term) drives the relative motion. Likewise the centrifugal term $-n^2y$ cancels the along-track gravity component. Hence

$$\ddot x-2n\dot y-3n^2x=0,\qquad \ddot y+2n\dot x=0$$
Key results
  • $-\mu\,\mathbf r_P/|\mathbf r_P|^3\approx-n^2R_T\,\mathbf e_x+n^2\big(2x\,\mathbf e_x-y\,\mathbf e_y\big)$ (using $\mu=n^2R_T^3$).
  • $\ddot x-2n\dot y-3n^2x=0$, $\qquad\ddot y+2n\dot x=0$.

Part (c) · 2 points

Closed-form solution

Solve the equations of (b) for the initial conditions $x_0,y_0,\dot x_0,\dot y_0$.

Step 1. First integral

$\ddot y+2n\dot x=\frac{d}{dt}\big(\dot y+2nx\big)=0$. Integrating from $0$ to $t$ (Barrow): $\big[\dot y+2nx\big]_0^t=0$, so

$$\dot y+2nx=C,\qquad C=\dot y_0+2nx_0$$

Step 2. Radial equation

Substitute $\dot y=C-2nx$ in the radial equation: $\ddot x-2n(C-2nx)-3n^2x=0$, i.e.

$$\ddot x+n^2x=2nC$$

A particular solution is the constant $x_p=2C/n$; the homogeneous solution is $A\cos nt+B\sin nt$. The initial conditions give $x(0)=2C/n+A=x_0$ and $\dot x(0)=nB=\dot x_0$:

$$A=x_0-\frac{2C}{n}=-3x_0-\frac{2\dot y_0}{n},\qquad B=\frac{\dot x_0}{n}$$
$$x(t)=4x_0+\frac{2\dot y_0}{n}-\Big(3x_0+\frac{2\dot y_0}{n}\Big)\cos nt+\frac{\dot x_0}{n}\sin nt$$

Step 3. Along-track position

From Step 1, $\dot y=C-2nx=-3C-2nA\cos nt-2nB\sin nt$. Integrating from $0$ to $t$ with the antiderivatives $\int\cos n\tau\,d\tau=\frac{\sin n\tau}{n}$ and $\int\sin n\tau\,d\tau=-\frac{\cos n\tau}{n}$ (chain rule: $\frac{d}{d\tau}\sin n\tau=n\cos n\tau$) and Barrow's rule:

$$y(t)-y_0=-3Ct-2nA\Big[\frac{\sin n\tau}{n}\Big]_0^t-2nB\Big[-\frac{\cos n\tau}{n}\Big]_0^t=-3Ct-2A\sin nt+2B(\cos nt-1)$$

With $C$, $A$, $B$:

$$y(t)=y_0-(6nx_0+3\dot y_0)\,t+\Big(6x_0+\frac{4\dot y_0}{n}\Big)\sin nt+\frac{2\dot x_0}{n}(\cos nt-1)$$

Check: $x(0)=x_0$, $y(0)=y_0$, $\dot x(0)=\dot x_0$, $\dot y(0)=-6nx_0-3\dot y_0+6nx_0+4\dot y_0=\dot y_0$.

Key results
  • First integral $\dot y+2nx=C\equiv\dot y_0+2nx_0$; then $\ddot x+n^2x=2nC$.
  • $x(t)=4x_0+\dfrac{2\dot y_0}{n}-\Big(3x_0+\dfrac{2\dot y_0}{n}\Big)\cos nt+\dfrac{\dot x_0}{n}\sin nt$.
  • $y(t)=y_0-(6nx_0+3\dot y_0)\,t+\Big(6x_0+\dfrac{4\dot y_0}{n}\Big)\sin nt+\dfrac{2\dot x_0}{n}(\cos nt-1)$.

Part (d) · 1.5 points

Secular drift: the trap of the lower orbit

Find the along-track drift per orbit and the condition for no drift. A chaser is placed 1 km below the target on its own circular orbit (radius $R_T-1$ km): does it keep its position below $T$? Compare with a chaser released 1 km below $T$ at rest in $S_1$.

Step 1. Drift per orbit

The periodic terms of $y(t)$ repeat after one orbital period $2\pi/n$, and $x(2\pi/n)=x_0$; only the secular term changes:

$$\Delta y_{orbit}=y\Big(\frac{2\pi}n\Big)-y_0=-(6nx_0+3\dot y_0)\,\frac{2\pi}{n}=-12\pi x_0-\frac{6\pi\,\dot y_0}{n}$$

It vanishes iff $6nx_0+3\dot y_0=0$:

$$\dot y_0=-2n\,x_0\quad(C=0)$$

Step 2. Chaser on its own circular orbit

The circular orbit of radius $R_T+x_0$ has angular rate $n'=\sqrt{\mu/(R_T+x_0)^3}=n\,(1+x_0/R_T)^{-3/2}\approx n\big(1-\tfrac{3x_0}{2R_T}\big)$. Its velocity in $S_1$ is the absolute velocity minus the transport velocity $n\mathbf k\times\mathbf r$: $\dot y_0=(R_T+x_0)(n'-n)\approx R_T\cdot n\cdot\big(-\tfrac{3x_0}{2R_T}\big)=-\tfrac32nx_0$ (first order). Then $C=\tfrac12nx_0$, $A=-3x_0+3x_0=0$, so $x(t)=x_0$ for all $t$ and

$$y(t)=y_0-\tfrac32n\,x_0\,t,\qquad \Delta y_{orbit}=-3\pi x_0$$

For $x_0=-1000$ m: $\Delta y_{orbit}=+3000\pi=+9425$ m per orbit. The trap: "same circular motion, so it stays below" is wrong. The lower orbit has a shorter period (Kepler), so the chaser moves ahead of the target at $\tfrac32n|x_0|$. The no-drift value $-2nx_0$ is not the circular-orbit value $-\tfrac32nx_0$: the drift-free relative motion is the ellipse of (e), not a fixed point.

Step 3. Chaser at rest in $S_1$

With $\dot y_0=0$ (and $\dot x_0=0$): $\Delta y_{orbit}=-12\pi x_0=+12\,000\pi$ m $=+37.70$ km per orbit, while $x$ oscillates between $x_0$ and $7x_0$ ($x(t)=4x_0-3x_0\cos nt$, i.e. down to 7 km below $T$): a point "at rest" below the target is moving slower than the local circular speed and falls into an eccentric orbit.

Key results
  • $\Delta y_{orbit}=-\dfrac{2\pi}{n}\,(6nx_0+3\dot y_0)=-12\pi x_0-\dfrac{6\pi\dot y_0}{n}$; no drift iff $\dot y_0=-2nx_0$ (i.e. $C=0$).
  • Own circular orbit: $\dot y_0\approx-\tfrac32nx_0$, $x\equiv x_0$, $\Delta y_{orbit}=-3\pi x_0=+9.42$ km per orbit for $x_0=-1$ km: it moves ahead of $T$.
  • At rest in $S_1$ ($\dot y_0=0$): $\Delta y_{orbit}=-12\pi x_0=+37.70$ km per orbit.

Part (e) · 1.5 points

Bounded relative orbit

For $\dot y_0=-2nx_0$, show that the relative trajectory is an ellipse centred on the along-track axis, with the along-track semi-axis twice the radial one. Find its centre, its period and its sense of motion.

Step 1. Substitution

With $\dot y_0=-2nx_0$: $4x_0+2\dot y_0/n=0$, $3x_0+2\dot y_0/n=-x_0$, $6nx_0+3\dot y_0=0$ and $6x_0+4\dot y_0/n=-2x_0$. Then (c) gives

$$x=x_0\cos nt+\frac{\dot x_0}{n}\sin nt,\qquad y=y_0-\frac{2\dot x_0}{n}-2x_0\sin nt+\frac{2\dot x_0}{n}\cos nt$$

Step 2. The ellipse

With $A_e\cos\delta=x_0$, $A_e\sin\delta=\dot x_0/n$ and the addition formulas, $x=A_e\cos(nt-\delta)$ and $y-y_c=-2\big(x_0\sin nt-\tfrac{\dot x_0}{n}\cos nt\big)=-2A_e\sin(nt-\delta)$. Using $\cos^2+\sin^2=1$:

$$\frac{x^2}{A_e^2}+\frac{(y-y_c)^2}{4A_e^2}=1$$

an ellipse centred at $(0,y_c)$, with semi-axes $A_e$ (radial) and $2A_e$ (along-track), run once per orbital period $2\pi/n$.

Step 3. Sense of motion

The $\mathbf k$-component of $\big(x\,\mathbf e_x+(y-y_c)\,\mathbf e_y\big)\times\big(\dot x\,\mathbf e_x+\dot y\,\mathbf e_y\big)$ is $x\dot y-(y-y_c)\dot x=A_e\cos\theta\,(-2A_en\cos\theta)-(-2A_e\sin\theta)(-A_en\sin\theta)=-2nA_e^2<0$ (with $\theta=nt-\delta$): the ellipse is run in the sense of $-\mathbf k$. Above the target ($x>0$) the chaser moves backwards ($\dot y<0$), below it moves forwards: higher means slower.

Key results
  • $x=x_0\cos nt+\dfrac{\dot x_0}{n}\sin nt$, $\ y=y_c-2x_0\sin nt+\dfrac{2\dot x_0}{n}\cos nt$, $\ y_c=y_0-\dfrac{2\dot x_0}{n}$.
  • $\dfrac{x^2}{A_e^2}+\dfrac{(y-y_c)^2}{4A_e^2}=1$, $A_e=\sqrt{x_0^2+\dot x_0^2/n^2}$; period $2\pi/n$; run in the sense of $-\mathbf k$ (opposite to the orbital motion).

Part (f) · 1.5 points

A low Earth orbit case: two manoeuvres

Data: $\mu=3.986\times10^{14}$ m$^3$/s$^2$, $R_T=6778$ km; the chaser is 1 km behind the target, $x_0=0$, $y_0=-1000$ m, at rest in $S_1$, and receives an impulse at $t=0$. (i) The impulse is radial, $\dot x_0=+0.5$ m/s. Describe the motion and give the position after half an orbit and after one orbit. (ii) Which along-track impulse $\dot y_0$ (with $\dot x_0=0$) brings the chaser onto the target after exactly one orbit? Which way does it point, how far does the chaser go from the orbit, and with what relative velocity does it arrive?

Step 1. Mean motion

$$n=\sqrt{\frac{3.986\times10^{14}}{(6.778\times10^6)^3}}=1.1314\times10^{-3}\ \text{s}^{-1},\qquad \frac{2\pi}{n}=5553\ \text{s}=92.6\ \text{min}$$

Step 2. Radial impulse

$x_0=0$, $\dot y_0=0$: $C=\dot y_0+2nx_0=0$, so the motion is the bounded ellipse of (e), with $A_e=\dot x_0/n=0.5/1.1314\times10^{-3}=441.9$ m:

$$x=441.9\,\sin nt\ \text{m},\qquad y=-1000+883.9\,(\cos nt-1)\ \text{m}$$

At half an orbit ($nt=\pi$): $x=0$, $y=-1000-4\cdot441.9=-2768$ m (the chaser rose and fell behind); centre of the ellipse $y_c=y_0-2\dot x_0/n=-1884$ m. After one orbit it is back at $(0,-1000)$ m: a radial impulse alone gains nothing.

Step 3. Along-track impulse for a one-orbit rendezvous

With $x_0=\dot x_0=0$, (c) gives $x=\frac{2\dot y_0}{n}(1-\cos nt)$ and $y=y_0-3\dot y_0t+\frac{4\dot y_0}{n}\sin nt$. At $t=2\pi/n$, $\sin2\pi=0$, $\cos2\pi=1$: $x=0$ and

$$0=y_0-3\dot y_0\,\frac{2\pi}{n}\ \Longrightarrow\ \dot y_0=\frac{y_0\,n}{6\pi}=\frac{-1000}{3\cdot5553}=-0.0600\ \text{m/s}$$

The trap: to catch a target 1 km ahead, the chaser must thrust backwards. The backward impulse lowers its orbit ($x$ reaches $\frac{4\dot y_0}{n}=-212.2$ m at half orbit), and the lower orbit is faster, so it gains $3|\dot y_0|\cdot2\pi/n=1000$ m in one period. A forward impulse would raise the chaser and make it fall behind.

Step 4. Arrival

At $t=2\pi/n$: $\dot x=2\dot y_0\sin nt=0$ and $\dot y=-3\dot y_0+4\dot y_0\cos nt=\dot y_0$. The chaser arrives at $T$ with $\mathbf v_{rel}=-0.0600\,\mathbf e_y$ m/s, so a second impulse of $+0.0600$ m/s along $\mathbf e_y$ stops it at the target (total $0.120$ m/s). A numerical integration of the full nonlinear two-body equations from the same initial state misses the target by about 3 m (2.8 m) after one orbit, consistent with neglecting terms of order $|\boldsymbol\rho|^2/R_T$.

Key results
  • $n=1.1314\times10^{-3}$ rad/s, period $2\pi/n=5553$ s $=92.6$ min.
  • (i) $C=0$: bounded ellipse with $A_e=\dot x_0/n=441.9$ m, centre $y_c=-1884$ m; at half orbit $(x,y)=(0,-2768$ m$)$; after one orbit back at $(0,-1000$ m$)$: no gain.
  • (ii) $\dot y_0=\dfrac{y_0\,n}{6\pi}=\dfrac{y_0}{3\cdot2\pi/n}=-0.0600$ m/s (backwards); $x$ reaches $4\dot y_0/n=-212.2$ m at half orbit; arrival with $\mathbf v_{rel}=\dot y_0\,\mathbf e_y$, so a braking impulse of $+0.0600$ m/s is needed. A nonlinear two-body integration misses the target by about 3 m (2.8 m).

Interpretation and checks

  • Dimensional check: $n$ is s$^{-1}$ ($\mu/R^3$ is m$^3$s$^{-2}$/m$^3$); every term of $\ddot x-2n\dot y-3n^2x$ is m s$^{-2}$; $\dot y_0/n$ is m, so $x(t)$ and $y(t)$ are m; $\Delta y_{orbit}=-12\pi x_0-6\pi\dot y_0/n$ is m.
  • Limiting cases: $\boldsymbol\rho=\mathbf 0$ with zero relative velocity stays at the origin (the target itself). $n\to0$ (no gravity, non-rotating frame): expanding $\cos nt\approx1-\tfrac12n^2t^2$ and $\sin nt\approx nt-\tfrac16n^3t^3$ in (c) gives $x\to x_0+\dot x_0t$, $y\to y_0+\dot y_0t$, uniform straight motion, as it must.
  • Physical meaning of $C$: $C=\dot y+2nx$ is constant; it measures the difference of orbital period between chaser and target (to first order the chaser's semi-major axis differs from $R_T$ by $2C/n$). Equal periods ($C=0$) means no drift; that is why the radial impulse of (f)(i) gives a closed loop.
  • Common mistakes in the exam: (1) forgetting the acceleration of the origin $\mathbf a_T$ or the centrifugal term, which leaves an $R_T$ term that does not cancel; (2) expanding $|\mathbf r_P|^{-3}$ with the wrong exponent ($1-2x/R_T$ instead of $1-3x/R_T$); (3) assuming that a chaser below the target on its own circular orbit, or at rest in $S_1$, keeps its relative position; (4) thrusting towards the target to catch it.
Problem · Unit 3 · 10 points · 60 min · Standard exam level

P16 — Particle on a free wedge: a moving constraint, the reaction, momentum and energy, and the limit of a massless wedge

A wedge of mass $M$ rests on a smooth horizontal table. Its upper face is a smooth plane inclined at the angle $\alpha$ ($0<\alpha<\pi/2$) to the horizontal, descending towards $-x$; the top edge is $A$ and the corner of the wedge under $A$ is $B$. A particle $P$ of mass $m$ is placed on the incline. The inertial frame is $S_0=\{O;\mathbf i,\mathbf j\}$ ($x$ horizontal, $y$ vertical upwards, gravity $-g\,\mathbf j$). The wedge translates without rotating (it does not tip), and its position is the abscissa $X$ of $B$. The position of $P$ on the incline is the distance $s=|AP|$ measured from $A$ down the slope, along the unit vector $\mathbf u=-\cos\alpha\,\mathbf i-\sin\alpha\,\mathbf j$; $\mathbf n=-\sin\alpha\,\mathbf i+\cos\alpha\,\mathbf j$ is the unit normal of the incline pointing out of the wedge. Both bodies are released from rest at $t=0$ with $s=0$.

Figure 1. Free wedge: the wedge (mass $M$) slides on the smooth table, its position is $X$ (abscissa of the corner $B$); $P$ (mass $m$) slides on the smooth incline of angle $\alpha$, at the distance $s$ from the top edge $A$ measured down the slope (unit vector $\mathbf u$); $\mathbf N=N\mathbf n$ is the normal reaction on $P$ (outward normal $\mathbf n$).

Setup

  • Data: $m$, $M$, $\alpha$, $g$. Numerical parts: $\alpha=30^\circ$, $M=2m$, and the particle has slid a distance $L$ along the incline.
  • Hypotheses: point particle, rigid wedge translating on the table, all contacts smooth (no friction), $S_0$ inertial; the contact of $P$ with the incline is unilateral ($N\ge0$, checked in (d)).
  • Frames: $S_0$ and the frame $S_1=\{A;\mathbf n,\mathbf u\}$ attached to the wedge, ordered so that it is right-handed: $\mathbf n\times\mathbf u=\big[(-\sin\alpha)(-\sin\alpha)-\cos\alpha\,(-\cos\alpha)\big]\mathbf k=\mathbf k$ (the order $\{\mathbf u,\mathbf n\}$ would give $\mathbf u\times\mathbf n=-\mathbf k$, a left-handed basis). $S_1$ translates with acceleration $\ddot X\,\mathbf i$ and does not rotate.
  • Basis change: $\mathbf i=-\sin\alpha\,\mathbf n-\cos\alpha\,\mathbf u$, $\ \mathbf j=\cos\alpha\,\mathbf n-\sin\alpha\,\mathbf u$ (from $\mathbf i\cdot\mathbf n=-\sin\alpha$, $\mathbf i\cdot\mathbf u=-\cos\alpha$, $\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf n=\cos\alpha$, $\mathbf j\cdot\mathbf u=-\sin\alpha$).

In matrix form:

$$\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf i\\ \mathbf j\end{pmatrix}=[R]\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf n\\ \mathbf u\end{pmatrix},\qquad [R]=\begin{pmatrix}-\sin\alpha & -\cos\alpha\\ \cos\alpha & -\sin\alpha\end{pmatrix}$$

with $[R][R]^T=\begin{pmatrix}\sin^2\alpha+\cos^2\alpha & -\sin\alpha\cos\alpha+\cos\alpha\sin\alpha\\ -\sin\alpha\cos\alpha+\cos\alpha\sin\alpha & \cos^2\alpha+\sin^2\alpha\end{pmatrix}=I$ and $\det[R]=\sin^2\alpha+\cos^2\alpha=+1$.

Part (a) · 1 point

Degrees of freedom and constraints

How many degrees of freedom has the system particle + wedge? Classify the constraint that keeps $P$ on the incline, as seen in $S_0$ and in $S_1$.

Step 1. Count

The wedge is a rigid body in the plane (3 coordinates); the table fixes its height and, since it does not tip, its orientation: 1 coordinate $X$ remains. The particle has 2 coordinates in the plane; staying on the incline removes one, leaving $s$. In total 2 degrees of freedom, $(X,s)$. Unlike a fixed incline, $X(t)$ is an unknown: the motion of the support depends on the motion of the particle.

Step 2. Classification

The condition "$P$ on the incline" reads $\overrightarrow{AP}\cdot\mathbf n=0$, i.e. $-(x_P-X)\sin\alpha+(y_P-h)\cos\alpha=0$ with $\overrightarrow{OA}=X\,\mathbf i+h\,\mathbf j$, a relation between the positions of $P$ and of the wedge: it is holonomic and, written in the system coordinates $(X,s)$ (or $(X,x_P,y_P)$), it contains no explicit time, so it is scleronomic. It would be rheonomic for $P$ alone only if the motion $X(t)$ of the wedge were prescribed from outside; here $X$ is an unknown, so the time enters only through the coordinates. In $S_1$ the plane is fixed. It is unilateral: $P$ can leave the plane upwards, so it holds only while $N\ge0$. Smooth: the reaction is $\mathbf N=N\,\mathbf n$, with no component along $\mathbf u$ (Notes §6.4).

Key results
  • 2 degrees of freedom, $(X,s)$.
  • $P$ on the incline: holonomic, unilateral, smooth; scleronomic as a relation between the coordinates $(X,s)$ of the system (no explicit $t$); it would be rheonomic for $P$ alone only if $X(t)$ were a prescribed function of time, which it is not here.

Part (b) · 1.5 points

Kinematics of $P$

Write the position, velocity and acceleration of $P$ relative to $S_1$ and in $S_0$, in the basis $\{\mathbf n,\mathbf u\}$.

Step 1. Relative motion

$\mathbf u$ is fixed in $S_1$, so $\mathbf r_{rel}=\overrightarrow{AP}=s\,\mathbf u$, $\ \mathbf v_{rel}=\dot s\,\mathbf u$, $\ \mathbf a_{rel}=\ddot s\,\mathbf u$. The relative velocity is tangent to the incline.

Step 2. Absolute motion

$\overrightarrow{OA}=X\,\mathbf i+h\,\mathbf j$ with the height $h$ of the wedge constant, so $\mathbf v_A=\dot X\,\mathbf i$, $\mathbf a_A=\ddot X\,\mathbf i$. Since $\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\mathbf 0$ (Notes §3.2):

$$\mathbf v_P=\dot X\,\mathbf i+\dot s\,\mathbf u,\qquad \mathbf a_P=\ddot X\,\mathbf i+\ddot s\,\mathbf u$$

Substituting $\mathbf i=-\cos\alpha\,\mathbf u-\sin\alpha\,\mathbf n$:

$$\mathbf a_P=(\ddot s-\ddot X\cos\alpha)\,\mathbf u-\ddot X\sin\alpha\,\mathbf n$$

and in Cartesian components $\mathbf v_P=(\dot X-\dot s\cos\alpha)\,\mathbf i-\dot s\sin\alpha\,\mathbf j$.

Key results
  • $\mathbf r_{rel}=s\,\mathbf u$, $\ \mathbf v_{rel}=\dot s\,\mathbf u$, $\ \mathbf a_{rel}=\ddot s\,\mathbf u$.
  • $\mathbf a_P=\ddot X\,\mathbf i+\ddot s\,\mathbf u=(\ddot s-\ddot X\cos\alpha)\,\mathbf u-\ddot X\sin\alpha\,\mathbf n$.

Part (c) · 2.5 points

Equations of motion and the reaction

Write the forces on $P$ in $S_1$, including the inertia force, and on the wedge. Obtain $\ddot X$, $\ddot s$ and $N$, and the reaction of the table on the wedge.

Step 1. Forces on $P$ in $S_1$

Weight $\mathbf W=-mg\,\mathbf j=mg\sin\alpha\,\mathbf u-mg\cos\alpha\,\mathbf n$; reaction $\mathbf N=N\,\mathbf n$; inertia force of the translating frame (Notes §4.9) $\mathbf F_{in}=-m\,\mathbf a_A=-m\ddot X\,\mathbf i=m\ddot X\cos\alpha\,\mathbf u+m\ddot X\sin\alpha\,\mathbf n$. There is no Coriolis or centrifugal force ($\boldsymbol\omega_{10}=\mathbf 0$).

Step 2. Projections for $P$

$m\,\ddot s\,\mathbf u=\mathbf W+\mathbf N+\mathbf F_{in}$:

$$\mathbf u:\quad m\ddot s=mg\sin\alpha+m\ddot X\cos\alpha\ \Longrightarrow\ \ddot s=g\sin\alpha+\ddot X\cos\alpha$$
$$\mathbf n:\quad 0=-mg\cos\alpha+N+m\ddot X\sin\alpha\ \Longrightarrow\ N=m\big(g\cos\alpha-\ddot X\sin\alpha\big)$$
